Opening: From the outside, an AI inference service looks just like a standard web microservice. A client sends an HTTP POST request carrying a JSON prompt, and the server returns a JSON response. That surface similarity tempts platform teams to scale and troubleshoot AI systems using web-application instincts. That mistake causes some of the most expensive outages and hardware waste in production infrastructure. A traditional web application is almost always bound by input and output waits. Host CPU threads spend most of their time waiting on database queries, disk reads, or upstream network sockets. Adding more stateless CPU servers behind a load balancer increases concurrency cleanly. An AI model, by contrast, is bound by dense matrix math and memory bandwidth. Generating a single response requires trillions of multiply-accumulate operations and terabytes per second of memory movement. Adding 100 standard CPU servers will not make a single prompt run faster. Instead, it saturates the network with synchronization traffic and stalls on motherboard DDR memory buses.
Middle: NVIDIA's work on special-function throughput and fast-math pipelines follows the same codesign pattern. Those changes target attention bottlenecks directly, in close cooperation with the researchers and practitioners who run LLMs on the hardware. That is mechanical sympathy in its purest form.

Opening: An engineer launches a custom kernel with 1,024 threads per block, expecting the most parallelism the hardware allows. Occupancy comes back at 15 percent and the GPU stalls on memory. A week later a compiler update raises a kernel from 40 registers per thread to 56, and the number of warps resident on each SM drops by a third. Neither problem shows up as an error. Both come from the way a GPU admits and runs work. A modern NVIDIA GPU is not a flat pool of 14,000 cores that shares out whatever code you give it. Work passes through hardware admission gates, and the threads that run it move in lockstep groups of 32. You can debug a production AI cluster without ever writing a CUDA kernel, but you cannot read its profiler output without this model.
Middle: Having 48 or 64 warps resident on an SM does not guarantee that the SM can issue an instruction on the next clock cycle. Even when an SM issues instructions every cycle at 95 percent utilization, a kernel can lose most of its throughput through the two Nsight Compute efficiency gates.

Opening: A GPU can have over a thousand TeraFLOPS of arithmetic horsepower and still crawl at a tiny fraction of its capability. Execution pipelines frequently spend 80% of their cycles waiting for bytes to arrive from memory. No single storage technology can be simultaneously tiny, fast, and huge. Every modern GPU solves this physical trade-off with a seven-tier memory hierarchy stretching from single-cycle per-thread registers out to off-chip HBM3e and host CPU RAM. As an AI infrastructure or platform engineer, you will diagnose memory-hierarchy bottlenecks constantly. - A thirteen-billion-parameter FP16 model fits effortlessly inside an 80 GB H100 with plenty of room to spare. Yet it hits a hard latency floor during unbatched LLM decode. - A compiler optimization that unrolls a loop and reduces instruction count can make a kernel slower. It does this by spilling just two float variables per thread into local memory. - A naive matrix transpose in fast on-chip shared memory stalls heavily on bank conflicts. Adding a single unused padding column makes that kernel three
Middle: :::info In one minute: Section 4 takeaways - Capacity in gigabytes decides whether weights, KV cache, and activations fit; bandwidth in terabytes per second sets the per-token decode floor. - Always verify that BAR1 Total is at least equal to VRAM Total via `nvidia-smi -q -d MEMORY`. - Replace synchronous `cudaMalloc` and `cudaFree` inside loops with stream-ordered `cudaMallocAsync` and `cudaFreeAsync` or `PYTORCH_ALLOC_CONF=expandable_segments:True`. Pair `cudaMallocManaged` with `cudaMemPrefetchAsync` and `cudaMemAdvise`. :::

Opening: When two workloads run on the exact same NVIDIA GPU, both can report 91% utilization in `nvidia-smi`, yet one finishes in 10 milliseconds while the other takes 500 milliseconds. The physical GPU is not one generic calculator. Each Streaming Multiprocessor contains multiple distinct execution engines: CUDA Cores, Tensor Cores, Special Function Units, Load/Store Units, and on graphics or universal SKUs, RT Cores. The top-level `nvidia-smi` utilization metric only reports that some engine was active during the sampling interval. On a modern data center GPU such as Hopper H100 or Blackwell B200, the vast majority of the chip's mathematical horsepower lives inside its Tensor Cores. Unlocking those matrix pipelines requires satisfying a strict joint contract between hardware and software. - On a Blackwell B200, standard scalar FP32 compute peaks at a tiny fraction of what dense NVFP4 Tensor Core compute delivers on the exact same silicon. - Lowering precision does not just speed up matrix math. It shrinks every weight tensor in HBM and multiplies the number of weights carried inside
Middle: Writing a raw Blackwell `tcgen05.mma` or Hopper `wgmma` kernel by hand takes extensive effort. It requires managing TMA tensor descriptors, `cuda::pipeline` ping-pong stages in shared memory, TMEM accumulator lifecycles, warp specialization, and Thread Block Cluster DSMEM multicast. NVIDIA CUTLASS wraps that entire pipeline into a C++ template `cutlass::gemm::device::Gemm<..., cutlass::arch::OpClassTensorOp, cutlass::arch::Sm100>`. It also powers `cuBLAS`, `cuDNN`, and PyTorch's TorchInductor `max-autotune` backend under the hood.

Opening: An engineering leader can easily waste $500,000 upgrading an A100 cluster to H100 GPUs for a real-time inference service. Even though the H100 offers three times higher Tensor Core compute, a single-stream service spends almost all its time waiting on memory reads. Adding more GPUs to an overloaded large language model decode service also fails to lower single-request token latency. Every worker is already waiting on memory bandwidth. Performance engineering begins before you open a profiler. It starts with an analytical model that connects what the workload demands to what the hardware can physically supply. Every processor is governed by its speeds and feeds.
Middle: In the third capture, single-GPU profiling on Rank 0 looks healthy. Yet distributed scaling collapses because two slow ranks take nearly three times longer per step. Cross-checking `nvidia-smi topo -m` and `nvidia-smi -q -d PCI` reveals whether those slow ranks landed across a cross-socket SYS NUMA link or a downgraded PCIe slot.

Opening: An AI cluster is only as fast as its slowest wire. Earlier in Part 10, we saw that an H100 GPU streams data out of on-package HBM3 at 3,350 GB/s. A Blackwell B200 reaches 8,000 GB/s. We also saw in the Roofline chapter that keeping Tensor Cores near their flat compute ceiling requires feeding them without stalls. Yet the moment a tensor leaves a GPU to synchronize gradients, exchange Tensor Parallel activations, or reach a network card, it hits the motherboard and rack interconnect hierarchy. Consider a concrete production incident from the cited notes. A team launches a four-GPU training job on a healthy dual-socket server. Two-GPU runs scale well, but four-GPU runs barely beat two GPUs even though every device passes health checks. When the engineer runs `nvidia-smi topo -m`, the physical wiring explains the stall.
Middle: Engineers building on Grace Hopper and Grace Blackwell Superchips often assume that every NVLink wire in the rack provides automatic hardware cache coherence. That assumption causes subtle memory-consistency bugs when writing custom multi-GPU kernels. You must distinguish the four hardware interconnect tiers and their exact cache-coherence boundaries.

Opening: An enterprise budget request lands on an executive desk, and the price difference looks hard to justify. An NVIDIA H100 SXM5 costs upwards of $30,000, whereas a consumer GeForce RTX 4090 costs around $1,600. A procurement director looking only at single-precision floating-point ratings notices that the gaming card advertises higher raw scalar compute than an enterprise A100. Inevitably, leadership asks why the company is spending millions on data center accelerators instead of filling racks with gaming cards. Answering that the H100 is simply faster fails the architectural review. The real distinction involves High-Bandwidth Memory versus consumer graphics memory, NVLink and NVSwitch fabrics versus isolated PCIe slots, and GPUDirect RDMA versus CPU bounce buffers. It also involves hardware ECC memory protection, passive front-to-back server airflow, and strict software licensing terms. Picking the wrong product family or form factor turns a multi-million-dollar purchase into stalled training jobs or tripped circuit breakers.
Middle: Crucially, four NVSwitch ASICs are soldered directly onto the 8-GPU HGX baseboard. Every SXM module routes all 18 of its NVLink lanes into these switches, forming a non-blocking all-to-all crossbar where any GPU can access any other GPU's memory without CPU or PCIe contention. This performance comes with steep facility demands. Stacking four 8-GPU DGX servers into a rack pushes power density to 40 to 60 kW, exceeding standard air cooling and requiring Rear-Door Heat Exchangers or Direct-to-Chip Liquid Cooling.
Exercise sample: 1. **The Gaming Card Defense:** A startup CTO wants to buy 64 consumer RTX 4090 cards for a distributed training cluster to save 80% on hardware. Opening: Every Linux administrator knows how to read a standard CPU server. When `htop` or Prometheus shows 95% CPU utilization, the processor is doing heavy work. When host RAM reaches 99%, an out-of-memory crash is seconds away. Applying that same mental model to an AI server leaves an operator completely blind. To standard Linux kernel tools, an NVIDIA GPU is just an opaque device sitting on the PCIe bus. The host kernel does not schedule GPU warps, track High-Bandwidth Memory allocations, or read silicon die temperatures. When a machine learning engineer reports that a distributed training job is crawling, an administrator checking `top` sees the CPU idling at 5%, disks quiet, and network links calm. Traditional host metrics declare the server healthy while the accelerators are silently failing (`nvidia-devops` V16 Ch 1).
Middle: Custom monitoring agents should call NVML directly and follow three rules. Initialize the library once at startup and shut it down on exit rather than re-initializing inside the polling loop. Bind device handles using immutable UUIDs instead of logical indices that can shift across reboots. Finally, query specific C structs for utilization, memory, temperature, power, and throttle bitmasks.
Exercise sample: 1. **Decoding Throttle Reason Bitmask:** A monitoring alert queries `clocks_throttle_reasons.active` and receives `0x0000000000000028`. Using the table in Section 3.1, decode this hex mask into its component flags and explain the physical condition on the GPU. 2. **Diagnosing the Utilization Lie:** On a two-GPU server, both cards report 85% SM utilization, but GPU 0 processes 2,048 samples/sec while GPU 1 processes only 180 samples/sec (`nvidia-devops` V16 Ch 3). Write the `nvidia-smi` command to inspect memory bandwidth utilization and explain why GPU 1 is stalling. 3. **PyTorch Memory Headroom Calculation:** Using the

Opening: When a containerized AI job fails to start, the Python error message rarely names the layer that is actually broken. A developer might see a missing-device error inside a pod on a node where host `nvidia-smi` reports eight healthy GPUs. Another pod may import PyTorch cleanly and report that CUDA is available, only to crash on its very first tensor allocation because no kernel image matches the GPU architecture. A third cluster upgrade from T4 GPUs to L40S GPUs can stall every pod at startup while exhausting host CPU memory across the node. These failures happen because a GPU container is not a virtual machine with its own operating system kernel. Saying that a container includes CUDA only describes the user-space half of a seven-layer execution contract. That contract starts in Python frameworks and domain libraries and passes through the CUDA Runtime API. It then crosses the Container Device Interface boundary into the host user-mode driver, Linux character device nodes, and ring-0 kernel modules.
Middle: NVIDIA's container plumbing evolved across four generations from a brittle Docker CLI wrapper into the declarative Container Device Interface standard supported natively by modern container runtimes. Instead of relying on custom runtime wrappers, `nvidia-ctk` scans the host GPUs and writes a declarative YAML specification to `/var/run/cdi/nvidia.yaml`. That specification tells `containerd` or `CRI-O` which character devices and read-only host libraries to bind-mount into the container.
Exercise sample: 1. **Isolating the Driver API library vs the Runtime API library.** Inside a container on a host with driver 550.90.07, running `ldconfig -p` shows the CUDA Runtime library under `/usr/local/cuda-12.4/lib64/`, but returns no lines for `libcuda.so`. State which component owns the User-Mode Driver library, which component owns the Runtime library, and which layer of the stack failed to inject the missing library. 2. **Diagnosing a missing kernel image error.** A container image built for A100 and A10 is deployed to an H100 node. Both `nvidia-smi` and the PyTorch availability check

Opening: In Chapter 10.6, we traced the physical wires of NVLink and NVSwitch across two-way PCIe bridges, eight-GPU HGX baseboards, and seventy-two-GPU NVL72 racks. Yet physical NVSwitch silicon does not forward a single packet by itself. Unlike a two-GPU NVLink bridge that the GPU kernel driver initializes directly, an NVSwitch crossbar is a switched fabric. It requires an active control-plane daemon to discover switch ASICs, train NVLink ports, program unicast and NVLink SHARP multicast routing tables, and fence faulted links. On an eight-GPU HGX or DGX server, that control plane is the NVIDIA Fabric Manager daemon `nvidia-fabricmanager.service`. On a multi-node NVL72 rack, it expands into NVOS on the switch trays coordinated by NMX-M. When Fabric Manager is stopped, version-mismatched, or beaten in a boot race by `kubelet`, workloads either fail peer-to-peer initialization or silently fall back onto the host PCIe bus.
Middle: Even when every server has healthy NVSwitches, GPUDirect RDMA, and matched ConnectX-7 adapters, the cluster will stall if the top-of-rack and spine switches are oversubscribed or shared with bulk storage traffic. Because all ranks in a synchronous training collective transmit at line rate at the exact same microsecond, traditional data-center oversubscription ratios immediately cause congestion.
Exercise sample: 1. **Systemd ordering for Fabric Manager.** On an eight-GPU HGX H100 server, Table 1.2 shows that Fabric Manager takes roughly 15 seconds at boot while `containerd` and `kubelet` start faster. Explain what happens to the first training pod scheduled after a reboot if `kubelet` starts before Fabric Manager completes. Then write the exact systemd drop-in file for `containerd.service` and `kubelet.service` that prevents the race. 2. **BCM `nvsm` and `nvdebug` incident workflow.** A DGX node logs an NVLink error. Write the BCM `cmsh` device mode commands to inspect active NVSM alerts

Opening: An AI infrastructure engineer rarely writes custom CUDA kernels from scratch every day. Yet production training jobs still crash with illegal memory access faults. Other jobs hang forever on an A100 after passing desktop unit tests, or spend most of their runtime inside memory allocation calls. When these failures wake up the on-call team, the root cause lives inside the fundamental rules of the CUDA execution and memory model. Four real production incidents from our reference notes show how quickly small kernel or memory mistakes turn into cluster outages. Each incident looks mysterious from the outside until you trace how threads, thread blocks, and device buffers map onto the physical GPU.
Middle: Even when tensors stay on the GPU for multiple kernels, a C++ inference server that calls `cudaMalloc` and `cudaFree` on every incoming request will suffer severe latency spikes. Worse, under variable-size requests, an application can crash with an out-of-memory error even while `nvidia-smi` reports gigabytes of free VRAM. Both problems come from calling synchronous OS-backed device allocations inside a hot loop and from allocator fragmentation.

Opening: In Chapter 10.11, we saw that moving data across the PCIe bus took roughly 13 times longer than executing the vector-add kernel itself. If a production inference server or training loop runs sequentially, it copies Batch 1 to the GPU, computes Batch 1, and copies Result 1 back to the CPU before touching Batch 2. Under that serial pattern, the hardware sits idle half the time. The GPU Streaming Multiprocessors wait while the PCIe bus transfers bytes, and the PCIe bus waits while the SMs run math. Modern NVIDIA data-center GPUs such as A100, H100, and B200 include independent hardware DMA Copy Engines alongside the SM compute engines. One DMA engine handles Host-to-Device transfers and a second DMA engine handles Device-to-Host transfers. When properly configured, the GPU executes three-way overlap simultaneously. Stream 0 computes Batch $t$ on the SMs, Stream 1 copies Batch $t+1$ from host to device, and Stream 2 copies the finished output of Batch $t-1$ from device to host.
Middle: In *AI Systems Performance Engineering* Chapter 11, Chris Fregly details the four LLM scenarios where dynamic allocations occur inside active streams. - Per-batch attention scratch buffers allocate a 16 MB workspace for raw attention scores before softmax and free it immediately after the layer completes. - Variable-length input sequences occur when Batch $N$ has 512 tokens and Batch $N+1$ jumps to 1,024 tokens, requiring larger intermediate activation buffers on the fly. - Autoregressive decoding causes KV-cache and activation buffers to grow as new tokens are generated. - Layerwise model pipelining runs Transformer layers 0 through 5 for Batch $t+1$ on Stream 0 while Stream 1 executes layers 6 through 11
Exercise sample: 1. **Pageable vs Pinned vs Remote-NUMA transfer math:** Using the Volume 03 Chapter 8 parameters in the table below, calculate the effective bandwidth in GB/s for pageable and local-NUMA pinned transfers. Then find the speedup factor of pinned over pageable, and the effective bandwidth when the pinned buffer sits on a remote NUMA socket. 2. **Proving fake vs real stream overlap from an Nsight Systems trace:** Using the trace timestamps in the table below, calculate the exact timestamp when Stream 14's Device-to-Host copy finishes. Then find the nanosecond gap before

Opening: An engineering team buys modern GPUs with massive high-bandwidth memory. Yet their custom CUDA and PyTorch kernels run at a small fraction of peak speed. Tensor Cores sit idle while warps wait hundreds of clock cycles for data from off-chip memory. The hardware has plenty of raw bandwidth, but the software wastes most of every memory transaction. A single NVIDIA Blackwell B200 GPU delivers massive HBM3e bandwidth, and a dual-GPU Grace Blackwell Superchip doubles that capacity. Yet a kernel that reads memory with a small element stride wastes three-quarters of that bandwidth. It achieves only a quarter of peak DRAM throughput because the hardware must fetch whole cache sectors. Threads only use a few bytes from each fetched sector and discard the rest.
Middle: Each block loads its tiles once, then reuses them for 32 multiply-accumulates per value. That cuts global reads for those values by 32 times. The book counts an 8 times drop in global memory accesses per thread. Its measured run cut 9,800 global load sectors to 1,200.
Exercise sample: 1. **Warp sector and cache-line math across strides:** Using `analyze_warp_global_memory_access()`, calculate how many cache lines and sectors a warp touches when reading floats under four patterns. Evaluate an aligned contiguous read, a contiguous read offset by four bytes, a stride of two, and a stride of eight. Report the theoretical Global Memory Load Efficiency for each case. 2. **Vectorized `float4` vs. Blackwell `float8` warp transactions:** For a single warp, calculate the total bytes moved per instruction, the number of cache lines, the number of sectors, and the PTX instruction emitted

Opening: An engineering team fixes every memory-layout defect from Chapter 10.13. Global loads coalesce into four sectors per request, vectors use wide types, shared-memory tiles have zero bank conflicts, and the Tensor Memory Accelerator prefetches buffers asynchronously. Yet Nsight Compute still shows the kernel running far below hardware peak. Both DRAM bandwidth and Tensor Core utilization sit below a third of capacity. This slowdown happens when Streaming Multiprocessors lack enough ready warps or independent instructions per thread to hide execution latency. It also happens when warps waste half their cycles executing divergent conditional branches. Even the fastest memory subsystem cannot save a kernel whose warps spend most of their time stalled at scoreboard dependencies or serialized conditional passes.
Middle: Use all of these signals together. Look at occupancy, warp stall reasons, memory throughput, and compute throughput at the same time. A healthy kernel shows high occupancy, high warp efficiency, and balanced use of the execution units, including Tensor Cores.
Exercise sample: 1. **Occupancy limiters and `__launch_bounds__` math:** On a Blackwell B200 SM, calculate three values using the hardware limits from Section 2.1. Opening: A kernel can have coalesced memory loads, conflict-free shared memory, high occupancy, and still leave the GPU mostly idle. This happens when every byte fetched from high-bandwidth memory performs only one or two floating-point operations. Compute throughput on modern GPUs has grown much faster than memory bandwidth. Low arithmetic intensity pins workloads under the slanted memory roof of the Roofline model long before Tensor Cores reach capacity. In *AI Systems Performance Engineering* Chapter 9, Blackwell B200 compute throughput jumps by two orders of magnitude across precision formats while memory bandwidth remains finite. Even inside a multi-GPU NVLink rack, kernels with low FLOPs per byte saturate memory and interconnect buses first. Infrastructure engineers must raise arithmetic intensity so each memory transaction feeds dozens of math operations on chip.
Middle: Chapter 9 highlights four specific inline PTX patterns. First, `cp.async.bulk.prefetch.L2.global` prefetches the next 128-byte cache line into L2 ahead of the loop index. Second, `ld.global.cg.f32` uses the cache-global modifier to cache streaming reads in L2 while bypassing L1 so hot reusable working sets are not evicted from L1. Third, `mad.wide.u32` computes 64-bit byte offsets in a single wide instruction while `membar.sys` enforces system-wide memory fences across NVLink-C2C and PCIe polling loops.
Exercise sample: 1. **Unfused versus fusedL2Norm arithmetic intensity math:** Using the byte and operation counts in Section 2.1, calculate the arithmetic intensity of a single one-FLOP elementwise pass moving twelve bytes. Then calculate the total bytes moved per element and arithmetic intensity of the three-pass unfused L2 normalization pipeline versus `fusedL2Norm` under both the conservative three-FLOP and full four-FLOP counts. 2. **Thread Block Cluster TMA multicast reuse:** Explain how a four-block cluster uses TMA multicast and Distributed Shared Memory to reduce global HBM tile traffic fourfold. State the default portable cluster limit

Opening: Even after coalescing global memory loads, tuning occupancy, and staging tiles in Shared Memory and Tensor Memory, a classical tiled kernel still runs in a stop-and-go cycle. Every warp in the thread block issues a global load into Shared Memory and stalls at a block-wide `__syncthreads()` barrier waiting for DRAM. It then computes on the tile and stalls at another barrier before loading the next tile. During the load phase, the Tensor Cores sit idle. During the compute phase, the memory subsystem sits idle. :::note About the numbers and code in this chapter *AI Systems Performance Engineering* states that the numeric values in its metric tables, including Tables 10-2 through 10-5, are illustrative and explain concepts. Therefore, none were rerun on live hardware for this handbook. The CUDA code is reproduced from the book and was not compiled here. Three cautions apply to the printed listings.
Middle: In `MyClusterKernel` below, each block initializes its local `shared_buffer` and waits at `cluster.sync()` so all member buffers are ready. Each block then maps a DSMEM pointer to Block 0's shared memory via `cluster.map_shared_rank(shared_buffer, 0)`. Non-zero ranks execute an on-chip `atomicAdd` directly into Block 0's shared memory over the SM-to-SM network. A second `cluster.sync()` barrier ensures all remote updates finish before Block 0 reads the combined result.
Exercise sample: 1. **Sizing `<cuda/pipeline>` ring buffers and 3-role warp-specialized SMEM:** Calculate the exact dynamic Shared Memory footprint in bytes for `gemm_tiled_pipeline` with a tile size of 32 at two stages versus three stages. Next, calculate the dynamic Shared Memory required by `warp_specialized_pipeline_kernel` at tile size 112 versus tile size 160 against Blackwell's per-block limit. Finally, compute the Shared Memory footprint of `warp_specialized_cluster_pipeline` at tile size 128 and the number of rows processed per CTA in a two-block versus four-block cluster. 2. **Tracing the 4-stage GEMM progression in Tables 10-2, 10-3, and

Opening: An individual kernel can reach nearly complete warp execution efficiency inside its own loop and still leave an AI cluster idle. Between kernels, the GPU often stalls waiting for host-to-device micro-batch transfers, blocking memory allocations, legacy default-stream barriers, or cross-GPU network copies. Training engineers and inference operators see these stalls as wide empty gaps on the Nsight Systems timeline and degraded token throughput. :::note About the timings in this chapter The stage timings and the Programmatic Dependent Launch figures in the table above come from small timeline models in `code/part10/inter_kernel_pipelining.py`. Those models use assumed stage durations such as 1.0 ms H2D. 2.0 ms compute, and 1.0 ms D2H to illustrate the diagrams in the book and are not GPU measurements. The book notes that its own metric tables are illustrative as well, and the CUDA listings reproduced from the book were not compiled here. :::
Middle: To capture across multiple streams, begin capture on the primary stream using `cudaStreamCaptureModeGlobal`. Fork work onto secondary streams with CUDA Events, and join all secondary streams back into the primary stream before calling `cudaStreamEndCapture`. Once instantiated, a single cudaGraphLaunch call replays the entire multi-stream compute and communication step.
Exercise sample: 1. Quantifying three-way multi-stream overlap versus pageable memory and blocking allocations. Using the timeline model in Section 1.2 across 8 batches. Opening: Hiding memory latency inside a kernel and overlapping copies across streams solves static pipeline bottlenecks. However, modern multi-GPU systems such as NVIDIA's DGX GB200 NVL72 still suffer from severe orchestration stalls when workloads are irregular or composed of thousands of micro-kernels. When thread work varies dynamically, when host CPU driver dispatch takes longer than GPU execution. Or when control flow requires a CPU round-trip, Streaming Multiprocessors sit idle across the entire rack. 1. Intra-kernel load imbalance across Streaming Multiprocessors stalls the tail of a grid whenever threads run variable-trip-count loops such as uneven_static.cu. Early blocks exit while slow blocks hold Streaming Multiprocessors hostage. Moving to a warp-batched L2-cache atomic work queue in computeKernelDynamicBatch inside uneven_dynamic.cu elects one warp leader to claim batches via atomicAdd. Broadcast indices via __shfl_sync, cutting runtime in half. 2. Host CPU launch overhead dominates autoregressive large language model decode when tiny kernels finish faster than the CPU can enqueue them. Launching tens of thousands of individual decode kernels per response pins CPU cores inside the CUDA
Middle: Scaling from one GPU to many keeps the same goal: hide data movement behind useful work. The host dispatches work to each GPU, and then the GPUs take over. While one stream computes, a second stream can move data peer to peer over NVLink or PCIe, without touching host memory.
Exercise sample: ```text 1. Warp-batched L2 atomic work queues in `uneven_static.cu` versus `uneven_dynamic.cu`. For `N = 1,048,576` elements, calculate the total number of `atomicAdd` operations executed when every thread increments by 1 versus when a warp leader increments by `batchSize` of 8, 16, or 32. State the target value of Nsight Compute's `atomic_transactions_per_request` metric and the speedup of `computeKernelDynamicBatch` at 100 ms over `computeKernel` at 200 ms on `work = idx % 256`. ``` ```text 2. Opening: Writing every operator in hand-tuned CUDA C++ or PTX across evolving GPU architectures takes months of engineering effort. A kernel tuned for Ampere often needs complete rewriting to use Hopper and Blackwell hardware features. Meanwhile, standard eager PyTorch launches a separate GPU kernel for every minor addition or activation. Those repeated round-trips to high-bandwidth memory leave expensive Tensor Cores sitting idle. Modern AI infrastructure solves this bottleneck with compiler stacks and Python domain-specific languages. The PyTorch compiler captures whole subgraphs of Python code and fuses elementwise operations automatically. Under the hood, it lowers those subgraphs into OpenAI Triton kernels or routes large matrix multiplications to vendor libraries. When automatic compiler heuristics fall short, engineers can write custom OpenAI Triton kernels directly in Python while still targeting hardware features like Tensor Memory Accelerator descriptors and fifth-generation Tensor Cores.
Middle: If you call a raw `@triton.jit` kernel directly inside a compiled region without registering it, TorchDynamo cannot trace inside the custom launch call. Instead, it treats the unregistered kernel as an opaque foreign function or triggers a graph break. That break prevents TorchInductor from reordering or fusing operations around your custom kernel.
Exercise sample: 1. **Tracing the four-stage compiler pipeline:** State the CPython PEP number used by TorchDynamo, how many total PyTorch ATen operators PrimTorch IR decomposes into how many core primitives, how in-place addition is functionalized, and how TorchInductor lowers its Python loop-level IR to NVIDIA PTX without calling nvcc. 2. **Eliminating graph breaks in `toy_example`:** State the graph count, graph break count, op count, and two break reasons returned by `dynamo.explain` on `toy_example`. Write both refactored versions (`model_where` and `model_cond`) that reduce the graph count to one with zero breaks. 3. **Dynamic

Opening: An AI training or inference pipeline can stall at any layer of the hardware and software stack. A job might lose half its speed to Python interpreter dispatch, CPU data loading starvation, or un-overlapped host-to-device memory copies. Another job might stall inside Mixture-of-Experts token routing, memory-bound CUDA kernels, caching-allocator fragmentation, or multi-GPU collective communication stragglers. Guessing where the bottleneck lives wastes days of engineering time and thousands of GPU hours. As our infrastructure notes emphasize, picking the wrong profiler is like using an X-ray machine to diagnose a cellular disease or an electron microscope to check a patient's fever. Suppose a team writes a custom sparse matrix multiplication kernel in OpenAI Triton that runs four times slower than standard PyTorch on an H100. If they open Nsight Systems, they see only a single long blue bar on the GPU timeline row. Because Nsight Systems cannot look inside a streaming multiprocessor's register file or L1 cache, diagnosing register spilling or uncoalesced sector loads requires Nsight Compute. Conversely, running Nsight Compute kernel
Middle: In Mixture-of-Experts models, the number of tokens routed to each expert changes every step. Those variable-sized activation tensors repeatedly split and fragment PyTorch's caching allocator until a multi-day training job crashes with an out-of-memory error despite constant model size. At the same time, standard GPU memory allocations cannot be driven directly by NVSwitch or InfiniBand hardware offloads without extra copy overhead.
Exercise sample: 1. **Mapping symptoms across the 4-Layer Evidence Ladder and Table 13-1:** State which profiling tool you should select for a cluster utilization drop from ninety percent to ten percent at midnight. Name the right tool for CPU DataLoader image decompression starving the GPU, a custom sparse Triton kernel running four times slower than PyTorch on an H100, and aligning multi-GPU traces across eight ranks. 2. **Reconciling `torch.profiler` (Table 13-2) with Nsight Systems `nvtx_gpu_proj_sum` (Table 13-3):** For the 64-expert MoE training iteration, calculate the combined CUDA time and call counts of

Opening: A single degraded GPU can halt a synchronous training run across 1,000 GPUs and force every rank back to the last checkpoint. Platform teams feel this pain whenever an unhandled hardware fault stalls an expensive cluster or an overly aggressive alert pages an engineer at 3 AM for a benign event. Reliable operations require separating harmless self-healing events from fatal silicon failures in seconds. We group GPU fault signals into three operational tiers so automation can act immediately without human guesswork. Tier 1 covers self-healing hardware events and early precursor warnings. Single-Bit Error events in HBM are fixed on the fly by Error-Correcting Code hardware, and Ampere, Hopper, and Blackwell GPUs remap degraded rows to spare rows in the same memory bank. No job crashes on a single event, yet a steep weekly climb in Single-Bit Error rate is the primary leading indicator of an imminent bus disconnect or Double-Bit Error crash.
Middle: Trying to unload `nvidia.ko` for a live driver upgrade while the exporter or a zombie CUDA process still holds `/dev/nvidia0` open fails with a module-in-use error and leaves the node half-configured. Platform engineers avoid unnecessary reboots by following a strict four-step node draining sequence before touching kernel modules.
Exercise sample: 1. **DCGM 4 architecture, watch multiplexing, and diagnostic levels.** State the difference between embedded `libdcgm` inside the exporter and standalone `nv-hostengine` along with their default TCP ports. State the sampling rate chosen by DCGM when two clients watch the same field at 5-second and 1-second intervals, plus the default exporter collection interval and Xid window size in milliseconds. Finally, list the duration and purpose of `dcgmi diag` levels 1, 2, 3, and 4. 2. **Classifying all 15 Xid codes across Tiers 1, 2, 3, and Fabric.** Partition the 15 core

Opening: User-reported error messages in production GPU clusters almost never name the actual failing hardware or software layer. When engineers react to surface symptoms without a structured triage order, they replace healthy motherboards, swap network switches, or raise collective timeouts while the true root cause remains untouched. Mastering cross-layer troubleshooting lets on-call teams isolate the exact broken subsystem in minutes. Consider three classic misdiagnoses from Volume 20. First, a researcher reports an out-of-memory error on an idle A100 where `nvidia-smi` shows zero total memory. Unracking the server wastes days when the real cause is MIG mode enabled with zero GPU instances created, a container shipping a stale NVML library against a newer host kernel, or a dead OS process holding memory inside a zombie CUDA context.
Middle: When GPU utilization, power, and temperature all look healthy yet end-to-end training is slow, aggregate dashboards cannot tell you which layer is stalling the step. Insert `torch.cuda.synchronize()` barriers around all seven stages of a single training iteration. That instrumentation measures the exact millisecond share of CPU data loading, PCIe transfer, GPU forward, backward, loss, and optimizer math, and network all-reduce.
Exercise sample: 1. **Triage for zero GPU memory, zombie contexts, DKMS, and Enroot.** Explain why GPU 0 reports zero memory while GPU 1 reports full capacity on an A100 node in Section 1.1, and write the two commands that fix it. State how to clear the dead OS PID holding memory in a zombie context. Finally, explain how to fix the DKMS kernel-patch break and the missing Enroot hook from Section 1.2. 2. **Thermal throttling vs. power limiting and fan rise-rate math.** Using the H100 thermal slowdown numbers in Section 2.1, calculate

Opening: Picture a fresh Kubernetes cluster that you have just built, with a server that has eight NVIDIA H100 GPUs. The node turns green and reports a healthy Ready status, so it looks finished. Then the training team submits a distributed PyTorch job. Every Pod stays Pending, because Kubernetes sees zero GPUs on the machine. Standard Kubernetes was built around CPU cores and system memory. The Linux kernel exposes those resources natively, through `/proc`, and enforces limits through control groups. A GPU works differently. It needs a dedicated platform layer between the hardware and the container scheduler.
Middle: Deploy the GPU Operator without deciding who owns the host kernel driver, and it often triggers outages during OS patching. Suppose an unattended host package manager and the GPU Operator both try to manage `nvidia.ko` or `/etc/containerd/config.toml`. Then nodes can randomly lose their GPU runtime hooks after reboots. Platform teams need to pick one of three explicit ownership models, and they must enforce the single-reconciler rule.

Opening: Picture an application engineer who submits a training Pod. The Pod requests a single GPU, on a cluster where every node reports eight allocatable GPUs. The scheduler binds the Pod at once, and the container starts running. Seconds later, PyTorch crashes with `CUDA_ERROR_NO_DEVICE`, because `/etc/containerd/config.toml` lost its NVIDIA runtime handler during an OS image update. Scheduling a GPU in Kubernetes is not the same operation as giving a Linux container process a usable GPU. Three distinct software contracts stand between a Pod manifest and a working CUDA context. Each one runs across a different infrastructure boundary. Confusing those boundaries is the single largest source of wasted incident-response hours on Kubernetes GPU platforms.
Middle: Before you inspect any Kubernetes DaemonSet, check the worker host itself. Confirm PCIe enumeration, the loaded kernel modules, and the major and minor numbers of the `/dev/nvidia*` character devices directly on the node. These checks show whether the problem sits below Kubernetes at all.

It intentionally does not describe what kind of GPU is installed or how much high-bandwidth memory each card holds. It also does not report whether the silicon supports BF16 or FP8 instructions, whether Multi-Instance GPU partitioning is active, or whether the node passed validation.

Opening: Picture a team that has just installed a GPU pool. Helm reports the release as deployed, so the team opens the pool to users. That status means less than it sounds. It means only that the Kubernetes API server accepted the rendered manifests and that the top-level Deployment objects reported ready within the configured timeout. It does not prove that the kernel module loaded on every worker node. It does not show that the container runtime can inject device files into a fresh container sandbox. It also does not show that the kubelet registered GPU capacity or that telemetry exporters scrape matching device identities. Upgrades fail in a quieter way. Suppose a team bumps the chart version on a Friday and one node comes back with Secure Boot enabled. The new driver cannot load there, and a chart rollback does not unload the wedged kernel module. The node stays broken while the release history says everything is fine. An upgrade changes the host kernel, driver, container runtime, and firmware together,
Middle: Suppose tenant workloads are still running on the canary node when the upgrade starts. Upgrading GPU drivers or runtime hooks at that moment will crash those active jobs. Kernel modules cannot unload cleanly while user containers hold open file descriptors to device nodes. So you cordon the canary node, confirm that training owners have checkpointed their jobs, and drain the tenant Pods while leaving DaemonSets intact.

Opening: Picture two training Pods on the same cluster that request the same number of GPUs. The default scheduler treats each GPU request as a simple integer subtraction against a node allocatable counter. That count is necessary, but for high-performance AI workloads it is rarely sufficient. On a multi-socket GPU server, CPU cores, host memory, PCIe switches, network cards, and GPUs sit in separate hardware domains. When the scheduler or kubelet ignores that internal wiring, two Pods with identical requests can run at vastly different speeds.
Middle: Kubernetes keeps background work running on every node. The kubelet, the container runtime, and monitoring agents all use some CPU and memory. Usually that is only a few percent of one core, so a training job barely notices. Consider what happens when a busy inference workload shares the same machine. If another container suddenly uses heavy CPU or I/O, your job feels it.

Opening: Picture a researcher who submits one Pod that asks for 64 GPUs on a cluster built from 8-GPU servers. The Pod stays Pending forever. Everything you learned about single-node Kubernetes hits a hard wall once a model or training run needs more capacity than one server can offer. A Kubernetes Pod cannot span multiple nodes. In the Kubernetes object model, a Pod is the smallest atomic unit of placement. Its `spec.nodeName` field holds one string that names one Node. Every container inside that Pod shares a single Linux network namespace, an IPC namespace and a local kubelet lifecycle. Physical GPU servers typically hold at most 8 or 16 GPUs per baseboard. That is why any large training job or sharded inference pipeline must be split into a group of coordinated Pods across multiple nodes.
Middle: Each row below pairs a symptom with its command evidence, its root cause and the fix. Scan the first column for the symptom you see, then read across to the remediation. When you triage, you can start with the row whose symptom matches yours.

Opening: Picture a four-Pod training job where 3 Pods fit on the cluster and the fourth cannot find a node. In Chapter 11.7, we saw why multi-node GPU jobs cannot run inside a single Pod. We also saw why standard per-Pod scheduling deadlocks distributed training runs when only a subset of Pods can fit on the cluster. Fixing that in a multi-tenant production AI cluster means answering three control-plane questions, one for each layer of Kubernetes involved. The first question is about kube-scheduler itself. How does the scheduler hold 3 already-filtered Pods of a 4-Pod job in memory, without binding them to kubelet, until the fourth Pod also finds a valid node? If the fourth Pod fails before the timeout expires, how does the scheduler roll back those tentative reservations? The Kubernetes Scheduling Framework and the Coscheduling PodGroup plugin answer both parts. They use the Reserve, Permit, and Unreserve extension points, which are hooks where a plugin can step into the scheduling flow.
Middle: Each row below starts with a symptom you can see on screen and traces it to the layer where the cause lives. Read a row from left to right, and the remediation sits at the end. The first and second symptoms both look like a job that is not running, but the causes sit in different places.

Opening: Suppose your platform team tries to run multi-node training on native Kubernetes controllers. The queue controllers and gang schedulers can admit and bind Pods, but only Pods that already exist in the Kubernetes API server. They do not create those Pods, configure peer discovery or manage distributed failure domains. Someone still has to set up rank zero DNS discovery, inject environment variables, distribute SSH keys and decide whether one crashed container means restarting the entire job. Teams that do this by hand end up maintaining fragile bash wrappers, and those wrappers break on the first node preemption. Kubernetes ships with workload primitives such as Deployment, StatefulSet and batch Job. Those were built for stateless web servers and for batch tasks where every worker is the same. Distributed AI workloads need something different: several roles inside one job, and a lifecycle that treats a group of Pods as one unit. The built-in primitives do not provide that.
Middle: Retrieval-Augmented Generation embeds documents into a vector database. At request time, it queries approximate nearest neighbors using cosine similarity, then injects only the relevant chunks into the prompt. Teams can refresh rapidly changing domain data within minutes, without running a TrainJob. When a task needs both dynamic retrieval and domain-adapted reasoning, Retrieval-Augmented Fine-Tuning combines both patterns.

Opening: Suppose a team starts distributed LLM training on a cluster and the GPUs spend much of each step waiting. The job runs over the default Pod network, and gradient synchronization saturates the management NIC. Training throughput collapses to a fraction of what the hardware can deliver, and control-plane health checks start timing out across the node. The root cause sits in the network design itself. Standard Kubernetes networking was built for stateless microservices, where every Pod receives a single virtual Ethernet interface and one cluster IP address. Packets leaving that primary interface traverse the Linux kernel TCP/IP stack, netfilter or eBPF rules, and often an overlay tunnel over a shared management NIC. That design suits web traffic, and training breaks it.
Middle: Next comes the job itself. When you submit a distributed PyTorchJob over InfiniBand or RoCEv2, the Pod template brings four things together. These are the Multus network annotation, the RDMA device plugin request, the `IPC_LOCK` capability for pinning memory, and explicit NCCL environment variables.

Opening: Picture one network rail dropping a single packet in the middle of a training run. That one loss can stall an entire distributed training cluster. In a synchronous collective job, every GPU waits at the barrier until the slowest rank finishes transmitting. When one network card drops a frame and hits a retransmission timeout, thousands of other accelerators sit completely idle. Platform engineers and model teams then watch cluster-wide Model Flops Utilization collapse, while expensive compute hardware keeps burning power and doing nothing. Notice that each operator owns its own resource, ClusterPolicy or NicClusterPolicy. Earlier chapters drew a clean line on every Kubernetes worker node. The NVIDIA GPU Operator manages only the accelerator stack through ClusterPolicy. The NVIDIA Network Operator manages the high-speed network and storage stack through NicClusterPolicy.
Middle: A database-style test of 4 KB random I/O gives misleading numbers for an AI filesystem. It hides the bottlenecks that matter in training. Validate AI storage with three tests, and set `direct=1` on each one to bypass the Linux page cache. Start with a large-block sequential read to simulate dataset streaming. Follow it with a synchronous large-block write burst to simulate multi-node checkpointing. Finish with an MPI `mdtest` job that stresses the Metadata Server.

Opening: Picture a platform team that asks for four GPUs for one training job. Under the classic Kubernetes Device Plugin API, the scheduler receives nothing more than a flat integer count. The control plane cannot tell whether those accelerators have enough framebuffer memory. It cannot tell whether MIG slicing is disabled, or whether the GPUs and RDMA NICs sit on the same PCIe switch and NUMA node. The result is a poor placement. kube-scheduler often binds a multi-GPU Pod to a node with fragmented sockets. The local kubelet Topology Manager then rejects the Pod right away with a `TopologyAffinityError`. Platform engineers and researchers end up fighting fragile node-label selectors, wasted scheduling cycles and rigid single-container device locks.
Middle: Kubernetes 1.34 and later use the stable `resource.k8s.io/v1` API. In that API, an exact device count goes under the `devices.requests[].exactly` stanza. The manifest below claims four GPUs from the `gpu.nvidia.com` DeviceClass. It then mounts them into a PyTorch training container.

On the full A100 node, two GPUs sit on NUMA 0 and two sit on NUMA 1. Call `match_dra_resource_claim()` with two GPUs and same-NUMA required, then confirm which devices it selects. The self-hosted mode runs both the control plane and the GPU cluster on premises. It can run in Connected mode or in fully disconnected Air-Gapped mode.

Opening: In Chapter 11.13, we saw why the default Kubernetes scheduler struggles on shared GPU clusters. We also saw how the NVIDIA KAI Scheduler handles those problems with hierarchical quotas, gang scheduling, fractional GPU sharing, and NVLink or InfiniBand topology placement. Now suppose a distributed training job hangs in the Pending state. You need to know which internal component is holding it back, and you cannot find that out by treating the scheduler as a black box. Unlike kube-scheduler, which runs as a single binary that watches raw Pod and Node objects, KAI Scheduler is built as a decoupled suite of five cooperating control-plane microservices. Those microservices talk to each other through a well-defined hierarchy of Custom Resource Definitions.
Middle: KAI Scheduler splits scheduling across five microservices and four custom resources. Each failure therefore leaves its own fingerprint in that pipeline. The table below helps you find the controller or quota rule behind a stall.

Opening: Picture a training job that needs eight worker Pods. If the scheduler starts only some of them, the job cannot make progress, and the GPUs that were allocated to it sit unused. Two scheduler behaviors decide whether that happens, and a healthy AI cluster needs both of them. Gang scheduling and topology-aware placement are two halves of one distributed-systems problem. Gang scheduling controls when the Pods of a distributed job start, which must be all together or not at all. Topology-aware placement controls where those Pods land in the physical interconnect hierarchy. Solving only one half leaves your AI cluster broken.
Middle: The first two commands give you the GPU inventory and the topology matrix. The sysfs loop then confirms each GPU and NIC's NUMA node independently. Check that the results agree with what `numactl --hardware` reports.

Opening: A developer submits a single-GPU Pod to a dedicated cluster, and it stays pending. On that cluster the troubleshooting has only two branches. Either the cluster is out of GPUs, or a worker node is broken. Running NVIDIA KAI Scheduler on a shared, multi-tenant cluster changes that picture. You now manage hierarchical Queue quotas, PodGroup gang scheduling, BindRequest reservation Pods, hardware MIG partitions, and software Time-Slicing or fractional GPUs. Each of these adds its own place to fail, so the failure surface spreads across six distinct layers. The failures show up at different layers depending on where the workload is. A Pod that asks for a MIG profile or a fractional GPU share can stay pending for several reasons. A hardware XID fault sits at the first layer. MIG mode disabled on the host sits at the second, and a crashed device plugin sits at the third. At the fourth layer, the request can be blocked by a namespace ResourceQuota admission denial, a missing PodGroup, an exhausted Queue limit, or
Middle: Read each row from left to right. The symptom is what you see, the root cause boundary is where the break sits, and the remediation is the narrowest fix you can apply. Try the narrowest one first.

Opening: A platform team that serves dozens of research groups soon hits problems that no single cluster scheduler can solve. When hundreds of GPUs are shared across several clusters, governance becomes the bottleneck. Administrators struggle to connect corporate identity providers to departmental GPU budgets. Researchers, meanwhile, lose hours writing raw Kubernetes manifests just to launch a short notebook session or a distributed training run. Worse, teams often confuse cluster scheduling software with hardware isolation. That mix-up can expose sensitive model weights and metrics to neighboring tenants. In Chapters 11.13 through 11.18, we examined the open-source NVIDIA KAI Scheduler. NVIDIA released that core scheduling engine under the Apache 2.0 license in early 2025, after acquiring Run:ai. KAI Scheduler handles Pod placement inside one cluster, along with gang scheduling and queue priorities. The commercial NVIDIA Run:ai platform sits above that engine. It adds multi-cluster fleet management, single sign-on, departmental showback, and self-service workload templates.
Middle: Picture a university or corporate research center where 50 labs share 200 NVIDIA L40S GPUs across 25 nodes. Without fair-share accounting and preemption guardrails, a single well-funded lab can flood the queue with multi-day training runs. Those runs then block every student's five-minute interactive debugging session. The opposite problem is also real. Aggressive preemption without checkpointing destroys hours of training progress and saturates shared NFS storage.

Opening: Sooner or later, every AI platform team faces the same multi-million-dollar architectural decision. Picture a team that runs research notebooks, foundation-model training, data engineering and production serving on 500 to 4,096 GPUs. Leadership has to choose the scheduling control plane for that fleet. The first decision is whether to standardize on bare-metal Slurm or Kubernetes. If the team chooses Kubernetes, it must then select between NVIDIA KAI Scheduler, Kueue, Volcano, Apache YuniKorn, and the Coscheduling plugin. Choosing the wrong scheduler, or relying on the default Kubernetes scheduler for distributed GPU work, causes failures right away. Consider a researcher who submits a 16-GPU distributed training job to vanilla Kubernetes while only 8 GPUs are free. The default scheduler starts 8 pods immediately and leaves 8 pods pending. The running ranks wait for collective rendezvous, then crash with NCCL timeout errors. The allocated GPUs are wasted for that entire time.
Middle: Start with the `slurm.conf` excerpt, which you can read as the Slurm side of the design. It sets the topology plugin, the GPU selection, and the Prolog, Epilog and health-check options that the three tiers rely on.

Opening: Picture two nodes with identical hardware. On one of them, every distributed training step takes twice as long as on the other. The difference is where the cards sit. An NVIDIA H100 GPU has a lot of memory bandwidth inside the chip. The moment a tensor leaves the GPU over the motherboard PCIe bus, the transfer speed drops by more than fifty times. A server may lack dedicated PCIe switches. Kubernetes may also place a GPU and its RDMA network card on opposite CPU sockets. Then every network packet must cross the CPU Root Complex and the inter-socket link, which doubles distributed training step times across identical nodes.
Middle: Consider a containerized PyTorch dataloader that stalls on a shared GPU node. Your first habit is to check host `mpstat`. Plenty of idle CPU shows up there, so the operator concludes that the CPU is not the bottleneck. That conclusion is often wrong. Software cgroup limits, or changes to CFS shares, can throttle a container severely, even on a partly idle host. Worse, CFS share bursting can cut a container's available CPU by ten times when neighboring pods wake up.

Opening: Suppose your training job runs slowly, and its network traffic passes through a software proxy in the hypervisor. Routing high-speed guest network or storage traffic that way adds severe latency and CPU overhead. Every packet or block operation takes five to ten extra control and data steps through the host proxy. Giving the whole PCIe card to one virtual machine removes that proxy and fixes the speed problem. It creates a different problem: no other tenant on that server can share the ConnectX-7 network card or the H100 GPU. Platform teams need both bare-metal passthrough speed and hardware sharing on the same physical card. The PCIe specification handles this trade-off with Single-Root I/O Virtualization, known as SR-IOV. One SR-IOV card can present dozens of independent, hardware-sliced Virtual Functions on the PCIe bus, and each one can be handed to a different tenant.
Middle: If you get an alert about a compromised pod, start with the first row, because the fix there is to contain the pod rather than delete it. If a VM's GPU throttles, check the license state before you blame the hardware.

Opening: Most GPU sharing debates start with a chart that looks harmless. A finance leader opens the dashboard for an H100 card and sees average GPU utilization hovering around fifteen percent across one-minute samples. The question follows right away: why does the platform team not pack eight inference services onto that single accelerator? The average hides what is really happening on the card. When you query the device directly with `nvidia-smi dmon -s puctem`, two facts appear. The model weights and the KV cache, which an LLM serving stack keeps in GPU memory, already fill nearly ninety percent of the card's HBM3 pool. HBM3 is the high-bandwidth memory on the card. The service also needs burst headroom, meaning spare capacity for sudden spikes, because two user requests can arrive at the same millisecond.
Middle: Symptoms on a multi-tenant vGPU or shared-GPU node often point you in the wrong direction. A guest or a pod shows an error, but the true cause sits in another layer. The fault table below maps the command evidence to the architectural boundary that broke, and it gives the safe fix for each case.

Opening: Picture several containers sharing one physical GPU. Before the NVIDIA Ampere architecture, all of them competed for the same L2 cache banks, memory controllers and HBM address space. When one model launched a memory-bound kernel, its neighbors on that card felt it straight away, and their tail latency spiked. Multi-Instance GPU, or MIG, ended that interference on Ampere, Hopper and Blackwell by moving GPU partitioning down into the physical silicon. Running MIG in production means respecting two physical limits, and platform engineering teams often learn them the hard way. The first limit is that MIG divides a single card, not a whole server. Every MIG instance on an H100 still shares the physical board, the PCIe bus, the host kernel driver, the firmware and the seven-hundred-watt power budget.
Middle: When you read `2g.20gb`, the first token is the compute share, two-sevenths of the H100 compute slices. The second token is the dedicated HBM3 memory, here twenty gigabytes. Seven ten-gigabyte slices add up to 70 GB, not 80 GB. The missing ten gigabytes is the eighth memory slice, which no seven-slice layout can use. </details>
Exercise sample: Work through these six exercises in order. Each one reuses the functions and numbers from the chapter. 1. Explain the difference between a GPU Instance and a Compute Instance. List the six validation layers in order, and state what `nvidia-smi` inside the test container reports in the H100 worked example. 2. Solve the 24-node H100 fleet capacity plan. Using `plan_vol11_ch3_h100_fleet()`, show why seven `1g.10gb` slices plus two `3g.40gb` slices is physically invalid. Then prove that four `1g.10gb` slices plus one `3g.40gb`

Opening: Picture a platform team with a busy cluster and a queue of developers who cannot get a GPU. Many GPUs in production clusters lack hardware Multi-Instance GPU silicon. NVIDIA T4, L4, L40S and A10G inference cards cannot split their compute engines or memory controllers in hardware. Even on A100 or H100 nodes that support MIG, interactive Jupyter notebooks, continuous integration runners and lightweight microservices sit idle most of the time between short bursts. You feel that pressure when dozens of developers wait in pending queues while the allocated accelerators burn budget at low utilization. Without hardware partitioning, NVIDIA offers two software ways to share a GPU, and each one trades hard isolation for higher density. The first is temporal sharing, known as Time-Slicing. The NVIDIA Device Plugin advertises several logical tokens for each physical GPU. Every scheduled Pod mounts the exact same `/dev/nvidia0` device file and takes its turn through round-robin GPU context switching.
Middle: Suppose a cluster turns on time-slicing but never renames the resource or blocks multi-token requests. Users inevitably schedule multi-GPU jobs or production APIs onto shared nodes. The ConfigMap below enables four logical replicas per physical GPU. It renames the resource to `nvidia.com/gpu.shared` and rejects any Pod that requests more than one token.

Opening: Many GPU sharing projects fail before they begin. The infrastructure team starts from a hardware feature instead of the workload contract. An engineer sees that H100 cards support MIG, or that the device plugin can advertise ten logical replicas, and switches the feature on. Nobody has checked latency sensitivity, peak memory bursts or tenant fault boundaries. Worse, a shared GPU platform can fail commercially before it fails technically. That happens when its capacity and chargeback model counts time-sliced replicas, MIG slices and whole GPUs as interchangeable units. The Volume 11 notes record a planning failure that shows the problem clearly. A platform team saw a low fleet utilization average and deferred a hardware purchase. Two weeks later, a new inference tenant was stuck in Pending state with nowhere to run.
Middle: In a production incident, you need to know how a worker node splits its GPUs. The node may partition them in hardware, multiplex them in a hypervisor or time-slice them in software. The first block below asks the host about MIG mode, active GPU Instances, virtual GPU attachments and process density on the physical card. The second block checks the Kubernetes allocatable counts and matches the device UUID seen inside the pod.

Opening: Chapter 11.24 showed how Multi-Instance GPU, or MIG, isolates GPU compute, HBM memory capacity, L2 cache banks, memory controllers and crossbar paths in silicon. That raises a direct question for AI platform engineers. Why run Virtual Machines with KubeVirt, or MicroVMs with Kata Containers, inside a Kubernetes cluster when hardware MIG already exists? Two hard security and operational problems answer that question. Both come from the GPU sharing and confidential computing threat models in Volume 18. The first problem is that standard Linux containers still share one host OS kernel, even with MIG enabled. Volume 18 Chapter 6 compares its isolation tiers in plain terms. Time-Slicing is a hostel room, MIG is a set of concrete-walled hotel suites, and vGPU or VM virtualization is a VIP penthouse guarded by a hypervisor. All seven MIG containers on a bare-metal worker node still make syscalls to the same host Linux kernel. Picture a hostile tenant in MIG Instance 0 who exploits a Linux kernel vulnerability to escape the container and gain host
Middle: NVIDIA Hopper H100 Confidential Computing mode places H100 VRAM inside a hardware-protected region that the host OS and hypervisor cannot read. All data moving between the CPU TEE and the H100 GPU over the PCIe bus is encrypted in hardware through encrypted bounce buffers. Session keys are negotiated directly between the CPU TEE and the GPU hardware Root of Trust.

Opening: Picture a GPU server that holds millions of dollars of compute and sensitive model weights for several tenants. Its network edge is the one door between that server and everything else. On a traditional server, virtual switching, firewall enforcement with `iptables`, intrusion detection and encryption all run on the host x86 CPU. That design causes two serious problems in an AI Factory. The first problem is called Shared Fate. When the firewall and intrusion detection daemons run on the same x86 Host OS as untrusted tenant workloads, they share that host's fate. In the Volume 18 Chapter 8 customer scenario, an attacker exploited an internet-exposed Jupyter notebook on a bare-metal GPU instance and gained a host root shell. The attacker immediately ran `systemctl stop iptables` to kill the host firewall and IDS daemons. Then the attacker used the high-bandwidth GPU server to launch a massive DDoS attack that took down the availability-zone control plane.
Middle: Here, the DPU on `node09` stayed online and forwarded traffic, so the fabric counters stayed green. It was running a stale image and policy revision, though. Its image pull timed out, and reconciliation was abandoned after five retries. When a node behaves differently from its peers, compare its image versions with theirs before you blame the fabric.
Exercise sample: 1. Using `code/part11/dpus_bluefield_and_doca.py`, explain why `show interface status swp14` on the leaf switch and `ip -br link show` on the host missed the root cause. Opening: Building a cluster is only the start of an AI platform team's work. Picture a GPU cluster that has been running for months. Its hardware is aging, several teams share the same machines, and the cloud bill keeps climbing. Day-two operations are the work that keeps all of this under control. Without strict isolation and automated recovery, one user mistake or one uncoordinated patch can take down hundreds of GPUs. This chapter draws on Volume 19 and Volume 11 of our operational notes, and on *Scalable Kubernetes Infrastructure for AI Platforms*, to form one operational blueprint. By the end, you will know how to isolate competing tenants, cut cloud GPU bills in half, upgrade drivers without downtime, and find silent hardware stalls. The rest of the chapter takes these controls one at a time, so you can read each one as the answer to a specific failure.
Middle: A thirty-day query over raw per-pod GPU metrics can time out Prometheus during an outage. Abandoned notebooks, meanwhile, quietly drain the cloud budget. This rule file pre-computes fleet utilization every five minutes. It also flags any pod whose Streaming Multiprocessor activity stays below five percent for four hours.

Opening: Across Part 11 you built the architecture of GPU-enabled Kubernetes one layer at a time. You covered kernel drivers and CDI, then the GPU Operator, the Network Operator, KAI Scheduler, MIG, Time-Slicing, vGPU, KubeVirt, Kata, and BlueField DPUs. This chapter moves from diagrams to a live cloud GPU cluster on Nebius, Google Kubernetes Engine, or Amazon EKS. That is where the hard lessons show up. A cloud GPU VM or managed Kubernetes node pool is not ready for production just because the instance state says running. The same is true of a `helm upgrade --install` that exits cleanly. A worker node can report Ready with eight physical GPUs in its capacity field. Yet it can expose zero allocatable GPUs to the scheduler once the device plugin socket drops. On a fresh Nebius GPU VM, `nvidia-smi` can succeed on the host while containers fail to launch, because the container runtime was never wired to the NVIDIA toolkit. Formatting the wrong block device, enabling a firewall before allowing SSH, or running Horizontal
Middle: Provisioning multi-node GPU pools on Nebius, GKE, and EKS means you pay cloud billing charges. You can run the local suite without any cloud spend. This lab therefore pairs the cloud steps with a local Python 3 verification suite, in `code/part11/multi_node_gke_and_nebius.py` and `code/part11/multi_node_gke_and_nebius_lab.py`. The suite tests the four-layer Nebius L40S VM audit, the multi-node Kubernetes GPU inspection gates, and the Terraform, HPA, VPA, KEDA, Karpenter, and cloud networking configurations from *Kubernetes for Generative AI Solutions*. All cloud CLI commands are marked `(untested here)`.

NVKE is a management plane that runs managed Kubernetes on your own GPU bare metal, and NVKR builds its images and charts. AICR pins which driver, operator and plugin versions belong together. NVCRE certifies a cluster under real load. KAI Scheduler, inside the Run:ai platform, gives queues, quotas and gangs. Grove with Dynamo runs a multi-pod inference replica as one unit. NVSentinel detects faulty nodes and cordons, drains and repairs them.
Middle: The tested module `code/part11/c31_stack_order.py` reads this shape. It returns an install order, rejects a missing dependency or a cycle, and checks a short list of prerequisites taken from upstream overlays. For the sample above, it puts `dynamo-platform` last. It is a teaching model. AICR's own engine does more, including constraint checks and validation phases.

This part walks the installation from the first image build to the first tenant cluster. It explains NVKR, which builds what you install, and NICo with its Cluster API provider CAPNICo, which supplies the machines. It ends with the day-two runbooks: add a pool, roll an upgrade, replace a failed GPU node.

Opening: At 02:10 a pager says the NVKE API returns 503 on `/readyz`. Forty tenant clusters keep running, because their control planes live on their own machines. But nobody can create a cluster, replace a node or renew a certificate until the management plane is back. On the same night a security reviewer asks who can delete a customer's cluster, and the true answer is anyone who can reach port 30080. This chapter is for the person who carries the pager and answers the reviewer. It explains the operations you can rely on, the gaps the preview documents plainly, and the habits that make up for them. It ends with a comparison with other ways to run Kubernetes and a model answer to the question "what is NVKE and how would you operate it".
Middle: 1. **Network.** Apply the NetworkPolicy from 11.33 with only operator and management networks, and filter port 30080 at the edge as well. The docs themselves warn that the policy alone may not tell callers apart. 2. **TLS.** Put a reverse proxy that terminates TLS in front of port 30080, as the documentation suggests, and never send a token to the plain port from outside a trusted network. 3. **Authentication.** Enable enforcing mode with a short-lived token and a narrow admitted group. 4. **Authorization.** Do not give customers API access. Build your own service in front of NVKE that checks the tenant and calls NVKE with a service identity. That service

Opening: Picture an expensive GPU cluster that sits idle. The training run crawls at single-digit efficiency while burning millions of dollars in compute budget. An expensive GPU cluster can sit idle for over ninety-six percent of wall-clock time if the network delays gradient synchronization. Every GPU in a synchronous job waits at a barrier until the slowest peer finishes exchanging tensors across the fabric. The wires, switches, and network protocols therefore govern the effective utilization of every accelerator in the data center. Traditional cloud networks are built for millions of independent web requests. Those requests tolerate occasional packet drops and millisecond queueing delays. Distributed AI training works more like a single giant supercomputer. Thousands of GPUs burst at line rate at the same microsecond. If one packet from one GPU hits a congested switch buffer or drops on the wire, every other GPU halts. Understanding collective traffic, elephant flows, and incast is the foundation for every hardware and protocol choice in Part 12.
Middle: To remove those bottlenecks, production GPU clusters use a 1:1 non-blocking rail-optimized fat-tree. The sizing logic is modeled in `size_two_tier_nonblocking_fattree()` in `code/part12/ai_traffic_collectives.py`. On a 64-port switch, 32 ports connect down to server NICs and 32 connect up to spine switches, so downlink and uplink capacity match one-to-one. In a Rail-Optimized Design, NIC 0 of 32 servers connects to Leaf 0, NIC 1 connects to Leaf 1, and so on across eight leaves in each Leaf Group.

Opening: Here is a situation many multi-node GPU engineers meet. Every physical link on the fabric is Active at 400 Gbps, basic IP reachability succeeds across every node, and the switches show zero drop counters. Yet the distributed training job still crashes at startup with a generic network timeout, and you have nothing in the switch logs to explain it. This failure repeats on multi-node GPU clusters for one reason. Engineers treat Remote Direct Memory Access, or RDMA, as a faster version of TCP sockets. In traditional socket programming, an application writes bytes to a file descriptor. The Linux kernel copies those bytes into kernel-space socket buffers, runs the TCP/IP state machine, and hands packets to the network interface card driver. That model hides the hardware behind the operating system, so you never see the adapter doing the work.
Middle: Second, `opcode` selects the verb operation, such as `IBV_WR_SEND`, `IBV_WR_RDMA_WRITE`, `IBV_WR_RDMA_WRITE_WITH_IMM`, `IBV_WR_RDMA_READ`, `IBV_WR_ATOMIC_CMP_AND_SWP`, or `IBV_WR_ATOMIC_FETCH_AND_ADD`. Third, `sg_list` and `num_sge` specify an array of Scatter-Gather Entries, `struct ibv_sge`. Each entry holds a buffer address, a byte length, and a local key. A single Work Request can gather a protocol header, a tensor chunk, and trailing metadata from separate buffers into one wire transfer. That avoids copying discontiguous segments into a staging buffer first.

Opening: A training job can look healthy on every GPU server and still run slower than it should. The cause is often in how the servers exchange data. RDMA over Converged Ethernet version 2, or RoCEv2, is the transport most Ethernet AI fabrics use to keep gradient, activation, and weight buffers in sync across GPU servers at line rate. RoCEv2 works by encapsulation, which means wrapping one set of headers inside another. It places InfiniBand transport headers inside connectionless UDP and IP frames, using UDP destination port 4791. Direct Memory Access then moves data straight between GPU or host memory and the ConnectX or BlueField network adapter. Because the transfer bypasses the server CPU and operating system kernel, RoCEv2 scales to thousands of parallel collective sessions far better than TCP transports.
Middle: Why do Ethernet AI fabrics go to such lengths to prevent a single packet drop? Picture a 400 Gbps RoCEv2 link with a 20.48 microsecond round-trip time. More than one megabyte of data is simultaneously in flight on the wire. Under classic RDMA hardware recovery, losing the first packet of that window forces the receiver to discard every later packet already in flight.

Repeat the calculation at 1024 bytes. Then explain why RoCEv2 over IPv4 saves 12 bytes per packet compared with RoCEv1 while adding full Layer 3 routability. 2. A GID index after a VLAN change.

Opening: Picture an AI cluster that looks healthy on every dashboard. Every optical link is up, and every packet-drop counter reads zero. Yet a training run slows to a crawl during an AllReduce or all-to-all collective. You need to understand this failure before you change any switch setting. The stall often starts in one place. One receiver-facing leaf egress port fills its queue and keeps sending Priority Flow Control pause frames. Within microseconds, the upstream switch ingress buffers fill too, and they pass pause frames further upstream, one hop at a time. That chain freezes unrelated GPU workers that happen to share the same traffic class.
Middle: You can read the table row by row, and each stage hands off to the next. Drop mode is the most common mitigation. It contains the damage, because SpineA never sends XOFF to LeafA or LeafB. Forward mode works differently. It keeps packets moving toward LeafC and never pauses the upstream hops.

Opening: Picture a training job that slows down every time someone writes a checkpoint. The GPUs are not broken. Their packets are stuck behind storage traffic on the same switch port. An AI Ethernet fabric rarely carries only RoCEv2 collective traffic. GPU AllReduce and AllToAll collectives on UDP port 4791 share network adapters and switch ports with many other services. Those other services include checkpoint writes, dataset reads, BGP routing keepalives, Kubernetes RPCs, Prometheus scrapes, and operator SSH sessions. If the fabric treats all of these streams as one undifferentiated queue, a checkpoint burst or container image pull can delay Congestion Notification Packets. It can also trigger Priority Flow Control pauses that stall synchronous GPU training steps across the cluster.
Middle: Take the 262,144-byte port receive buffer from `mlnx_qos -i swp12` as your worked example. Each lossless priority reserves 65,536 bytes. Inside that reservation, XON sits at 32,768 B (50 percent), XOFF at 49,152 B (75 percent), and headroom at 16,384 B (25 percent).

Opening: Distributed training jobs synchronize every rank across the network at the end of every step. That means one slow link does not hurt only itself. If even one network link slows down or stalls, the entire cluster waits for that single straggler path to finish. In a large GPU cluster, a minor fabric misconfiguration can turn microsecond network delays into hours of wasted GPU compute time. Picture a brand-new cluster on the day you hand it to the research team. Every switch port shows a green physical LED, and the basic ping tests pass. Then a real training job starts, and it fails or crawls. You may lose days chasing collective timeouts, and those timeouts often come from four quiet fabric failure modes.
Middle: As Chapter 12.2 introduced, RDMA applications bypass the operating system kernel by posting Work Requests straight to hardware queues, through the `libibverbs` and `rdma-core` API. Before you debug a collective failure, inspect the adapter's exact hardware capabilities and active port MTU with `ibv_devinfo -v -d mlx5_0` `(untested here)`.
