Opening: Suppose you bring up a new InfiniBand cluster. Every optical cable is seated, and every switch port shows a green physical link LED. The fabric can still fail to move a single byte of training data. Even when every port reaches an active logical state, a large training run can take twice as long per step. That slowdown happens when static forwarding tables pile twenty gigabytes of traffic onto two leaf uplinks while the adjacent uplinks sit nearly idle. Ethernet switches run distributed routing protocols on every switch CPU. InfiniBand works differently. Its switches depend on one central control plane, the Subnet Manager. OpenSM or NVIDIA Unified Fabric Manager can implement it. The Subnet Manager discovers the topology over Virtual Lane fifteen, the management lane, and assigns Local Identifiers (LIDs) to the ports. It then programs the switch Linear Forwarding Tables and Multicast Forwarding Tables, applies Partition Keys and Service Level maps, and moves ports into the active state. Until an authoritative Master Subnet Manager finishes that sequence, the physical
Middle: Ethernet AI fabrics must solve the same Clos multipathing, rail isolation and multi-tenancy problems that OpenSM handles on InfiniBand. They do so with distributed or controller-driven IP routing protocols. Understanding both sides lets you compare InfiniBand and RoCEv2 designs on equal footing.

Opening: Picture a training run that is crawling, while every port on the fabric reports a healthy state. Every physical port can report an `Active` logical state, with a `LinkUp` physical state, and still show zero symbol errors or packet drops. Yet collective step times double whenever multiple jobs run together. A lossless InfiniBand fabric can still bring a multi-thousand-GPU training run to its knees. Lossless flow control avoids drops by pausing the sender instead of discarding packets. In Credit-Based Flow Control, a sender transmits only when the link partner downstream advertises free 64-byte buffer credits for the target Virtual Lane. Losslessness protects packets in flight. It does not create infinite link bandwidth or infinite switch buffer depth.
Middle: Here is the arithmetic for the oversubscribed case. Sixteen 400 Gbps downlinks carry 6,400 Gbps. Eight uplinks of the same speed carry 3,200 Gbps. That 2:1 ratio caps each node at 200.0 Gbps. A 2 GB AllReduce step therefore doubles in time. It goes from 40.0 ms to 80.0 ms. The paired `ib_write_bw` test shows the same effect. Throughput drops from 397.62 Gbps on the same leaf to 201.18 Gbps across racks. That is a 1.98:1 ratio.

Opening: Picture a training job that stays green on every reachability dashboard, yet slows a little more each day. An InfiniBand fabric can behave exactly like that, and the erosion is hard to see. Every rank in a collective barrier waits for the slowest link in the cluster. When one cable negotiates down to a single lane, or starts piling up physical bit errors, the stalls spread across thousands of GPUs. If you only check whether a port is up, or you keep tuning collective timeout variables, you can lose hours while the real link keeps degrading. Three production incidents from our operational archives show why shallow checks fail at cluster scale. In the first, every port reported active status, yet step times varied widely because one cable had trained at reduced width. In the second, multi-day throughput declined while a switch port's physical symbol errors grew faster and faster. In the third, engineers spent hours raising collective timeouts while a narrow, error-prone link corrupted RDMA retries underneath.
Middle: 4. The fourth mistake is relying on `ibstat` alone for link width, or on typical latency alone in `ib_write_lat`. On many adapters, `ibstat` prints an active state and a rate integer. It does not flag a four-lane port that fell back to one lane. When you check the lane width, use `iblinkinfo`, `ibstatus` or `mlxlink`. A normal typical latency can also hide a nearly threefold regression in the tail percentile.

Opening: Fabric generations often shrink to a single speed label on a purchase order. High Data Rate is sold as two hundred gigabits per second, and Next Data Rate as four hundred. Extended Data Rate doubles that again, and Giga Data Rate reaches the terabit range. Treating those headline ratings as the throughput your application will actually get is a mistake that keeps happening. Imagine you are on the team for a production upgrade. An infrastructure team swaps HDR Host Channel Adapters and switches for NDR hardware. Every port reports an Active logical state and a LinkUp physical state, yet distributed training gets only modestly faster across the cluster. A systematic audit of the hosts and switches turns up three independent bottlenecks, stacked in series.
Middle: 1. Lucent Connectors are compact two-fiber duplex plugs. They appear on single-mode WDM modules such as FR4 and LR4. Inside those modules, an internal optical multiplexer combines four lanes onto one fiber pair. 2. Multi-fiber push-on connectors pack a parallel fiber array into one ferrule. They suit parallel optics and breakout harnesses. Single-mode DR4 links use an angled polish to prevent back-reflection, while multi-mode SR8 links use a flat polish. 3. Passive direct attach copper cables carry signals over shielded twinaxial wire, with no electronics and very little power draw. Active electrical cables add retimer chips inside the connector shells, which reach across wider in-rack spans. 4. Active optical cables

Opening: Picture a training job that runs well for hours, then slows to a crawl. The cause is often a single congested leaf uplink, not the GPUs. An AI Ethernet switch is not a passive box of fast ports. It is the place where hundreds of RDMA Queue Pairs meet and compete for the same buffer memory. Every microsecond, frames enter lossless or lossy queues, and microbursts get absorbed in shared silicon memory. On a large multi-rack cluster, every optical link can report UP with zero Forward Error Correction uncorrectable errors. Yet a few destination-facing leaf uplinks can still suffer hash collisions or incast congestion. That local contention stalls an entire synchronous training job. Traditional enterprise Ethernet switches and standard five-tuple Equal-Cost Multi-Path routing were built for thousands of short, high-entropy TCP flows. They were never designed for a handful of synchronized RoCEv2 elephant flows generated by NCCL collectives. An elephant flow is one very large stream of data, and a few of them can overwhelm a path. Under static five-tuple
Middle: Spraying the packets of one Queue Pair across several spines means those packets take paths with slightly different queue depths and fiber lengths. They therefore reach the destination out of Packet Sequence Number order. On a standard RDMA NIC, one out-of-sequence packet is enough to trigger a sequence-gap Negative Acknowledgment. That NAK starts a Go-Back-N retransmission storm, and throughput collapses.

Opening: An engineering team can build an Ethernet fabric that passes every link test and saturates an isolated benchmark. The same fabric can still freeze a multi-node training job the moment a second workload starts. The opposite mistake costs just as much. A team proposes an expensive InfiniBand fabric for a small cluster without checking the payback period. At sixty-four GPUs, the upfront hardware premium takes ten years of compute savings to recover. When the interconnect does not match the cluster scale and workload pattern, either training jobs stall or capital is wasted. Choosing between InfiniBand, Standard RoCEv2, NVIDIA Spectrum-X, and the Ultra Ethernet Consortium specification is one of the foundational decisions in an AI Factory. A slogan about port speeds will not give you the answer. You get it by running a systems engineering calculation. That calculation weighs six concrete factors: workload synchronization, flow control physics, adapter capabilities, storage isolation, operational maturity, and three-year Total Cost of Ownership.
Middle: Picture an infrastructure review board asking you for a fabric recommendation. A recommendation made without fixing four constraints first is only a guess. You must evaluate workload synchronization, target GPU scale including eighteen-month expansion, multi-tenant security boundaries, and wide-area storage replication distance. Only after those four are settled can you select the right switch architecture.

Opening: Picture a training run that suddenly takes twice as long as it did last week. Nothing crashed, and the GPUs look busy, but every collective step now waits on the network. The cause can sit in the fabric, because a cluster behaves the way its wiring allows. An AI cluster is not a pile of switches joined by whatever fiber happens to reach. It is a physical communication graph. Its bisection cuts, hop counts, cable spans, and thermal limits decide how much throughput the cluster delivers. Those limits determine whether thousands of GPUs act like one supercomputer or like isolated islands that keep stalling each other.
Middle: Losing one plane of four still leaves 75.0 percent of capacity, or 600 Gbps. That is the resilience argument in one number. The cost is fiber, so count the strands before you commit to a multi-planar design.

Opening: Picture an AI training cluster whose leaf-to-spine links are fully provisioned, with no oversubscription on paper. It can still run at half of its effective fabric capacity. The cause lies in how the switches spread the traffic across those links. You will recognize it by hot uplinks sitting next to idle ones. Enterprise web and microservice workloads open thousands of short, high-entropy TCP connections, and those connections spread evenly across switch ports. Distributed large language model training behaves very differently. Its natural language understanding and natural language generation phases send massive east-west synchronization bursts across a small, fixed set of GPU pairs, over RoCEv2 on UDP port 4791. When two elephant flows hash to the same leaf-to-spine uplink, that link saturates and pauses traffic while adjacent uplinks sit idle. If the switches rely on traditional static 5-tuple Equal-Cost Multipathing, the cluster also suffers persistent Priority Flow Control stalls.
Middle: Suppose an AI backend grows to tens of thousands of GPUs, or adopts a low-diameter DragonFly topology. At that scale, standard eBGP gets hard to automate. It needs intricate per-link policies and external failure detection. Routing in Fat Trees, standardized in IETF RFC 9692, is a hybrid Interior Gateway Protocol built for dense data center fabrics. It is specialized for that job.

Opening: A training run on thousands of GPUs has a steady rhythm. Each GPU finishes its share of the math, then waits while the others catch up, because the step ends with a gradient exchange. The physical wire sets only an upper bound on that exchange. An 800 Gb/s InfiniBand rail fabric or a high-speed NVLink switch domain tells you how much data the cables can carry. What decides whether your cluster reaches high Model FLOPS Utilization is the collective communication runtime that moves the data across those links. On NVIDIA GPU clusters, that runtime is the NVIDIA Collective Communications Library, usually called NCCL and pronounced "Nickel." When thousands of GPUs synchronize gradients or exchange sharded weights and Mixture-of-Experts tokens on every step, the path the data takes matters a great deal. Sending multi-gigabyte tensors to a single master GPU serializes the whole cluster behind one network port. Falling back to a CPU-staged Gloo process group over TCP collapses throughput by two orders of magnitude compared to GPUDirect RDMA. Even with
Middle: Picture a large synchronous training job. One degraded cable, one bad NIC, or one wrong environment variable can stall every GPU in the communicator. They all wait until the watchdog timer fires. Meta's 54-day pretraining study of Llama 3 405B, run on 16,384 H100 GPUs, recorded 419 unexpected interruptions. Those interruptions show how hardware, memory, and network faults surface as collective timeouts.

Opening: Picture a training run that slowed down after a container update. The GPUs still show work, the job still finishes, and no error appears anywhere. The first sign of trouble is that each iteration takes twice as long, or three times as long, as it did before. A well-chosen collective algorithm can still fail in production. That happens when NCCL maps its logical rings or trees onto the wrong physical links. Training engineers often see GPU utilization stall while iteration time doubles or triples. Nothing crashes, and no message points at the cause. The usual suspects are a silent transport fallback, a cross-socket path mistake, or an aggressive channel setting that starves compute kernels.
Middle: Consider the `eth0` line in mode 1, which is the one to watch. A short filter looks harmless, yet it quietly widens the match. Test each filter against your own interface list before it goes into a manifest.

Opening: Picture a distributed AI cluster that finishes a training job and returns answers from an inference endpoint. It may still run well below what its hardware can do. When a cluster scales poorly or suffers storage and network stalls, almost half of the rented accelerators sit idle. They spend their time waiting on collectives or data loaders. Over a multi-week training run, those idle accelerators burn tens of thousands of dollars in wasted cloud compute. Here is the cost in one picture. A 64-GPU job at 52% scaling efficiency keeps only about 33 of its GPUs busy, and the rest of the fleet pays for idle time. You cannot diagnose a degraded optical transceiver, a PCIe root-complex downtrain, or a missing peer-memory kernel module from inside a full PyTorch model script. High-level frameworks mix data loading, autograd kernel launches, memory caching allocators and asynchronous CUDA stream hooks, so the fault gets buried. An infrastructure engineer therefore isolates the hardware and middleware layers from the bottom up. You validate intra-node PCIe
Middle: That formula separates initial prefill responsiveness from streaming decode speed. **Tokens per Second**, or `TPS`, measures token generation throughput. **Requests per Second**, or `RPS`, measures end-to-end API request capacity. Disaggregated prefill and decode across nodes and racks, such as NVIDIA Dynamo, avoids synchronous NCCL collectives for KV cache movement. Instead, point-to-point KV cache transfers use NVIDIA NIXL over UCX, GPUDirect RDMA and IBGDA.

Opening: A training job stops in the middle of a run. The logs show a timeout, and the ranks that printed it look perfectly healthy. Your first instinct may be to blame the network. This chapter explains why that instinct so often sends you in the wrong direction. A collective timeout is the most frequently misdiagnosed error in distributed AI infrastructure. Synchronous collectives such as AllReduce, ReduceScatter, AllGather, and Broadcast enforce barrier semantics across all participating GPUs. That means the ranks that print the timeout are almost always the healthy ones. They arrived on time, then waited until the process-group watchdog timer ran out. The real fault sits on the one rank that never entered the collective. It may also sit on a rank that died with a hardware fault, which the NVIDIA driver logs as an Xid error, or on a rank stalled behind a degraded physical link.
Middle: Sweep the kernel logs on every node in the job with `dmesg -T` or `journalctl -k`. Triage each Xid by its numeric code, across three operational tiers. Xid 74 gets its own row, because it is a link-level fault.

Opening: Suppose your GPUs finish their math and then sit idle, waiting for the network card or the storage array to deliver the next batch. The cause is older than most of the hardware in the rack. Standard Remote Direct Memory Access and standard Linux POSIX file I/O were designed for a time when the host CPU and its system DRAM were the final destination of every packet and storage block. In an AI factory, that is no longer true. Network collective payloads and training datasets never get computed in host DRAM. Matrix multiplications, attention kernels and optimizer updates all run inside GPU High-Bandwidth Memory (HBM), so that is where the data has to end up.
Middle: Look at the redirection and validation flags on every downstream port between your GPUs and your NICs or NVMe drives. If any of those ports reports a plus sign on a redirection or validation flag, ACS is actively redirecting P2P traffic upstream. The example below shows a bridge with those flags set.

Opening: When a training job grows from one server to thousands of GPUs, the network stops being a detail. In a classical distributed training cluster, the network switch acts as a passive byte pipe. The GPUs compute partial gradients in High-Bandwidth Memory and pass intermediate shards around a logical Ring or Tree. They also run every floating-point addition inside the GPU Streaming Multiprocessors. As clusters grow, this endpoint-driven approach wastes network bandwidth and takes compute cores away from matrix multiplication. 1. **Redundant Data Movement Across Endpoints:** In a standard Ring ReduceScatter across $N$ GPUs, each rank receives $N - 1$ chunks over $N - 1$ sequential steps. A full Ring AllReduce makes each rank transmit and receive almost twice the full tensor size, over $2(N - 1)$ steps. When the switch fabric has arithmetic reduction units and hardware multicast engines, the switch can sum the incoming shards in silicon. It returns only the final shard to each GPU, so per-endpoint receive traffic drops to a fraction of the ring volume. 2.
Middle: On a traditional HPC cluster, or a hybrid MPI plus PyTorch cluster launched with Slurm PMIx and OpenMPI, MPI collectives skip NCCL. They go through the OpenMPI Modular Component Architecture instead. Without explicit configuration, `MPI_Allreduce` runs on host CPUs over standard point-to-point RDMA. NVIDIA speeds up MPI collectives with the Hierarchical Collectives library, `coll_hcoll`.

NVMe-o-TCP (Port 8009) and NVMe-o-RDMA (RoCEv2 Ports 8009 -> 4420 -> 4791) Host Operations
Opening: Picture a training job that was fast last week and now crawls. The GPUs sit idle for long stretches, the compute dashboards look normal, and nobody sees an error. The cause is often the network, but "the network" is not one thing. An AI factory runs three distinct fabrics. Each one has its own latency target, bandwidth scale, and failure boundary. Treat them as interchangeable byte pipes and you will see checkpoint stalls, packet-retransmission storms, or sub-microsecond tensor starvation, and you will not know which fabric to blame. 1. **AI storage networks.** Curated training datasets must stream continuously into GPU memory. Multi-terabyte checkpoints must also flush to flash without pausing the training steps. A local PCIe drive responds in roughly a hundred nanoseconds. But a drive inside one server cannot hold a petabyte-scale corpus, and it cannot give thousands of nodes a shared namespace. Dedicated storage fabrics bridge that gap. They connect compute servers to shared flash arrays using NVMe-o-TCP, NVMe-o-RDMA over RoCEv2, and parallel file systems with GPUDirect Storage.
Middle: Most UEC IP fabrics keep a high port radix from leaf to spine. Those switches must balance traffic based on the semantics negotiated at the SES layer. Early on, some feared the Ethernet header would change to reduce segment latency. Compression or field removal remains an option, but it is optional, like the link-layer features.

Opening: Suppose a multi-day training run is getting slower each day, and nothing on the status dashboards is red. Every port says it is up. An AI data center network does not exist to pass `ping` checks or show green port icons on a legacy SNMP dashboard. Its main job is to minimize Job Completion Time and time-to-train for distributed training runs. It must also keep tail latency tight enough for real-time inference. Synchronous collectives such as AllReduce, AllGather, ReduceScatter, and AllToAll lock every GPU to the slowest participating rank on each step. A single slow link therefore sets the pace for the whole cluster. One brief microburst, or one degraded optical lane on one rail, can throttle every GPU in the job.
Middle: Scan the table to see what each row lets you learn. Rows 1 through 3 identify the device, the queue, and the ports. Rows 4 and 5 hold the timing you need for latency work.

Opening: The first deployment mistake is accepting a new GPU cluster because every port reports `LinkUp` or `Active` and `ping` gets a reply. The second mistake is sizing fabric capacity by adding up port speeds on a datasheet. That sum ignores the bottleneck cut, the set of links that separates the senders from their receivers. Picture a multi-rack InfiniBand cluster that passes commissioning. Every host is reachable, every port shows `State: Active`, and isolated point-to-point RDMA tests succeed. Once distributed training starts, however, step time varies by more than twenty percent. Three defects were stacked on top of each other. A replacement cable had trained at narrow lane width. Adaptive routing was not balancing flows across spines. Several ranks were bound across NUMA sockets.
Middle: Design an AI fabric from marketing slides or guesswork, and you will pay for it later. The usual costs are recabling in the middle of the project, tail-latency SLA breaches, or spine failures you cannot recover from. Before any hardware is ordered, each architecture decision should rest on seven evidence inputs.

Opening: Picture a thousand-GPU training run that stalls in the middle of a step. The job prints a collective timeout, and half of its bandwidth disappears. Each team looks at the same incident and blames a different layer. The application team sees an NCCL timeout and suspects a broken network switch. The network team sees active link states on every port and points at PyTorch. The platform team sees full GPU utilization in `nvidia-smi` and assumes the server hardware is completely healthy. A synchronous collective, such as AllReduce, ReduceScatter, AllGather or AllToAll, works like a meeting that cannot start until everyone arrives. Every rank must reach the barrier before any rank can move on. If 999 GPUs arrive on time and one rank lags behind, the other 999 sit idle until the watchdog timer expires. That lagging GPU might have a degraded cable, an unprogrammed InfiniBand port, a jumbo frame mismatch or a pause storm. Just as often, the culprit is a down-trained PCIe slot, a failed NVLink lane, thermal throttling,
Middle: When every lower gate looks clean, or when a thousand-GPU job crashes with an NCCL timeout, you have to look above the switch ports. An NCCL timeout proves only one thing. At least one rank failed to finish a collective before the timer expired. Finding the true cause means checking per-rank operation counts, bisecting the cluster and seeing past metrics that mislead at a single layer.

Opening: A GPU that sits idle waiting on a data loader or a stalled checkpoint costs the same hourly rate. So does a GPU waiting on a broken collective. Still, storage is routinely sized by two numbers from a vendor datasheet: total usable terabytes and peak sequential bandwidth. Consider what happens when 128 or 1,024 GPU workers start a distributed training run. A storage array that passed a synthetic sequential benchmark can still collapse to 24 percent GPU utilization. During an epoch shuffle across eighteen million small files, metadata lookups starve the data loaders long before the network links saturate. An hour later, a thousand GPUs pause at once to flush a checkpoint of up to 2 TB. The synchronized write burst overwhelms the storage controller queues, and forward progress stops across the entire cluster.
Middle: The Linux kernel automatically caches recently read file blocks in free host DRAM. You can see that cache as `cache` in `vmstat` and as `Cached` in `/proc/meminfo`. Whether the cache helps or hurts depends on how the dataset working set compares with available host RAM:

Opening: In enterprise web infrastructure, the golden rule is the stateless compute node. Local disks are a liability, and all application state lives on remote storage. In large-scale AI training, following that rule too closely turns the storage network into a choke point. The expensive GPUs end up waiting for their next batch of data. An AI training job rarely reads a dataset just once. A vision or multimodal model may iterate over the same thousands to millions of files for hundreds of epochs. Every one of those passes has to keep thousands of GPUs fed at the same time.
Middle: A `/raid` volume deserves a few checks before you trust it with a multi-week job. Benchmark it under the access patterns that training really produces. Confirm that checkpoint traffic will not wear the drives out early. And make sure cleanup between jobs happens automatically.

Opening: Picture a training job that runs at half speed. The GPUs show gaps between steps, and the model code looks fine. Often the cause is the storage pipeline. Feeding training samples, media shards, and model checkpoints into GPU memory matters as much as collective communication does for cluster goodput. When the storage pipeline cannot keep up, expensive GPUs sit idle waiting for the next batch, or they freeze while a checkpoint is written. Modern AI racks need a lot of storage bandwidth. Take an eight-GPU compute node. Each GPU consumes 200 MB per second, so the node needs 1.6 GB per second of continuous reads. A 72-GPU Grace Blackwell NVL72 rack needs 14 to 20 GB per second of sustained storage ingestion. Multimodal video pipelines and 100,000-GPU clusters that write multi-terabyte checkpoints push per-node storage streams to 10 to 100 GB per second.
Middle: When PCIe lanes or network links are already full, the best way to raise effective storage bandwidth is to move fewer bytes. Store datasets compressed on disk, then unpack them inside GPU memory right after a GDS read. For image and video pipelines, nvJPEG and NVIDIA DALI move decoding onto the GPU's hardware media engines. On an eight-core vision loader, that shift cuts host CPU usage from 800 percent to 200 percent.

Opening: Picture a training run on thousands of GPUs. Every epoch starts by reading data, and every recovery barrier waits on the parallel file system. When that file system slows down, the whole cluster waits with it. Chapter 6.19 covered the basic anatomy of Lustre, including the MGS, MDS, MDT, OSS, OST, quotas, and basic formatting. Running Lustre for AI factories adds three severe failure modes. Classic single-job HPC work rarely triggers all three together. The first failure is a metadata storm at the start of each epoch. A single workspace holds millions of tiny raw files, next to multi-gigabyte packed shards and multi-terabyte checkpoints. When an epoch begins, hundreds of GPU clients flood one MDS with open and attribute requests. Open latency jumps from a few milliseconds to tens of milliseconds. Every GPU then sits idle waiting for file handles, while the OST data drives remain ninety percent idle.
Middle: Consider a loader that reads training shards, or loads a hundred-gigabyte model into GPU memory. For example, if you run a job that loads weights at startup, every batch you feed the GPUs pays for those extra copies. A standard POSIX buffered read copies the data twice on the host. Bytes travel from the OSS through the LNet NIC into the Linux page cache in host DRAM. Then they copy into a pinned user buffer, and finally cross PCIe or NVLink-C2C into GPU memory. Those extra copies burn CPU cycles that tokenization needs, and they add latency to every batch.

Opening: Picture a training run that should take a week and takes two. The GPU dashboard shows the cards busy, yet progress is slow. Often the cause is not the compute. The storage layer is not delivering batches or saving checkpoints fast enough, so the GPUs spend their time waiting. Every minute spent waiting on storage is a minute of wasted cluster power and lost training progress. When the storage fabric falls behind, expensive GPU nodes sit idle waiting for batches. They also block on synchronous weight dumps, which means the training loop stops until each dump finishes.

Opening: Suppose your training job looks healthy on the dashboard, yet the GPUs keep waiting for data. The cause is often not the GPUs at all. It is a dataset made of millions of tiny files, and the storage system spends its time opening them. Datasets with millions of independent small files can consume over 80 percent of training wall-clock time on I/O and metadata overhead. The storage system and the GPU Tensor Cores may have plenty of unused bandwidth. Tiny file operations still starve the compute loop. Every extra millisecond spent opening and closing files leaves expensive accelerators waiting for their next batch.
Middle: Next, the tokenized shards are compiled into a pair of contiguous binary files, which Megatron-LM and NeMo read. The `.bin` file stores packed `uint16` or `int32` token IDs back to back, with no delimiters between documents. The `.idx` file stores a compact table of byte offsets and token lengths for each document. A memory-mapped file is one the process maps into its address space, so reads look like ordinary memory access.

Opening: Picture a thirty-day pretraining run on a thousand or two thousand GPUs. Something in that cluster will break before the run ends. At this scale, hardware failure is the normal state of the job, not a rare event. An HBM3 memory stack can develop an uncorrectable double-bit ECC error. A GPU can fall off the PCIe bus, an optical transceiver can flap, a power supply can trip, or the Linux kernel out-of-memory killer can terminate a worker. Over a campaign like this, at least one component will fail.
Middle: The second rule is parallel collective restore. Suppose you read a 500 GB checkpoint through one unstriped OST at 2.0 GB/s. The read alone takes 250 seconds. If you stripe the directory across eight Lustre OSTs with `lfs setstripe -c -1`, and read it with GPUDirect Storage, you get 16.0 GB/s of aggregate bandwidth. The read drops eightfold, to 31.25 seconds, and the total Recovery Time Objective falls to 46.25 seconds.

Opening: Suppose you are asked to approve the storage budget for a new GPU cluster. The usual habit is to ask how many terabytes or petabytes you need, and to treat storage as a cost center to keep as small as possible. In an AI factory that habit points the wrong way. A GPU that sits idle while it waits for dataset reads, epoch-start metadata lookups, or synchronous checkpoint writes costs the same electricity and lease money as a GPU running at full speed. Consider a 128-GPU A100 training cluster. Between electricity and capital lease amortization, the cluster burns four hundred and thirty-five dollars every hour. Now suppose storage stalls leave those GPUs idle for just ten percent of the day. The cluster then wastes over a thousand dollars per day. Over a year, that adds up to roughly three hundred and eighty thousand dollars in lost compute.
Middle: The same logic applies to cloud Spot instances. An 8xH100 Spot instance can save sixty-five percent compared with on-demand pricing. The catch is that the cloud provider gives only a two-minute warning before it preempts the instance. To keep that discount, your storage pipeline must flush an emergency checkpoint in under two minutes.

Opening: If you have ever watched a training run slow from 2 hours to 8 hours, you know how expensive guessing can be. Every minute spent poking at the storage array burns accelerator time. A GPU that sits idle while it waits on a stalled file lookup costs the same hourly rate as one running at full speed. The trouble is that a production AI storage path crosses eight distinct hardware and software layers. Those layers run from GPU memory and PCIe interconnects down through host CPU queues, local NVMe drives, client filesystems, network switches, metadata servers, and storage targets. Without an ordered diagnostic sequence, teams misdiagnose storage incidents and fix the wrong layer. Some teams scale out storage bandwidth when the real culprit is a small-file metadata storm that wastes 47 minutes of every training epoch. Others tune PyTorch worker counts while a tiny network ring buffer silently drops hundreds of packets under multi-job congestion. Still others assume GPUDirect Storage is bypassing the host CPU, when an alignment mismatch has
Middle: At the hardware level, `gdsio` compares two paths. Flag `-x 2` selects the CPU bounce path, and flag `-x 0` selects direct DMA. In a four-worker read test on a 10 GiB file, switching from `-x 2` to `-x 0` raises throughput and cuts latency by 20 percent. In the same test, host CPU load drops from 85 percent to 25 percent.

Opening: A team asks for 64 GPUs to train a 7B model. The request looks reasonable, because the checkpoint file is only 14 GB. Then the first training step fails with an out-of-memory error. The same team later doubles the GPU count and finds that the loss curve improves no faster than before. Both surprises come from treating the training loop as a black box. A machine learning system is much more than the network and its optimizer. It also contains business goals, data pipelines, feature stores, training clusters, checkpoint storage, serving engines and monitoring. An infrastructure engineer who cannot read the training loop cannot size the cluster, cannot explain a crash at step one, and cannot tell whether low throughput is a model problem or a network regression.
Middle: When you scale data parallelism across many GPUs, the global batch size equals the per-GPU micro-batch size multiplied by the GPU count. Because each global step now averages gradients over many more examples, the gradient estimate has lower variance. The model also takes far fewer steps per epoch. To maintain training speed without destabilizing AdamW, engineers scale the peak learning rate by the square root of the batch-size multiplier and add warmup.

Opening: An infrastructure engineer who treats a foundation model as a generic box of floating-point weights will repeatedly miscalculate cluster memory, network bandwidth, and serving latency. Suppose a model architect switches a 13B or 70B Large Language Model from Multi-Head Attention to Grouped-Query Attention or Multi-Query Attention. Or suppose the architect adopts DeepSeek Multi-Head Latent Attention instead. That change shrinks the key-value cache by 5x to 40x. It immediately determines whether a long-context conversation fits inside one GPU's High Bandwidth Memory or must be paged across a multi-node RDMA pool. Similarly, moving from a dense model to a sparse Mixture-of-Experts architecture like DeepSeek-V3 or Mixtral 8x7B splits memory and compute scaling in two. Total HBM capacity still scales with the total parameter count across all experts. By contrast, per-token compute FLOPs scale only with the active parameters routed per token. At the same time, network traffic shifts from predictable data-parallel AllReduce rings to latency-sensitive AllToAll expert dispatch across nodes.
Middle: In a dense Transformer, every token activates 100 percent of the model's parameters. In a sparse Mixture-of-Experts Transformer, each dense SwiGLU MLP block is replaced by a pool of parallel expert MLPs. A lightweight router then dispatches each token to only the top-k experts. Because only a small fraction of experts run per token, per-token compute FLOPs drop by 3.6x to 18x while total capacity scales to hundreds of billions of parameters. **When provisioning an MoE cluster, size HBM capacity for total parameters, size FLOPs for active parameters, and engineer your network for AllToAll dispatch.**

Opening: An infrastructure engineer often receives a request to train a large language model on a single GPU node. Five minutes after the job starts, the training script crashes with an out-of-memory error. Model parameter counts have grown exponentially over the last five years. Single-GPU high-bandwidth memory and compute throughput have grown only linearly. **Distributed training exists because modern models exceed both the memory capacity and the compute throughput of any single accelerator.** Even storing the raw weights of a frontier model overwhelms a single GPU before a single token of data enters the network. Training adds gradients, optimizer states, forward activations, and workspace buffers on top of those weights. Compute hits an equally hard physical wall during training. A single pass of a 175B parameter model takes well over an hour on one A100 GPU.
Middle: When a training job scales beyond one GPU, every gradient synchronization call traverses a six-layer hardware and software stack. A misconfiguration at any layer can silently drop throughput by an order of magnitude. Understanding how PyTorch maps logical process groups onto physical network links is essential for cluster debugging.

Opening: Once a model and its micro-batch activations fit inside the memory of a single GPU, an engineering team immediately hits a second problem: training takes far too long on one accelerator. A job that requires two weeks on a single server can finish in an afternoon if you spread the dataset across dozens of GPUs. **Data parallelism is the fastest and least invasive way to multiply training throughput: replicate the model on every GPU, split each incoming batch across workers, and average the gradients before every optimizer step.** Even inside massive 3D-parallel foundation model jobs that combine Tensor Parallelism and Pipeline Parallelism across thousands of GPUs, the outermost scaling loop is almost always data parallelism. For an infrastructure engineer, PyTorch DistributedDataParallel is the boundary where deep learning code meets the physical cluster network.
Middle: Launching distributed jobs with legacy `torch.distributed.launch` or manual `mp.spawn` scripts is brittle and error-prone. Always use `torchrun`, which spawns one worker process per local GPU, manages rendezvous over a `c10d` TCPStore, and injects standard environment variables into every worker. When configuring a multi-node architecture, validate InfiniBand RDMA device mappings first with `ibdev2netdev` and `ib_write_bw`.
Exercise sample: 1. **DP vs. DDP traffic and NVLink scaling efficiency:** Using the 7B FP32 model and four-GPU H100 parameters in the reference block below, (a) calculate the per-GPU Ring AllReduce data volume in DDP versus GPU 0's traffic in legacy DataParallel. Also state their ratio. Opening: In Chapter 14.4, DistributedDataParallel scaled training throughput by giving every GPU an identical copy of the model. Each worker then synchronized gradients through a single AllReduce operation per step. That replication-first design hits a hard physical wall as soon as model state outgrows a single accelerator's HBM capacity. Even a 7B parameter model trained with AdamW in mixed precision exceeds an 80 GB A100 or H100 GPU. At 70B parameters, a single 80 GB GPU holds less than ten percent of the required training state. Fully Sharded Data Parallel breaks this single-GPU memory ceiling by applying the Zero Redundancy Optimizer principle natively inside PyTorch. Instead of replicating parameters, gradients, and optimizer states on every rank, FSDP shards them across all GPUs. Each rank stores only a fraction of the model state at rest. On eight GPUs, a 7B model's state drops to a fraction of an 80 GB card. On sixteen GPUs, a 70B model fits comfortably with tens of gigabytes of headroom left for activations and temporary buffers.
Middle: Activation checkpointing solves this by discarding intermediate layer activations during the forward pass and recomputing them on the fly during backpropagation. Wrapping every Transformer block or every second block for selective checkpointing cuts activation memory by 50 to 80 percent in exchange for roughly 30 percent extra forward compute FLOPs. When you also need a large effective batch size without extra activation memory, pair activation checkpointing with gradient accumulation across micro-steps.
Exercise sample: 1. **Sharding Stages & Memory Anatomy:** Calculate per-GPU steady-state memory for the 7B and 70B models across DDP, ZeRO-1, ZeRO-2, and ZeRO-3 using the parameters below. 2. **Wrapping Granularity & Rate-Limiting Peak Buffer Math:** Compute the transient AllGather buffer size and peak state memory for monolithic root wrapping, 80-block wrapping, and unthrottled dispatch. 3. **Collective Communication Volume:** Calculate asymptotic and exact eight-GPU per-step bytes transferred for DDP, FSDP ZeRO-2, and FSDP ZeRO-3. Also state the communication multiplier of ZeRO-3 over DDP. 4. **Hybrid Sharding Bandwidth Sizing Across Four Nodes:** Calculate

Opening: Before PyTorch introduced native FSDP in Chapter 14.5, Microsoft Research introduced the Zero Redundancy Optimizer and the DeepSpeed engine to break the single-GPU memory wall. When teams launch distributed training with standard DistributedDataParallel, every GPU stores a full redundant copy of the model weights, gradients, and AdamW optimizer states. That duplication causes immediate out-of-memory crashes on 80 GB GPUs as soon as models pass a few billion parameters. Platform engineers feel this pain when expensive multi-node jobs fail at initialization or crawl at single-digit GPU utilization. While FSDP2 via `fully_shard()` is functionally equivalent to ZeRO Stage 3 on GPU HBM, DeepSpeed remains widely deployed in production AI infrastructure. It provides progressive state sharding, heterogeneous memory offloading, communication quantization, and long-context sequence parallelism. **You must match the ZeRO stage, offload tier, and collective compression strategy to your physical interconnect bandwidth and per-node memory capacity.**
Middle: When sequence length grows to 32K or 128K tokens, single-GPU activation memory easily exceeds 40 GB for a 32-layer model. Sequence and context parallelism solve this problem by splitting the input sequence across multiple GPUs so each rank starts with a local sequence chunk.
Exercise sample: 1. **ZeRO Stage 0, 1, 2, and 3 Static Memory Math:** - **(a)** Using the sixteen-byte mixed-precision AdamW convention for a 70B model, compute the exact per-GPU static memory in GB on eight GPUs for Stage 0, Stage 1, Stage 2, and Stage 3. Also compute the reduction factor versus DDP and the Stage 3 footprint on sixteen GPUs. - **(b)** Repeat the calculation for the 10B pure-FP32 model on eight 80 GB GPUs. Then evaluate the 30B model under the twelve-byte convention with ZeRO-2 on sixteen GPUs comparing FP32

Opening: State sharding methods like FSDP2 and DeepSpeed ZeRO solve the storage redundancy problem. They partition model weights, gradients, and optimizer states across the cluster. Yet state sharding still makes one major assumption. Once a single layer is reconstructed by an AllGather collective, one GPU must hold and execute that entire layer. When a team scales up to frontier models with hundreds of billions of parameters, that assumption breaks down. Training jobs stall on network latency or crash with out-of-memory errors inside a single layer. 1. **Single-layer matrices exceed single-GPU efficiency.** A single projection matrix in a 175B model holds over 600 million parameters and consumes 1.2 GB in half precision. At 1 trillion parameters, mixed-precision state reaches 12 TB, far exceeding the memory of two 8-GPU nodes. 2. **Per-layer AllGather collectives saturate inter-node links.** Even when sharded state per GPU is small, gathering full layers across hundreds of nodes on every pass stalls on slower InfiniBand fabric links. 3. **Long-context activations overwhelm GPU memory.** At sequence lengths of 32K to
Middle: Even after splitting layers with Tensor and Pipeline Parallelism, replicating full optimizer states across the Data Parallel group wastes tens of gigabytes per GPU. External frameworks like DeepSpeed ZeRO or Colossal-AI with its Gemini heterogeneous memory manager can shard state. However, Megatron Core includes three native subsystems that coordinate directly with its model-parallel groups:

Opening: A distributed training or fine-tuning job rarely stalls in only one place. A single Mixture-of-Experts or dense Transformer iteration crosses Python frame evaluation, host data loading, and file system reads. It also traverses host-to-device memory copies, compiled GPU kernels, caching allocator buckets, and multi-GPU collective synchronizations. When an engineer tunes one layer in isolation without an end-to-end profile, the bottleneck simply moves somewhere else. Even worse, a localized change can trade a small compute speedup for a massive spike in reserved GPU memory that crashes a multi-node job hours after launch. Chapter 10.20 built the hardware profiling foundations across DCGM, Nsight Systems, Nsight Compute, and the PyTorch profiler. **This chapter turns those diagnostics into an end-to-end PyTorch tuning and distributed scaling playbook.** You will learn how to correlate operator, timeline, Roofline, and host CPU profiles, and how to compile models with `torch.compile` and regional compilation. You will also eliminate launch overhead and allocator fragmentation with CUDA Graph Trees and `PYTORCH_ALLOC_CONF`. Finally, you will overlap superchip and fabric transfers with SuperOffload
Middle: 1. **Meta Holistic Trace Analysis and Perfetto.** Each rank records a Kineto JSON trace over a few steps using `torch.profiler.schedule(wait=1, warmup=1, active=3, repeat=1)` into a shared folder. HTA ingests all rank traces at once and aligns NVTX ranges on a unified Perfetto timeline. It splits GPU time into compute, communication, and idle time, and highlights straggler ranks that force peers to wait at collective barriers. 2. **TorchBench CI and PyTorch HUD regression rules.** Performance gains erode through software bit rot unless guarded in continuous integration. Run `torchbench run --model moe --iters 10 --batch-size 4 --json results.json` on dedicated hardware runners. That command checks throughput, peak allocated memory, data-loading latency, and

Opening: Running PyTorch in eager mode leaves expensive GPU hardware idle between tiny kernel launches. In eager mode, the Python interpreter dispatches every operator one at a time. The GPU reads a tensor from HBM, applies one simple operation, and writes the result right back to HBM. Training engineers pay both host-side Python dispatch overhead and massive memory-bandwidth penalties on every step. In Chapter 10.19, we saw how OpenAI Triton, `torch.compile`, and OpenXLA lower Python operations down to hardware instructions. Distributed training adds a much harder requirement than inference. A training compiler must trace, fuse, and memory-plan **both the forward pass and the backward pass simultaneously**. It also has to coordinate cleanly with DDP gradient buckets and FSDP parameter shards.
Middle: Beyond `TORCH_LOGS`, three debugging and numerical-accuracy workflows are essential for production training: - **Disk artifact dumps and trace parsing:** Setting `TORCH_COMPILE_DEBUG=1` writes a per-process debug directory containing FX graphs, runnable Python code, Inductor IR dumps, and compiled PTX. Setting `TORCH_TRACE="/tmp/tracedir"` and running `tlparse` renders a hierarchical stack-frame tree and a Perfetto timeline. The trace overhead is low enough for production runs. - **Pinpointing numerical discrepancies with the Level 4 minifier:** If a compiled training step produces `NaN` values or mismatches eager mode, export `TORCHDYNAMO_REPRO_AFTER="aot"` and `TORCHDYNAMO_REPRO_LEVEL=4`. Checking after AOTAutograd tests both forward and backward passes. Level 4 runs every subgraph in both eager and compiled mode, compares their outputs numerically,

Opening: A single HGX server is a tightly coupled island connected by high-speed intra-node NVLink bandwidth. Yet neither the memory nor the compute of a single server can train modern foundation models. Training engineers hit two hard physical walls the moment they scale past small prototypes: Crossing the server boundary to connect hundreds or thousands of GPUs changes the physics of distributed training. Inside an H100 server, each GPU enjoys 900 GB/s of NVLink bandwidth. Across servers in a rail-optimized fabric with NDR InfiniBand or RoCEv2 NICs, each GPU gets 50 GB/s. That drop creates an **18x bandwidth cliff per GPU** at the server boundary.
Middle: Multi-node jobs must write sharded checkpoints using Megatron-LM `.distcp` or PyTorch Distributed Checkpoint (DCP) instead of gathering state onto the first rank for four reasons: 1. **Avoiding rank 0 memory exhaustion:** Gathering a large model's weights and optimizer state onto one rank triggers a GPU or host RAM out-of-memory kill. Sharded writes distribute the checkpoint payload evenly across all ranks in parallel. 2. **Fitting inside the Slurm preemption window:** In production Slurm clusters, `#SBATCH --signal=SIGUSR1@90` instructs the controller to send a warning signal ninety seconds before the time limit expires. Immediate cancelation or node drain sends `SIGTERM`. Writing a sharded checkpoint across a parallel Lustre, WEKA, or GPFS filesystem completes
Exercise sample: 1. **Rail-Optimized Bandwidth and TP-Across-Nodes Penalty:** - **(a)** An H100 server has 900.0 GB/s of bidirectional intra-node NVLink bandwidth per GPU. Also compute the intra-node-to-inter-node bandwidth ratio per GPU. - **(b)** Explain why allowing Tensor Parallelism to cross the node boundary causes a 16.7x slowdown. Opening: When you play a long video game for a hundred hours, you save your progress periodically so a power outage does not force you back to the beginning. In distributed AI training, saving that progress is called checkpointing. On a small workstation, hardware crashes are rare enough to treat as bad luck. Across thousands of GPUs running for months, hardware failures are not a possibility. They are a mathematical certainty. Consider a job training on a thousand GPUs for thirty days, or ten thousand GPUs for three months. Even if every individual GPU is 99.9 percent reliable each day, a hundred-thousand-GPU fleet suffers roughly one hundred device failures every twenty-four hours. If you have not saved your training state, a single overheated GPU, broken transceiver, or power supply fault wipes out millions of dollars of compute time.
Middle: By default, PyTorch's NCCL process group timeout is thirty minutes, or 1,800 seconds. Suppose one rank hangs due to a dead NIC port or kernel deadlock. The remaining thousand GPUs sit idle at 100 percent static power draw for half an hour before the watchdog finally aborts the job.
Exercise sample: 1. **70B Checkpoint Sizing and Naive Gather vs Sharded Parallel Write.** - **(a)** A 70B-parameter model trains on 128 H100 GPUs under mixed-precision Adam at 16 bytes per parameter. Calculate the total checkpoint payload in GB and the per-GPU shard size. Also compute the 980 GB payload and per-GPU and per-node shard sizes on 64 GPUs when 2-byte BF16 gradients are omitted. - **(b)** Compare the total checkpoint pause of gathering all 1,120 GB to rank zero against a distributed parallel write where each GPU writes at 5 GB/s up

Opening: A distributed training job that runs without crashing is not necessarily a healthy job. On a large GPU cluster, silent performance bugs routinely waste half of your compute budget while every dashboard light stays green. Consider a team training a foundation model across sixty-four GPUs for two weeks. The job finishes and the loss converges, yet scaling efficiency is only 52 percent. The team pays for sixty-four GPUs while receiving the effective compute of thirty-three GPUs, wasting over fifty thousand dollars on idle silicon. Two structural traps hide these slowdowns from inexperienced operators. - **The utilization lie:** The coarse GPU utilization counter in `nvidia-smi` only reports whether at least one kernel was active on the device during a sampling window. A GPU can report 99 percent utilization while spending four-fifths of its time spinning inside an NCCL wait loop. It can also report high utilization while executing tiny memory-bound kernels that leave its Tensor Cores almost completely idle. - **Synchronous collective amplification:** Distributed training collectives such as AllReduce, AllGather, ReduceScatter,
Middle: 1. **MFU, HFU, and Goodput Calculation:** - **(a)** A 70B-parameter dense Transformer trains on an eight-GPU H100 SXM node rated at 989 TFLOPS dense BF16 per GPU. After increasing DataLoader workers and moving augmentations to the GPU, throughput rises to 9,000 tokens per second. Calculate the cluster peak TFLOPS, achieved TFLOPS, and MFU before and after the fix. - **(b)** If full activation checkpointing is enabled, calculate the achieved HFU at 9,000 tokens per second. Next, compute the Goodput efficiency of an eight-GPU node that completes 100,000 useful tokens in ten seconds. Compare that

Opening: Pre-training a frontier language model from scratch consumes trillions of tokens and thousands of GPUs for months. Yet the resulting base model is only a statistical next-token predictor. If you send a user question to a raw pre-trained model, it may respond by appending three more questions instead of answering the prompt. Turning that raw foundation model into a reliable domain assistant requires **post-training**, which combines Supervised Fine-Tuning and preference alignment. While pre-training consumes roughly 98 percent of the total compute budget, post-training consumes only about 2 percent. That final 2 percent unlocks the capabilities already stored in the weights. For an infrastructure engineer, post-training changes the memory, compute, and serving profile of the cluster in three critical ways. - **The trainable-parameter memory wall:** Full fine-tuning updates every weight in the network. It also stores gradients and Adam optimizer states for every parameter. Under a standard sixteen-byte-per-parameter mixed-precision baseline, full fine-tuning requires 112 GB of VRAM for a seven-billion-parameter model. A seventy-billion-parameter model requires 1,120 GB of VRAM before counting
Middle: Suppose you have multiple fine-tuned models or LoRA adapters specialized for different tasks, such as coding, SQL generation, and medical question answering. Training a single multi-task model simultaneously requires massive data, while sequential fine-tuning suffers from **catastrophic forgetting**. **Model merging** combines the parameters of multiple models offline—often on CPU without any GPU training—into a single model that inherits the capabilities of all constituent models without increasing inference cost. 1. **Linear Combination and Task Arithmetic:** Subtracting the base model weights from a fine-tuned model produces a **task vector**, also called delta parameters. You can add task vectors to combine skills, or subtract a task vector to erase undesirable behaviors or biases.

Explain why Llama 2-7B dropped from 0.353 to 0.219 under regular fine-tuning and why RAG outperformed fine-tuning across all three models. - **(b)** Suppose fine-tuning a proprietary model via API costs 25,000 dollars for 1,000,000 training tokens. Opening: An expensive GPU cluster is only as productive as the tokens flowing into its Tensor Cores. Even the best distributed training team with thousands of accelerators cannot fine-tune a reliable model if the dataset is noisy, repetitive, or poorly formatted. As foundation model architectures converge across the industry, data engineering has become the primary differentiator between mediocre models and frontier systems. In early deep learning benchmarks, researchers held the dataset fixed and competed to design better model architectures. Modern post-training has inverted that paradigm into data-centric AI. Teams now hold the base model architecture fixed and compete to engineer cleaner, more diverse, and more compute-efficient datasets.
Middle: Synthetic data is not a free substitute for real human provenance. Four structural failure modes govern its use. - **Superficial imitation:** Training a small student model on complex answers from a proprietary teacher teaches the student to mimic the teacher's formatting and tone without acquiring the underlying reasoning capacity. When asked novel questions outside its pre-training knowledge, the student model confidently hallucinates plausible-looking steps. - **Recursive model collapse:** Recursively training new model generations on unverified synthetic outputs from previous generations collapses the tails of the probability distribution. Probable events become over-represented while rare events vanish. Mixing verified synthetic data with real human data prevents collapse. - **Bias amplification:** Training on

Opening: An application team asks to customize a seventy-billion-parameter open-weight language model for their domain. One engineer proposes buying twenty dual-socket enterprise servers filled with eighty PCIe H100 GPUs. That choice appears to save thirty percent on hardware purchase price. Another engineer proposes running full-weight supervised fine-tuning on every weekly dataset update. They also plan to deploy a separate merged checkpoint for each of their one hundred enterprise customers. Both proposals will bankrupt the platform before a single user query is served. Fine-tuning sits at the intersection of GPU memory physics, interconnect topology, and cloud unit economics. Small architectural choices change hardware requirements from a single midrange PCIe card to a multi-node InfiniBand cluster. You must manage three infrastructure cliffs on every customization project.
Middle: Once supervised fine-tuning teaches a model domain formatting, teams often run a second post-training stage to align outputs with human preferences or reasoning rewards. Choosing between Proximal Policy Optimization, Direct Preference Optimization, and Group Relative Policy Optimization changes your cluster memory requirement by up to fourfold.

Opening: An open-source foundation model downloaded from Hugging Face or NGC knows general language patterns, but it does not know your proprietary banking policies, clinical workflows, or internal codebase. Putting a raw model into an inference container does not create a domain specialist. Teaching a model proprietary domain reasoning or strict institutional behavior requires modifying its weights through post-training customization. Treating model customization as a casual single-GPU script causes immediate production failures. - **Unreproducible shadow models:** If a fine-tuning run does not pin the base model commit hash, the dataset SHA-256 digest, and the `nvcr.io/nvidia/nemo:24.07` container digest, you cannot audit or roll back a misbehaving model in production. - **Data-pipeline GPU starvation:** Feeding raw JSONL files over network-attached NFS forces CPUs to parse text on the fly. That bottleneck traps Megatron-LM and NeMo runs at 18 percent MFU and causes GPU utilization to oscillate between 95 percent and 8 percent. - **Single-file checkpoint stalls:** Writing a monolithic checkpoint from one thousand GPUs locks the parallel filesystem metadata server. That mistake turns
Middle: Mutable tags can change silently between runs, making it impossible to audit, reproduce, or roll back a production model. Enterprise lineage requires pinning the exact base model Git commit or SHA-256 digest, the dataset `sha256sum`, the `nvcr.io/nvidia/nemo:24.07` container digest, and the training config commit in `training_metadata.yaml`. </details>

Opening: In today's AI landscape, putting a foundation model into production requires a fundamental shift in how we design, measure, and operate GPU systems. In this chapter, we trace the end-to-end anatomy of a production inference request from the moment an HTTP packet arrives at the gateway to the final streamed output token. We'll explore these mechanics in the context of modern accelerators like the NVIDIA L40S, A100, H100, H200, and B200 / GB200 NVL72. You'll learn how network proxies, CPU tokenizers, PCIe DMA engines, Tensor Cores, and High Bandwidth Memory work together to serve interactive AI applications at scale. Suppose an engineering team finishes training a seventy-billion-parameter language model across sixty-four H100 GPUs. During training, the cluster ran offline batch jobs with predictable tensor shapes, continuous Tensor Core saturation, and static memory allocations. When we place that same trained model behind a live chat API, the infrastructure rules flip overnight.
Middle: Every inference request begins and ends with vocabulary translation. During Stage 3, a Byte-Pair Encoding tokenizer such as tiktoken or Hugging Face's Rust tokenizers splits raw UTF-8 strings into subword units and maps them to integer IDs. In English prose, one token corresponds on average to roughly 0.75 words.

Opening: Training a frontier language model consumes thousands of GPUs for several months. Yet the lifetime cost of serving that model in production quickly eclipses the training bill. Across deployed enterprise AI platforms, inference accounts for up to 90 percent of recurring compute expenditure. A single naive deployment decision can multiply cluster hardware costs by tenfold while breaking user latency targets. Autoregressive language models generate responses in two distinct computational stages that stress completely different parts of the GPU silicon. When a prompt arrives, the engine executes the **Prefill** phase. It processes all input tokens in parallel to compute initial hidden states and populate the KV Cache. Once the first output token is emitted, the engine enters the **Decode** phase. It then generates one token per step conditioned on all prior tokens.

Opening: Training a foundation model optimizes for raw token throughput across predictable batch sizes over weeks. Serving that model to live users flips the engineering problem upside down. An online inference cluster must deliver responses in milliseconds while handling thousands of concurrent requests whose arrival times, prompt lengths, and output lengths are completely unpredictable. Early serving systems borrowed contiguous tensor allocation directly from training frameworks. For every incoming request, they reserved a single contiguous slab of GPU memory large enough to hold the model's maximum sequence length. Because actual generation lengths are unknown ahead of time, contiguous allocation wastes 60% to 80% of GPU memory on unused reservations and fragmented gaps. Worse, batching variable-length sequences together required padding shorter sequences with dummy tokens, wasting a third or more of attention compute on meaningless math.
Middle: Dynamic batch composition and asynchronous multiprocessing can introduce tiny floating-point variations across different batch shapes. Therefore, setting greedy decoding alone does not guarantee bit-for-bit reproducibility in reinforcement learning rollout pipelines. When strict determinism is required, disable V1 multiprocessing via `VLLM_ENABLE_V1_MULTIPROCESSING=0` and enable batch-invariant kernels.
Exercise sample: 1. **KV Cache Sizing and PagedAttention Fragmentation Math.** - **(a)** Consider Llama-3-70B with 80 layers, 64 query heads, 8 KV heads, 128 head dimension, and 2-byte BF16 precision. Compute the number of allocated physical blocks,

Opening: Most inference engines optimize a single request at a time. When a model exceeds the memory of one GPU, vLLM splits the weights across GPUs using Tensor Parallelism or Pipeline Parallelism. It also uses PagedAttention to cut internal memory fragmentation inside each sequence. That intra-request focus works well for isolated prompts, but production workloads rarely consist of isolated prompts. Consider an enterprise assistant where one hundred users send requests at the same moment. Every request begins with the same five-hundred-token system prompt. A traditional engine computes the Key-Value cache for those five hundred tokens one hundred separate times. That burns fifty thousand tokens of prefill compute on identical instructions. Multi-turn conversations, few-shot templates, retrieval-augmented generation pipelines, and multi-step agent chains repeat that waste on every turn.
Middle: When an application attaches a `session_id` to `/v1/chat/completions` payloads, the router enables **session affinity**. Consecutive turns of that conversation stay pinned to the same healthy worker where previous turns are already warm in the Radix Tree. That locality cuts follow-up latency by two to three times and reduces Time to First Token by 50 to 90 percent. If the worker holding an active session fails 5 consecutive health checks, the circuit breaker opens and **session migration** remaps the session to a healthy worker. Only the first migrated turn pays a full cold prefill penalty. Subsequent turns immediately benefit from the new worker's warm Radix Tree.
Exercise sample: 1. **RadixAttention Tree Splitting, Lookup, and Leaf-First LRU Eviction.** - **(a)** Insert the sequences `[1, 2, 3, 4, 5]`, `[1, 2, 3, 6, 7]`, and `[1, 2, 8, 9]` into a `RadixCache` with `max_size = 1000`. State how many total unique token slots are stored in the Radix Tree compared to storing all three sequences without sharing. Also compute the longest matching prefix length and hit rate for query `[1, 2, 3, 4, 10, 11]`. - **(b)** Suppose a `RadixCache` with `max_size = 10` contains `[10, 20, 30, 40, 50]`

Opening: Training frameworks like PyTorch prioritize eager-mode flexibility. Researchers need to modify layers, inspect intermediate tensors, and track autograd graphs on the fly. In high-concurrency production serving, that flexibility becomes a heavy tax. Every separate operator launches a distinct CUDA kernel from the host CPU. Each kernel writes intermediate activations back out to High Bandwidth Memory, leaving Streaming Multiprocessors waiting on memory round-trips or Python dispatch overhead. Consider two real production bottlenecks from enterprise GPU deployments. - **High-throughput multimodal and vision pipelines.** A feature-extraction cluster serving 25,000 requests per second on H100 GPUs using standard ONNX Runtime suffered severe tail-latency spikes above 85 ms. Those spikes violated its strict 30 ms service-level objective. Whenever bursty traffic drove dynamic batch sizes from 1 to 64, uncalibrated intermediate activation buffers triggered GPU out-of-memory crashes. - **Distributed large language model serving.** A platform team served Llama-3-70B across eight H100 SXM5 GPUs using a naive PyTorch and Hugging Face pipeline. At 150 concurrent sessions, Inter-Token Latency averaged 115 ms per token, missing the 25 ms

Opening: Early AI prototypes almost always start as a trained model wrapped inside a lightweight Python web server such as FastAPI or Flask. That pattern works on a laptop, yet it breaks down quickly in a production data center. Platform teams hit three structural walls when they scale custom Python wrappers across a GPU fleet. NVIDIA Triton Inference Server solves these challenges with an open-source, high-performance C++ serving core. **Triton decouples client network protocols from backend execution runtimes.** One standardized server can host any model framework across GPUs and CPUs.
Middle: Guessing whether an inference slowdown is caused by network queueing, host tensor staging, or GPU kernel execution leads to blind hardware upgrades. Triton exports fine-grained microsecond counters and gauges on port 8002 at `/metrics` so SREs can decompose every microsecond of request latency.

Opening: Building a high-performance model serving stack from raw open-source components takes weeks of fragile engineering. Before standardized inference containers existed, deploying a foundation model like Llama-3 required six manual steps. Engineers had to download raw weights from Hugging Face, compile hardware-specific TensorRT-LLM engines, and write a custom FastAPI web wrapper. They also had to wire that wrapper into Triton Inference Server or vLLM, design Kubernetes health probes, and maintain custom Docker build pipelines. Whenever a new model version or CUDA security patch arrived, the platform team had to rebuild and re-test the entire stack. NVIDIA NIM, short for NVIDIA Inference Microservices, and the Kubernetes NIM Operator solve both problems. **A NIM packages an OpenAI-compatible API server, Triton Inference Server, pre-optimized TensorRT-LLM and vLLM engines, and a hardware-aware model profile selector into one immutable, SLA-backed container**. On Kubernetes, the NIM Operator automates shared model caching, multi-node deployment, and autoscaling through declarative custom resources.
Middle: Deploying a single NIM container with docker run or a static Helm chart works for initial staging tests, such as Volume 14 Lab 02. At cluster scale, however, platform teams must coordinate model pre-caching jobs, ReadWriteMany PVCs, multi-node Tensor Parallelism LeaderWorkerSet pods, Prometheus ServiceMonitor objects, KServe InferenceService resources, and Horizontal Pod Autoscalers.

Opening: Running prefill and decode on the same GPU pool forces two incompatible workloads to fight for the same silicon. When a user submits a long document prompt alongside dozens of active chat sessions, the compute-heavy prefill pass monopolizes the Tensor Cores. Every ongoing token stream stalls until that prompt finishes. Engineers call this bottleneck prefill-decode interference. It forces traditional monolithic serving clusters into a painful compromise. - **Head-of-line blocking on streaming tokens:** A single multi-thousand-token prompt can pause ongoing decode iterations for hundreds of milliseconds. Users see frozen text streams and severe spikes in Time Per Output Token. - **Mismatched hardware and parallelism:** Prefill is compute bound and thrives on high Tensor Parallelism and dense matrix math. Decode is memory-bandwidth bound and suffers when cross-GPU synchronization is too wide. A colocated server must apply one hardware type and one parallelism degree to both phases. - **Collapsed cluster goodput:** Goodput counts only the requests per second that satisfy both Time To First Token and Time Per Output Token Service Level Objectives
Middle: On the parallelism side, prefill processes thousands of prompt tokens at once. Splitting layers across multiple GPUs with Tensor Parallelism, `TP_p = 2` or 8, and Pipeline Parallelism, `PP_p = 2`, drastically reduces TTFT. Decode generates one token per step, where wide Tensor Parallelism adds per-layer AllReduce overhead and Pipeline Parallelism introduces stage bubbles. Thus, decode pools prefer `PP_d = 1` and the smallest Tensor Parallelism degree that fits the model, `TP_d = 1`, 2, or 4.
Exercise sample: 1. **DistServe 2P1D Goodput and MLPerf v5.0 SLO Sizing:**. -, a, A colocated single-GPU server sustains 3.0 RPS under a `P90 TTFT <= 400 ms`, 0.4 s, SLO and 1.6 RPS under a `P90 TPOT <= 40 ms`, 0.04 s, SLO. When disaggregated into 2 prefill GPUs, 5.6 RPS per GPU. 1 decode GPU, 10.0 RPS, calculate the colocated goodput per GPU, the aggregate 3-GPU disaggregated goodput, the disaggregated goodput per GPU, and the per-GPU speedup ratio. -, b, State the MLPerf Inference v5.0 p99 TTFT and TPOT SLO targets

Opening: A single high-end GPU cannot hold a frontier foundation model. Even an eight-GPU server runs out of memory when serving massive dense models like Llama-3-405B. The same memory wall hits sparse Mixture-of-Experts models like DeepSeek-R1 with 671 billion parameters. You must split these models across multiple GPUs and multiple physical nodes. Scaling inference across nodes is much harder than scaling distributed training. Training jobs optimize for aggregate token throughput over hours or days. They can easily hide network latency behind large micro-batches. Online inference must deliver each output token in under 25 milliseconds while handling unpredictable user prompts. Partitioning a model places network communication directly on the synchronous critical path of every generated token.
Middle: Spawning new expert replicas during inference is hard. It requires pre-provisioned capacity or very fast model loading, so treat it as an advanced step. Grouping experts differently across GPUs can help too. The GPUs then finish each layer more in step. When imbalance persists, schedulers can add expert replicas on the fly, which softens hotspots caused by uneven gating.
Exercise sample: 1. **Tensor Parallelism vs Pipeline Parallelism Communication Math:** - **(a)** Consider an eighty-layer model with hidden dimension 8,192 running in BF16 at single-request batch size and single-token decode length. Calculate the number of AllReduce calls per token, the ring transfer steps per token at eight-way versus sixteen-way Tensor Parallelism, and the per-AllReduce byte volume per rank at eight-way Tensor Parallelism. - **(b)** Compare the per-token communication overhead of eight-way Tensor Parallelism over NVLink 4 at 900 GB/s against sixteen-way Tensor Parallelism across two nodes over 100GbE TCP at 12.5 GB/s.

Opening: An autoregressive language model executes two very different computational workloads inside every single request. During the prefill phase, the engine ingests the entire input prompt in parallel to populate the attention Key-Value cache. That pass runs dense matrix-matrix multiplications that saturate GPU Tensor Cores. During the decode phase, the engine generates output tokens sequentially one step at a time. Each step reads the full model weights and historical Key-Value cache from High Bandwidth Memory to execute skinny matrix-vector multiplications. Monolithic serving engines place both phases on the same GPU pool and multiplex them inside a shared continuous batching loop. Under real production traffic, that colocation triggers severe cross-phase interference. - **Prefill bubbles stall active decode streams.** When a user submits a long document prompt into an H100 engine, the unchunked prefill pass monopolizes the Streaming Multiprocessors for hundreds of milliseconds. Every ongoing chat or voice stream sharing that batch freezes until the prompt finishes. That stall spikes tail Inter-Token Latency more than eighteenfold. - **Chunked prefill trades tail spikes for
Middle: When GPU memory fills up, decode workers can offload older KV blocks to CPU RAM or NVMe. Suppose a sequence has generated 1,000 tokens, but only some of them are needed right now. The earlier KV can wait in host memory until attention needs it. Offloading adds latency when those blocks come back, so use it carefully. Prefetching and overlapping transfers with compute reduce the penalty.

Opening: Separating prefill and decode workers into distinct cluster pools eliminates first-order phase interference. However, macroscopic disaggregation alone leaves severe hardware bottlenecks on the table. When platform teams scale long-context and reasoning models past tens of thousands of tokens, they encounter four low-level physical walls. During the prefill phase, a transformer processes all prompt tokens simultaneously. Standard FlashAttention-2 achieves near-peak compute utilization by tiling the query matrix along the input sequence dimension. It assigns independent thread blocks across both batch items and attention heads.
Middle: GPUs are not always the best tool. At extreme scale, very large models and prompts can push past GPU memory. CPUs are much slower at neural network math. But they have ample RAM, no contention for GPU memory bandwidth, and they handle tasks GPUs do less well. Those tasks include very long sequences and nontransformer work such as tokenization and padding.

Opening: A static serving configuration works only when every user sends the exact same prompt length at a steady arrival rate. Real production traffic is never that predictable. One second your cluster faces a morning rush of short chat queries. A moment later, an autonomous coding agent submits a million-token repository prompt or spawns sixteen parallel reasoning branches. When an inference engine relies on static settings across those shifting workloads, four expensive failures occur. - **CPU dispatch stalls during decode.** Autoregressive decoding executes hundreds of tiny memory-bandwidth-bound kernels per token. Without static graph capture and fused operators, the GPU sits idle waiting for Python and the CUDA driver to launch each kernel. - **Speculative decoding regressions.** A fixed draft length accelerates structured code generation where draft acceptance is high. That same fixed draft length slows the cluster down when creative prompts or large batch sizes cause draft rejections and compute bottlenecks. - **Wasted depth and precision on trivial tokens.** Running all eighty layers of a frontier model in sixteen-bit precision just
Middle: Separate streams keep the pipeline full. While one token computes, the next token's KV is already in flight, which hides the transfer latency. Modern engines do this automatically through paged KV caching. Treat weight and KV movement as part of the pipeline. Always keep the next piece of data in flight while the current one computes.
Exercise sample: 1. **Decode CUDA Graph Bucket Padding and Shared Pool Sizing.** - **(a)** Use `select_cuda_graph_bucket()` with the default bucket ladder up to 256. Compute the selected bucket, dummy tokens added, and percentage padding overhead for active decode batches of 19 and 52, and explain what happens at batch size 300. - **(b)** Calculate the total HBM workspace allocated for buckets 8, 4, 2, and 1 at 32.0 MB per batch unit under isolated graph pools versus a FireworksAI decreasing-batch shared pool. State the exact megabytes and percentage saved. 2. **Fused Triton
