Opening: Traditional stateless web services fail and slow down in predictable ways. Each microservice call performs roughly the same work and returns within a uniform time window. Large language model serving behaves very differently. A single inference endpoint receives short chat prompts alongside massive document digests. Each request transitions from a compute-bound prefill phase into a memory-bandwidth-bound autoregressive decode loop. When tail latency spikes or throughput collapses across an LLM cluster, intuition often misleads engineers. Teams frequently assume the GPUs are saturated. Upon closer inspection, they discover that the Streaming Multiprocessors sit idle while a single host CPU core is pinned at 100% inside a Python tokenizer. In other incidents, `nvidia-smi` reports 100% GPU utilization while a deadlocked NCCL rank spins inside a busy-wait loop. That single deadlocked pod silently drops 12.5% of customer traffic into a black hole.
Middle: Output streaming can overlap with compute too. Send a few generated tokens to the user as soon as they exist, while the model works on the next ones. The network latency then hides behind the computation. The user sees a steady stream with no pauses.
Exercise sample: 1. **Three-Layer Profiling Attribution and Roofline Regime Analysis.** - **(a)** Use `evaluate_nvtx_kineto_and_ncu_profile()` to compute the combined GPU time of matrix multiplication and linear layers versus MoE dispatch and combine. Opening: Traditional web microservices finish a request in a single database query or function call. Site reliability engineers benchmark those services using aggregate HTTP Requests Per Second and end-to-end response time. Large language model serving breaks both metrics. A single prompt generating five hundred tokens executes five hundred and one separate forward passes through the neural network. The first pass runs a compute-bound matrix-matrix multiplication over the entire input prompt. The next five hundred passes run memory-bandwidth-bound matrix-vector multiplications that emit one token at a time. Because generation streams across hundreds of steps, end-to-end response time hides what users actually experience. Consider two serving clusters that both take 5 seconds to write a thousand-word essay. The first cluster sits silent for 4.9 seconds and then dumps all one thousand words onto the screen at once. Users assume the application has hung and close the tab. The second cluster emits the first word in 0.2 seconds and streams the remaining text smoothly over the next 4.8 seconds. Both systems report the exact
Middle: In *Hands-On LLM Serving and Optimization* Chapter 9, Yuan Tang demonstrates how systematic benchmarking guides real-world optimization of Qwen3-14B on an AWS g6e.2xlarge instance equipped with a single **NVIDIA L40S GPU**. That GPU provides 46 GiB of total VRAM.

Opening: Stateless web microservices scale predictably on CPU and memory utilization. When incoming HTTP traffic doubles, processor usage rises proportionally. A Kubernetes HorizontalPodAutoscaler then launches lightweight containers that become ready in a few seconds. Large language model serving breaks every assumption behind that traditional control loop. In a modern serving engine such as vLLM, SGLang, or TensorRT-LLM, the host CPU only parses HTTP requests, runs fast tokenization, and dispatches asynchronous GPU kernels. Meanwhile, the engine pre-allocates nearly all GPU High-Bandwidth Memory at startup to store model weights and the PagedAttention KV cache pool. A single active user streaming tokens in a continuous batching loop keeps the GPU kernel engine active nearly 100 percent of the time.
Middle: Traditional Cluster Autoscaler polls predefined cloud Auto Scaling Groups or GKE Node Auto-Provisioning limits and provisions fixed-template nodes. By contrast, **Karpenter** watches unschedulable pods directly via the Kubernetes API server, solves bin-packing constraints in **33.66 milliseconds**, and provisions optimal GPU instances using `NodePool` and `EC2NodeClass` resources. When jobs finish or KEDA scales pods down, Karpenter `WhenEmptyOrUnderutilized` consolidation automatically applies the `karpenter.sh/disrupted:NoSchedule` taint, drains empty nodes, and terminates the cloud instance within minutes.

Opening: Getting a model to answer a prompt on a single GPU is only the start of inference engineering. Once you expose that model to real users and autonomous agents, the failure modes change completely. Stateless web microservices either return a fast response or crash cleanly. By contrast, a large language model serving stack can fail partially and silently across several infrastructure layers at once. When a serving platform relies on standard microservice defaults instead of AI-native reliability controls, four operational disasters happen regularly. - **Silent GPU deaths and traffic blackholes.** An uncorrectable hardware memory fault on one GPU inside an eight-GPU Tensor Parallel group deadlocks the collective ring forever. Meanwhile, the Python HTTP web server on the host CPU stays alive and returns HTTP 200 to naive Kubernetes health checks. The broken pod then drops one-eighth of all cluster traffic into a black hole. - **Probe-induced cascading outages.** When an autoscaler launches new replicas that pull seventy-gigabyte weight files over shared storage, transient I/O contention delays responses on existing pods.
Middle: In latency-critical production serving, **always set recompute-only preemption (`--swap-space 0` on vLLM before v0.18)** and cap `--gpu-memory-utilization` between 0.85 and 0.92. When GPU KV cache usage exceeds 95 percent or waiting requests exceed 50 for 5 seconds, the gateway circuit breaker trips Open. To prevent rapid flapping between Open and Closed on every completed request, the circuit breaker enforces hysteresis. It stays Open until GPU KV cache usage drops below 80 percent and the waiting queue drops below 20.

Opening: Getting an inference container to answer a single test prompt on a laptop is easy. Keeping that same endpoint below a strict tail-latency target under heavy production load is much harder. Hundreds of users send variable-length requests simultaneously on a cloud cluster. Teams routinely discover that a cluster passing basic health checks is actually burning half its hardware budget on hidden bottlenecks. Consider what happens when a team deploys a sixty-four-GPU cluster without systematic benchmarking or telemetry. The service processes requests, and nobody notices that average GPU utilization sits near 45 percent. Scaling efficiency hovers around 52 percent across the fleet. Over a two-week run, thirty-one of those sixty-four GPUs perform no useful work. That idle capacity wastes roughly fifty thousand dollars in cloud spend.
Middle: This deterministic verification lab runs locally in Python via `code/part15/serve_benchmark_gke_nebius.py` and `code/part15/serve_benchmark_gke_nebius_lab.py` with zero external cloud dependencies. The Kubernetes manifests and cloud CLI commands in Sections 1 through 5 are designed for live GKE and Nebius MK8s GPU clusters and were **not run on live hardware** during handbook builds. All twenty-eight quantitative models, memory budgets, benchmark throughputs, latency decompositions, and autoscaling decisions in the Python lab are **tested and verified**.

Opening: Training a foundation model is a capital expenditure event with predictable batch shapes and sustained compute saturation. Serving that model behind a live API flips the economic equation into a continuous operating expense. Over the lifetime of a successful product, inference accounts for up to 90 percent of total machine learning infrastructure spend. This chapter builds a first-principles financial and capacity model for LLM inference. You will learn how to price input and output tokens, adjust unit economics for latency Goodput, and size KV cache concurrency across precisions. You will also plan multi-region fleets for diurnal traffic, select the right NVIDIA accelerator tier, and compute the break-even point between self-hosted GPUs and managed APIs.
Middle: Training is a one-time upfront capital investment. By contrast, inference scales continuously with every user query, multi-turn chat, and multi-step agent loop. A model provider recovers its training investment only when cumulative revenue across all inference calls exceeds total training cost.

Opening: You spent Parts 1 through 15 learning to wire the data center floor. You learned how Linux boots, how BGP chooses a path, how the Kubernetes scheduler places a Pod on a NUMA node, how RDMA bypasses the CPU, and how a vLLM engine streams tokens out of an H100. Each of those layers had a hardware or systems problem with a clean answer. The work in this part is a different kind of problem. The model you put into production tomorrow was trained yesterday, on a dataset that nobody can name precisely. The training ran in a notebook on one engineer's laptop, with a random seed that was picked because the first run looked good. None of the systems knowledge from the earlier chapters protects you from that notebook. The defect sits upstream of the GPU.
Middle: The diagram rests on one rule. Every arrow carries an artifact with an identifier, and no arrow carries an anonymous blob. If the pipeline cannot tell you which dataset trained the model on the canary stage, one of the arrows is missing its identifier.

Opening: It is 2 a.m., and a model that worked last week now gives poor predictions. The on-call engineer has one question first. What is different between this run and last week's run? Chapter 16.1 named the four pillars of MLOps and the gates a model must pass. This chapter opens two of them. Data versioning gives a dataset a permanent address. Experiment tracking turns each training run into a record you can compare with the next one. The on-call engineer can answer that question only if the tracking server responds in seconds. If the data layer cannot point to the exact bytes that trained the live model, rolling back is guesswork. These two tools are the audit trail that lets you prove where a production model came from.
Middle: The experiment record is the scientist's notebook. The registry entry is closer to a deployment manifest. The promotion gate from Chapter 16.1, Section 4.3, sits between the two. Here is that gate applied to this chapter's run record.

Opening: Picture a training run that finishes without errors while the model it produces gets slightly worse each week. Nobody changed the training code. Someone upstream renamed a column, the features drifted, and nobody noticed. This chapter is about the pipeline that stops that kind of silent failure. The previous chapter gave the training script and its dataset permanent addresses. This chapter builds the pipeline that produces those artifacts. A run record is only as reproducible as the pipeline that fed it the bytes. A feature store lets two teams share one feature definition instead of rewriting it from scratch.
Middle: The taxonomy matters because the feature store must make each function reproducible in both views. If the offline and online paths compute `log(amount)` with different libraries, parity breaks, and the model degrades without an error. The fix is one helper function that both views call, and that helper is versioned and signed.

Opening: Imagine a churn model that retrains every night. One Monday the feature build stops halfway, and the training job quietly uses last week's table. Nobody notices until the scores drift and someone asks why. An orchestrator exists to stop this kind of silent failure. It runs the stages in order, retries the ones that fail, skips the ones that have not changed, and records every run. If you operate a GPU cluster for AI, you also operate an orchestrator, whether or not you call it that. It schedules the training jobs, the feature builds, the evaluation runs, the promotion gate, and the rollback. When it is slow or down, every one of those workflows waits with it.
Middle: A nightly job fails at one in the morning with an image pull error. Run it again a few minutes later and it succeeds. A retry policy decides which failures deserve another try, and which ones should wake someone up.

Opening: Imagine a fine-tune of a large language model that must run for days. One machine cannot hold the job, so it spreads across several nodes and dozens of GPUs. Other AI work looks similar. A hyperparameter sweep launches hundreds of trials, and a reinforcement learning run adds and removes workers as it goes. The cluster primitives from Parts 1 through 15 can run these jobs. The frameworks in this chapter are what let your team write them as code. If you run a GPU cluster for AI, you also run a distributed compute layer, whether you name it or not. That layer schedules a PyTorch job across 64 GPUs with NCCL all-reduce, for example. It can also run a Ray Tune sweep across 200 trials and gather their metrics. Its most important job is elasticity. When one node fails, the job should continue on the surviving nodes instead of starting over. Kubernetes and the GPU operator are necessary, but the data scientist writes against this layer, not against them.
Middle: Suppose a 14-day run on 64 GPUs loses a few nodes over its life. The causes include disk failures, ECC errors, network blips and scheduled maintenance. The naive response is to restart the job. The cost of that restart is the full 14-day run, lost.

Opening: The earlier chapters gave training runs and datasets permanent addresses. This chapter turns those artifacts into deployments you can roll back. A production model passes through a chain. A training run produces a registry entry, the entry gets a stage, the stage drives a deployment, and the deployment starts serving replicas. Each link can break, and the platform team owns the contract at every one. The cluster you built in Parts 1 through 15 is where a registry fails. A registry that loses its stage transitions cannot say which model is live. A registry whose image tag cannot be re-pointed cannot roll back. A registry that lets any user write any stage does not enforce the promotion gate from Chapter 16.1. Each of these failures ends with someone paged at 2 a.m.
Middle: The reconciler's identity is what enforces the promotion gate. Only the reconciler can move a stage. The training pipeline can write a model version, and the reconciler can move it to staging or production, and no other identity can do either. This is the same split between writer and promoter that Chapter 16.2, Section 5.2 describes.

Opening: Chapter 15 showed what happens inside one inference engine. This chapter covers the system that sits in front of those engines on Kubernetes. The serving platform gives a model a URL, a canary, an autoscaler, an SLO, and a place in the cluster's IAM model. Without one, each team writes its own Flask service and runs it in a pod. With one, a team declares an InferenceService and the platform handles the rest. If you are the one paged when it breaks, you feel every gap. Read the rest of this chapter with that pager in mind. The serving platform is where MLOps meets Kubernetes. Think of the InferenceService as a single description of the whole serving path. You can read that path in one place. It records the registry's stage, the canary's traffic split, the autoscaler's GPU trigger, the predictor's runtime, the transformer, and the explainer. Each of those fields depends on cluster basics from Parts 1 through 5, such as namespaces, access rules, pod security, network policies, ingress,
Middle: Some teams need hundreds or thousands of small models, one per tenant, product, or user cohort. Giving each model its own replicas would waste most of the cluster. *Generative AI on Kubernetes* Chapter 1 introduces ModelMesh, a KServe-adjacent project that serves many models through one endpoint.
Exercise sample: Try these after the lab. If you get stuck, the solution sketch shows the shape of the answer. Then confirm the predictor receives the transformer's output unchanged. *Solution sketch:* an InferenceService with a transformer section. The transformer image runs `python transform.py --mode preprocess` and forwards the JSON. 2. **Add a custom GPU metric.** Expose the GPU's KV cache utilisation as a Prometheus gauge,

Opening: Picture a team that already runs a GPU cluster for training and serving models. Now the product team wants a chatbot that answers questions from the company's private documents. That chatbot is an LLM application, and it sits on top of the foundations from Chapter 16.1, the data pipelines from Chapter 16.3, and the serving platforms from Chapter 16.7. Three patterns make up most of this work. Retrieval-Augmented Generation, or RAG, lets a model answer from a private corpus. Vector databases give the model fast lookup across millions of embeddings. Agents let the model call tools and chain its reasoning steps together. If you run a GPU cluster for AI, you also run an LLM application. The application is what pays for the cluster, and its quality is the part the user sees. Its cost is the part the CFO sees. Your job as the platform team is to make the application fast, accurate, observable, and easy to roll back.
Middle: Production Kubernetes, chapter 9, explains that a distributed trace is built from spans, one for each service that handles a request. Your application has to emit those spans itself, and it has to pass the trace headers from one call to the next. An agent that calls a retriever and then a tool is a chain of spans, and the headers must survive every hop.
Exercise sample: Work through these after the lab. Each exercise turns one rule from this chapter into code you can test. If you are short on time, start with exercises 1 and 2, because they guard against the most expensive failures you will meet. 1. Add a freshness check. Implement a check that refuses to serve if the index age exceeds the freshness SLO, then verify that the check fires. *Solution sketch:* a middleware that reads the index metadata and rejects requests when the index is stale. 2. Add a hard iteration

Hands-on lab / 7.1 Prerequisites / 7.2 Step 1: deploy the eval suite runner / 7.3 Step 2: deploy the guardrail classifiers / 7.4 Step 3: run the eval suite / 7.5 Step 4: query the eval results
Opening: Chapter 16.1 introduced the promotion gate, the check that decides whether a model may reach production. For a classical model, that gate is usually one accuracy number on a held-out set. For an LLM, the gate is an eval suite. The suite answers four questions: does the LLM still do what you asked, does it avoid what you forbade, does it stay within its cost and latency budget, and does it stay within its safety limits. Without an eval suite, you ship prompt changes and model versions blind. LLM evaluation is harder than classical evaluation. A classifier has one main number to watch, but an LLM has dozens of behaviours, and each one can regress on its own. A prompt change can improve one question type while it hurts another. A model upgrade can help with code and hurt with chat. Your job as the platform team is to build a suite that catches each regression before a customer does.
Middle: Thresholds involve a trade-off. A low threshold catches more unsafe output, but it also blocks more safe output. A high threshold lets more unsafe output through. Tune the threshold on the eval set. The example goal in this chapter is to catch every unsafe output in the eval set while keeping the false-positive rate between 1 and 5 percent. Set your own target, based on your product's risk. The false-positive rate is the price you pay for safety.
Exercise sample: 1. Add a regression test for a new failure mode. Find a recent production failure you have seen, add it to the regression set, and confirm the test catches it. *Solution sketch:* a JSON entry in `eval/regression-v4.jsonl`, and a CI step that runs the new entry. 2. Reduce LLM-as-judge bias. Switch the judge to a different model family, then check whether the bias drops. *Solution sketch:* change the `judge-model` config to another family, rerun the suite, and compare scores. 3. Opening: Chapter 16.1 described the stale model. The model in production is months older than the training pipeline, because someone has to retrain it by hand. This chapter is the fix. Monitoring shows you the symptoms, drift detection points to the cause, and continual learning closes the loop with a retraining trigger. Your job as a platform engineer is a pipeline that catches drift in hours, not months. Monitoring is easy to skip, because its cost stays hidden until the model breaks. Suppose, as an example, that a model scores 0.92 AUC on the day it deploys. If the data drifts, the same model might score 0.78 six months later. Nobody notices, because nobody is watching. The customer notices first, and the on-call hears about it only when the failure is too large for the dashboards to miss.
Middle: Teams usually combine these mechanisms rather than pick one. For example, one team might run shadow mode for 24 hours, a canary for 30 minutes, and an A/B test for 7 days. Those durations are examples. Set yours from how fast your traffic and labels arrive. Before you promote a model, decide which of these mechanisms it must pass, and in what order.
Exercise sample: Each exercise builds one piece of the loop from this chapter, and you can do them in order. The solution sketches describe one way to do each one, not the only way you can do it. 1. Add a feature-drift detector. Use Evidently to compute the KS statistic for each feature, and alert when it exceeds 0.1. *Solution sketch:* a CronJob runs Evidently on the last hour of features, writes the result to Prometheus, and raises an alert on the metric. 2. Add a performance-triggered retraining. Fire the retraining DAG

Opening: The earlier chapters assumed a self-hosted Kubernetes platform on GKE, EKS, or AKS. In that setup the platform team runs the registry, the orchestrator, the feature store, and the serving stack itself. This chapter looks at the other option, where the cloud provider runs those same pieces as managed services. Three managed stacks cover this ground today: Vertex AI on GCP, SageMaker on AWS, and Azure Machine Learning on Azure. Each one bundles a studio (a notebook and IDE), a pipeline orchestrator, a feature store, a model registry, and an endpoint service for serving models. You get all five from one vendor, under one bill.
Middle: The data scientist gets a managed notebook and pipeline, so nobody has to provision infrastructure by hand. The platform team keeps the GPU pool, the feature store, and the vector store. The cost of that arrangement is that the GPU pool is yours to run.
Exercise sample: Try these after the lab. Opening: Parts 1 through 15 gave you the cluster primitives, and Chapter 16.5 added the scheduler for training jobs. Chapter 16.7 added the serving platform for inference. This chapter covers the NVIDIA layer that sits on top of all three. It has five parts: the NVIDIA AI Enterprise (NVAIE) software stack, the NGC catalog of containers and models, the NIM inference microservice, the NeMo framework for LLM customization, and the RAPIDS library for GPU-accelerated data science. NVAIE is NVIDIA's production software stack, sold with enterprise support. You can assemble the same pieces from open source, such as PyTorch, TensorRT, Triton, NeMo, and RAPIDS, and skip the support contract. That choice looks like the managed-versus-self-hosted decision from Chapter 16.11. You either pay for the support contract, or you pay engineers to maintain the pieces.
Middle: When someone asks which model runs in production, the answer should not depend on memory. A NIM image is a model in the NVAIE sense and also in the MLOps sense. The platform team therefore mirrors each NIM deployment in its own model registry.

Opening: Imagine a loan model that declined a customer last month. A regulator asks which data shaped that decision, and your team has days, not weeks, to answer. This chapter covers that kind of question. It is about the cases where your team does not automatically have the right to train or serve a model on its data. Privacy rules limit what you can train on. Fairness rules limit what you can serve. Model risk rules limit how you can deploy. The EU AI Act adds a class of "high-risk" AI systems with explicit obligations. Your platform team's job is to make the ML platform auditable against every rule that applies to the company.
Middle: 1. Read the request from a queue. 2. Identify the artifacts that hold the customer's data, such as warehouse tables, feature store rows, and training data. 3. Soon, data science, ML engineering, applied research, and product engineering all want the same GPU pool, registry, orchestrator, feature store, and serving platform. The platform team owns those shared parts. The teams own their own models and applications. Multi-tenancy is not optional. A platform that is not built for many teams becomes multi-tenant anyway, by accident. Teams end up sharing a namespace, a model registry, an ingress path, and an IAM policy. Their traffic and service levels then start to affect each other.

Opening: Read the diagram from left to right, and notice where you would look first during an incident. Every workload feeds one or more of the three pillars, and each pillar feeds a different consumer. Dashboards show trends, alerts fire when something crosses a line, and runbooks tell the on-call what to do next. Imagine an on-call engineer paged at night because a training job has slowed down. The dashboard is blank, the logs are spread across dozens of pods, and nobody knows which component changed. Without an observability foundation, the first hour of that incident goes to guessing.
Middle: Every exporter and every scraper has to agree on one format. The Prometheus exposition format is widely used for AI infrastructure metrics, and it is plain text. Each sample is one line that holds the metric name, its labels, and its value.
Exercise sample: Work through these in order. Each one checks a single idea from the chapter. 1. **Add a high-cardinality label test.** Add a metric with a label that has 10000 unique values. Watch how Prometheus behaves, then remove the label. *Solution sketch:* an exporter that emits `http_requests_total{user_id=...}`; watch the `prometheus_tsdb_head_series` metric. 2. **Add a structured log schema.** Define a JSON schema for your logs. Emit it from the application, then query Loki by the schema fields. *Solution sketch:* a Pydantic model in Python that emits structured JSON; Loki's label extraction from

Opening: Imagine you are the on-call engineer at two in the morning. The inference service has slowed down, and you have a few minutes to find out why. Chapter 17.1 named the three pillars of observability. This chapter covers the first one, metrics, which show how much of something is happening over time. The Prometheus and Grafana stack sits on the critical path of every alert, every SLO, and every capacity model. A misconfigured stack either pages the on-call at the wrong moment or stays silent while the cluster is down. That is why the platform team runs the stack as a production service, with SLOs, observability, and a runbook.
Middle: The Prometheus adapter ships in the kube-prometheus stack, but it is not an exporter. It also plays no part in Prometheus core functions. Instead, it is a client of Prometheus. It reads metrics from the Prometheus API and serves them through the Kubernetes metrics APIs. That lets workloads autoscale on those metrics.
Exercise sample: Work through these exercises after the lab, while the stack is still running. Each one extends the stack, and each comes with a sketch you can check your own answer against. 1. **Add a recording rule for the drift detector.** Precompute the per-feature drift score so that dashboards read it cheaply. *Solution sketch:* a recording rule that aggregates `evidently_drift_score` per feature. 2. **Add an alert for high cardinality.** Fire an alert when any metric's cardinality exceeds 1000. The 1000 limit is an example value. *Solution sketch:* a Prometheus alert on

Opening: It is 3 a.m., and a model endpoint has started returning errors. The on-call engineer needs three answers fast: what broke, what the system was doing at that moment, and where the time went. Chapter 17.1 named the three pillars, and Chapter 17.2 covered metrics, which answer the first question. This chapter covers the other two, logs and traces, using Loki and OpenTelemetry. Without logs, an engineer has to open a shell in each pod and read text by hand. Without traces, an engineer who sees 500 ms of latency cannot tell which step is slow, such as a 100 ms feature fetch hiding inside it.
Middle: Events are a native Kubernetes resource. Platform components use them to report what happened to an object, so they work as a kind of platform log. Unlike other logs, Events usually stay out of the logging backend. They are stored in etcd and kept for one hour by default. Operators most often read them when they want to know what actions were taken against an object.
Exercise sample: 1. **Add a structured log schema.** Define a JSON schema for the application logs, emit it from the application, and check it in CI. *Solution sketch:* a Pydantic model that emits structured JSON, and a CI step that validates the log output against the schema. 2. **Add a tail-sampling policy.** Keep 100% of error traces and 10% of healthy traces. *Solution sketch:* the tail_sampling processor from Section 2.2, with its expected_new_traces_per_sec and num_traces settings. 3. **Add a trace-to-logs link.** Put the trace ID in every log line, then check that

Hands-on lab / 6.1 Prerequisites / 6.2 Step 1: install the DCGM exporter / 6.3 Step 2: verify the metrics are flowing / 6.4 Step 3: build the GPU dashboard / 6.5 Step 4: add the GPU alert rules / 6.6 Step 5: simulate a GPU failure / 6.7 Step 6: verify the alert fires / 6.8 What to record / Exercises
Opening: Picture a training job that stops making progress at three in the morning. Nothing has crashed, the logs are quiet, and the on-call engineer has no idea which GPU is failing. Without GPU metrics, the first sign of trouble is usually a stuck job or a failed inference request. The DCGM exporter changes that. It turns "the job is stuck" into a specific finding, such as one GPU on one node logging memory errors. A stuck job also wastes GPU time you already paid for, and it can cost you a deadline. The exporter gives you a chance to act before that time is gone.
Middle: Error-correcting code (ECC) is the memory's built-in repair. *AI Systems Performance Engineering*, chapter 3, explains that ECC fixes single-bit errors as they happen and detects double-bit errors, raising them to the calling code. ECC is on by default on data center GPUs, and the book advises keeping it on for serious AI work.
Exercise sample: Try these after the lab. Each one asks you to build something you could use on your own cluster. 1. **Add a GPU temperature dashboard.** Build a Grafana dashboard that shows temperature for each GPU. Alert when temperature passes a limit you choose. The draft's example is 85 degrees. *Solution sketch:* start from `DCGM_FI_DEV_GPU_TEMP_CELSIUS`, add a Grafana panel, and write the alert rule. 2. **Add a retired-GPUs list.** Keep a list of GPUs retired because of ECC errors, and alert when a GPU joins it. *Solution sketch:* store the list

Opening: Every chapter in this part so far has been about collecting signal. This chapter is about acting on that signal. Alerting turns metrics and traces into pages for the on-call engineer. SLOs turn the same metrics into commitments the business can read. Health checks let Kubernetes decide whether a workload is alive and able to serve traffic. Imagine each piece is missing. Without alerting, the on-call learns about an outage from a customer. Without SLOs, nobody can say which alerts the business cares about most. Without health checks, Kubernetes keeps sending requests to a pod that cannot answer them.
Middle: Inhibition tackles a different kind of noise. When a whole cluster is down, its many lower-severity alerts add little new information. An inhibit rule can mute them, so the on-call sees the root cause first.
Exercise sample: Work through these on a lab cluster, not on production. Try to explain each result to a teammate before you move on. An on-call engineer who knows the failure modes can name the cause from the symptom and start the right fix. An engineer who does not know them spends the first minutes searching the wiki while the cluster stays stuck.

Opening: Picture a training job that used to finish overnight and now runs past the morning shift. The GPU graphs look busy, so nobody can say what changed. Without a method, the team guesses. It adds a node, raises the batch size or rewrites a kernel, and hopes for the best. Performance engineering replaces that guessing with a repeatable way to find the real bottleneck. The payoff is both speed and money. A team that finds the real bottleneck can often run the same work on fewer GPUs, with lower latency. A team that skips that step tends to pay for capacity its workload never uses.
Middle: Suppose the training job is slow and the on-call starts at the top of the tree. The walk below follows the steps the way the tree asks them. Each step records a reading and a decision.

This master checklist is that list. It ties together the components covered in Part 17 and in most of Parts 10, 11, 14, and 15. The checklist serves three readers in three ways. The platform team uses it as the reference for performance engineering. The on-call engineer uses it to ask whether the cluster is doing everything it should. The finance owner uses it to ask whether GPU spending is well used.
Middle: Avoid single-file bottlenecks. If every worker reads one large file, stripe it across storage targets or split it into chunks. Let each loader worker read a different file at the same time. This raises aggregate bandwidth from the storage system.

Opening: Imagine a Saturday night upgrade that leaves the GPU nodes stuck halfway through. Training jobs stop, the on-call engineer gets paged, and nobody knows which change caused it. The chapters in Part 17 so far have been about watching the cluster and acting on what you see. This chapter is about changing a cluster without breaking the work that runs on it. Upgrades are among the riskiest things a platform team does, because each one can break the service-level objectives (SLOs) the business depends on. Without a lifecycle process, upgrades happen whenever someone finds time, and the first real test often happens in production. With one, every upgrade follows the same path. It is planned, tested in staging, rolled out with a way back, and checked against the SLOs.
Middle: The software process has five generic activities: communication, planning, modelling, construction, and deployment. Umbrella activities run through the whole life of the project. A maintenance change moves through its own sequence of steps. The sequence can run in cycles, and a team can extend it with its own steps.
Exercise sample: 1. **Add a change request template.** Build a template with sections for what, why, risk, rollback plan, and test plan. *Solution sketch:* a Markdown template that becomes the default for every change request you open. 2. **Add an automated rollback test.** Add a CI step that runs the rollback and checks that the SLO recovers afterward. *Solution sketch:* a CI step runs the rollback, then verifies the SLO is met. 3. **Add a traffic forecast integration.** Add a step to the change request that checks the forecast for the proposed

Opening: Imagine a GPU training cluster that starts failing at two in the morning. Nobody knows who is in charge, so three engineers change things at the same time. The outage runs longer, and the post-mortem never gets written. An incident response process prevents that. It decides ahead of time who responds, how fast they act, and what the team records afterwards. The earlier chapters in Part 17 were about stopping incidents from starting. Alerting, failure modes, and cluster lifecycle all lower how often things break. They cannot bring the number to zero. This chapter covers what you do once prevention has failed.
Middle: The IC needs authority to act without waiting for a committee. In practice, the IC can roll back a change, drain a node, scale a workload, or change an SLO for the length of the incident. Agree on these powers before the incident starts. Nobody wants to debate who may roll back production at three in the morning.

The FinOps dashboard and right-sizing look back at what you already spent, and right-sizing feeds the order too. Picture a platform team that has just bought a rack of GPUs. A few months later, finance asks why the GPU bill keeps rising, and nobody can explain it. Part 17 so far has taught you to keep the cluster running. This chapter teaches you to pay for it on purpose.
Middle: Read these four metrics together. Low GPU use with full memory can still mean a workload needs its GPUs. The targets in the table are examples, so choose your own once you see how your workloads behave.

Hands-on lab / 7.1 Prerequisites / 7.2 Step 1: define the SLOs / 7.3 Step 2: track the error budget / 7.4 Step 3: run a chaos engineering experiment / 8 (lab step 4) -- skipping this part because the rest is rendered below / 7.5 Step 4: observe the SLO during the experiment / 7.6 Step 5: run the failover drill / 7.7 What to record
Opening: Suppose a platform team is asked how reliable its AI factory is. The honest answer is often "we hope it stays up." Reliability engineering replaces that hope with measurements, budgets and drills that show the service really does stay up. This chapter explains how to build that discipline on top of the observability work from Part 17. Customers do not care how many GPUs your cluster has or how it is wired. They care whether the service answers when they call it. An AI factory without reliability engineering depends on luck. An AI factory with it knows its uptime, because the targets are measured, the budget is tracked and the failure drills are run.
Middle: GPU hardware fails, and a spare node is the cheapest way to keep a training run going. N+1 redundancy keeps one spare for every group of nodes a workload needs. When a node fails, the scheduler moves the work to the spare. The AI Factory book, Chapter 9, makes the same point at the level of cloud services: the goal is no single point of failure, so one failed server does not stop the service.
Exercise sample: Work through these on your own cluster or in a sandbox, and write down what you find. 1. **Add an SLO alert.** Write a Prometheus alert that fires when the SLO is breached, and link it to a runbook. *Solution sketch:* the alert watches the SLI, carries critical severity and points to the runbook for that SLI. 2. **Add an error budget policy.** Write down what the team does when the budget runs out. *Solution sketch:* a short Markdown document that states the policy and gets reviewed on a schedule.

Opening: Chapter 17.13 teaches you how to prevent incidents. This chapter starts where that work stops, when prevention fails and something has to be brought back. Disaster recovery is the discipline that lets a team restore service within a time it agreed on in advance, with a known amount of lost data. For example, a team might aim to be back within 4 hours with no data loss. Those figures are examples you choose for your own platform, not industry standards. A disaster is any event that takes the cluster down beyond what the normal recovery steps can handle. It might be a regional outage, a ransomware attack, or a hardware failure that takes out a whole zone. When that happens, the disaster recovery plan is what your team follows, because the usual playbook no longer works.
Middle: Think about what the cluster stores. Model files and datasets live in object storage, the cluster's own state lives in etcd and Kubernetes secrets, and MLflow and Feast keep their records in PostgreSQL. Each target needs a backup method that fits it.
Exercise sample: 1. **Add a backup verification cron job.** Configure a CronJob that verifies the backup monthly. *Solution sketch:* Use a Kubernetes CronJob that restores the backup to staging, measures the RTO and RPO, and reports the results. 2. **Add a multi-region failover drill.** Schedule a quarterly drill that tests the multi-region failover. *Solution sketch:* Write a Chaos Mesh experiment that cordons all nodes in the primary region. The drill measures the failover latency and produces a post-mortem. 3. **Add a ransomware defence drill.** Schedule a quarterly drill that tests the ransomware

This chapter covers the person who responds when they happen. The runbook decides how long that response takes, because it tells the on-call engineer what to check first.

Opening: Picture the on-call engineer at the start of a shift. The dashboard shows thousands of time series, and the alert queue keeps growing. Every chapter in Part 17 has taught you to run the cluster you have today. This chapter prepares you for the larger cluster you will run next. That cluster is bigger, busier, and harder to watch by hand. Its metrics, logs, and incidents now outnumber what one person can read. The on-call engineer is being asked to do more with the same headcount, and AI-assisted operations is the answer many teams reach for. It uses AI to spot anomalies across huge numbers of metrics, draft runbooks from incident transcripts, rank likely root causes across traces and logs, and forecast capacity needs. The argument of this chapter is that without this help, operations effort grows with the number of people. With it, effort grows much more slowly.
Middle: The practical advice is to use these tools rather than fight them. Use high-level compilers such as Triton, which know what your hardware can do. Also watch new AI-driven optimization services. They can look like black boxes, but they hold a lot of hard-won performance knowledge.

You are done when the alerts reach the on-call engineer and you can name each false positive. 2. Add an LLM-based runbook generator. Opening: Picture an engineer on call at two in the morning. An alert shows that an unknown client is pulling large volumes of responses from your inference API. Is this a traffic spike, or is someone copying the model? The engineer can answer that quickly only if the team already wrote down what must be protected and how it could be attacked. That written list is the threat model. Every other chapter in this part starts from a known threat. This chapter explains how the team builds that list in the first place. Without a threat model, security controls are educated guesses. With one, each control points to the threat it answers, so an auditor who asks why it exists gets a clear answer.
Middle: Traffic from outside your network is the surface most people think of first. The public API is the entry point, and anyone on the internet can send it requests. The threats here are DDoS, API abuse, prompt injection, bypassed authentication, and rate-limit bypass.

Opening: Chapter 18.1 listed the threats to your GPU platform. This chapter covers the controls that close the gaps at the hardware and firmware layer. A GPU cluster is a stack of hardware, firmware, drivers, and workloads, and any layer in that stack can be attacked. Your job is to confirm that the hardware is trusted before a workload runs, and then to keep checking. The firmware sits at the bottom of that stack, so a problem there reaches everything above it. A model signing key on a compromised host protects nothing. A supply chain attestation for a tampered GPU proves nothing either. Hardware and firmware trust is what gives every other security control its value.
Middle: The BMC, or baseboard management controller, has its own attestation. It proves that the BMC is genuine and unmodified. The BMC sits outside the operating system, so check it as well and do not assume the operating system's checks cover it.
Exercise sample: These exercises turn the chapter's controls into alerts and pipelines. Opening: Imagine a model-serving team that ships a new image on a Friday afternoon. The image runs fine in staging, and nobody checks where its base layer came from or which libraries it bundles. On Monday, a scanner flags a serious flaw in one of those libraries, and nobody can say which running pods contain it. This chapter closes that gap. Chapter 18.2 verified the firmware. This chapter verifies the software that runs on top of it. A model-serving workload is a container image. That image was built from a Dockerfile, the Dockerfile started from a base image, and the base image came from a registry. Each link can be attacked, and each link can also be checked before the workload runs. Your job is to check them, and to keep checking after deployment.
Middle: 1. What is the current image signing rate? 2. What is the current image verification rate? 3. What is the current SBOM freshness? 4. What is the current vulnerability count (HIGH/CRITICAL)? 5. What is the current dependency pinning rate?

Opening: Read the diagram from left to right. A workload first proves who it is, through its ServiceAccount and Workload Identity. RBAC and NetworkPolicy then decide what that identity may do and where it may connect. Pod Security Standards and Secrets control what runs inside the pod itself. You can check each layer on its own, and that is what makes the whole stack auditable. Suppose three teams share one GPU cluster, and each team runs its training jobs in its own namespace. Chapters 18.2 and 18.3 checked the firmware and the software supply chain. This chapter checks the access controls on the cluster itself. A namespace marks a tenant. A ServiceAccount is the identity of a workload. A NetworkPolicy marks the network boundary, and a Pod Security Standard limits what a pod may do.
Middle: Writing patches by hand gives you the most flexibility. It also requires more domain knowledge, and it is harder to build and maintain. Weigh that cost against your use case and your team's skills. If the effort grows, the next section shows an approach with much less boilerplate.
Exercise sample: 1. **Write a least-privilege Role for the MLflow tracking server.** Give the server only the verbs and resources it needs. If you are unsure, start with read-only verbs, and add more only when a real request fails. *Solution sketch:* the Role YAML in Section 1.1, a matching RoleBinding, and the ServiceAccount reference. 2. **Configure Workload Identity for the model registry.** Bind the ServiceAccount to a cloud IAM role that can read and write the registry bucket. Before you finish, confirm that you can reach the bucket from inside the pod.

Opening: Chapter 18.4 checked who can reach the cluster. This chapter checks what a workload can reach once it is running on a GPU. A GPU reads system memory directly through DMA, which stands for Direct Memory Access. When two tenants share a GPU, that direct access is the path you have to contain. Three mechanisms draw the boundary between tenants. The IOMMU governs memory addresses, MIG governs the GPU's internal resources, and SR-IOV governs PCIe access. Your job as the platform team is to configure all three correctly. A mistake in any one of them can leave one tenant's memory open to another.
Middle: The cluster schedules the Pod onto a node that has that partition, and the workload runs inside it. Request the smallest partition that fits the workload. Then check the resource name against what your device plugin advertises.
Exercise sample: Try each exercise yourself before you read its solution sketch. Opening: Chapters 18.2 and 18.5 covered firmware trust and GPU sharing, and this chapter builds on both. Picture a regulated model that scores loan applications or reads patient records. The data is valuable, and an attacker who reaches the host can try to read it from memory. Standard controls tell the platform team what to configure. They do not prove that a privileged user on the host cannot look inside. This chapter covers the design that gives that proof. A BlueField DPU (Data Processing Unit) runs security work on its own processor, away from the host CPU. A confidential VM encrypts the workload's memory and registers. An attestation chain lets a remote verifier check that the stack started the way you approved.
Middle: Imagine a cloud host where the operator has root access. Normally that operator can read the memory of any VM on the machine. A confidential VM (CVM) closes that gap by encrypting its memory and registers with a key that only the VM holds.
Exercise sample: 1. **Add a BlueField provisioning alert.** Write an alert that fires when a BlueField is present but not provisioned. *Solution sketch:* a Prometheus exporter that reports the BlueField state, and an alert that fires on the "not provisioned" state. 2. **Add a DOCA services alert.** Write an alert that fires when any DOCA service is down. *Solution sketch:* an exporter for the DOCA services, and an alert that fires when the running count falls below the expected count. 3. **Add a CVM attestation alert.** Write an alert that fires when

Opening: Chapters 18.2-18.6 covered firmware, the supply chain, access control, GPU sharing, and the high-assurance stack. This chapter covers the data and the models, which are the assets the AI factory exists to protect. Every byte in the cluster carries a security requirement. Every model in the registry carries an intellectual property requirement. Every audit log entry carries a compliance requirement. Data and model protection is the last line of defence. Suppose an attacker compromises the firmware, gets past the access controls, and breaks the GPU sharing. The data on disk is still encrypted, and that encryption is what saves the customer's data. The audit log then shows the regulator that the controls worked.
Middle: An audit log is only useful if nobody can change it. Lock the bucket so entries cannot be deleted or overwritten during the retention period. The bucket should also alert on any attempt to modify it.
Exercise sample: Each exercise asks you to add one alert. Build it, then break the control on purpose in a test environment to confirm the alert fires. Opening: It is 2 a.m. A 24-hour training run has just died, and the on-call engineer is staring at a cluster that, by every individual check, looks healthy. `sinfo` shows every node healthy. The dashboards show GPU allocation near saturation. But fairshare never fired, so one team's 500-job hyperparameter sweep quietly consumed the entire cluster. And two co-scheduled jobs on the same node just corrupted each other's results, because neither was pinned to its own GPUs. Nothing is broken. The pieces are simply not integrated. Every piece in that story has its own chapter. The installation lives in 6.7, the configuration in 6.8, the accounting policy in 6.9, and the GPU scheduling in 6.10. The job scripts are 6.11, the day-2 operations are 6.14 and 6.15, the GPU metrics are 17.4, and the incident response is 17.11. A team that has read all of them can describe each piece in isolation. The capstone exists to answer the harder question, which is what holds the pieces together.
Middle: GPUs are generic resources, declared per node in the `Gres=` tail of the `NodeName` line, as `Gres=gpu:h100:8` in Section 3. Chapter 6.10 builds this up in detail, and the capstone keeps two settings from that chapter that are easy to lose. The first is `AutoDetect=nvml` in `gres.conf` on the compute nodes, which makes `slurmd` enumerate the GPUs it actually has instead of trusting a number that drifts. The second is `ConstrainDevices=yes` in `cgroup.conf`, which puts the allocated devices into the job's cgroup so the runtime can only see what was given to it. With both set, the corruption scenario from the opening cannot happen: the job's cgroup exposes exactly the
Exercise sample: 1. **Build the fairshare tree.** Create two accounts, `team-data-science` and `team-product`, each with a fairshare weight, and verify with `sacctmgr show account` that both are visible. *Solution sketch:* `sacctmgr add account name=team-product`, `sacctmgr modify account team-product set fairshare=80`, then `sacctmgr show account`. 2. **Reserve nodes for a critical run.** Create a reservation named `launch-window` for two nodes starting tomorrow at 08:00 with a 6-hour duration, restricted to one user, and submit a job into it with `--reservation`. *Solution sketch:* `scontrol create reservation name=launch-window StartTime=2026-11-09T08:00:00 Duration=6:00:00 Users=alice NodeCnt=2`, then `sbatch --reservation=launch-window

Opening: A team submits a two-node training job to a cluster that looks healthy on every dashboard. The nodes report `Ready`. The GPUs are advertised as schedulable. The queue says the team is within quota. Yet the job sits half-scheduled for the afternoon, one node busy and the other waiting, and no training step runs. In the same cluster a serving endpoint misses its latency target because a batch sweep holds the GPUs it needed. Nothing in that story is broken. The pieces were never integrated. The node pool, the GPU software, the queues, the gang scheduler and the health signal each have their own chapter in Parts 5, 11, 16 and 17. This capstone shows what holds them together.
Middle: The queue label has a counterpart in configuration. By default Kueue manages only labeled jobs. The setting `manageJobsWithoutQueueName: true` makes unlabeled jobs wait for a queue. The values file below also enables the PyTorchJob integration, which is commented out in the chart defaults, and excludes system namespaces.

Autoscale on in-flight work / 8. Build the reliability layer and trigger each part / 9. Hands-on lab / Exercises / Checkpoint questions / Sources and review notes
Opening: It is the week before a launch. The team has a model, a GKE project and an approved A100 quota. Three engineers each have a favourite engine, and nobody can say what number would settle the argument. The risk is not picking a slow engine. The risk is shipping with no way to tell whether the service meets its promise. Most inference outages are not caused by the engine. They come from the glue around it: a node that never advertised its GPU, a probe that killed a pod during weight loading, an autoscaler watching CPU on a machine that works on GPU. A capstone exists to join those parts and test the joints.
Middle: The engine caps are `--max-model-len=16384`, `--max-num-seqs=64` and `--gpu-memory-utilization=0.90`. They are starting values from the arithmetic above, not constants. The v0.32.0 defaults on an A100 are 256 for `--max-num-seqs` and 2048 for `--max-num-batched-tokens`, and 0.92 for the memory fraction. The old draft also set `--swap-space=0`. That flag was removed in v0.32.0, so it is gone.
Exercise sample: **Exercise 1.** A model has 80 layers, 8 KV heads of 128 dimensions and bf16 cache. How much KV memory does one 128K-token sequence need, and what does that say about a 40 GB GPU? *Solution sketch:* the per-token size is 2 times 80 times 8 times 128 times 2 bytes, which is 320 KiB. Times 131,072 tokens is 40 GiB, which does not fit a 40 GB GPU beside any weights. You must cap context, use more GPUs, or shrink the cache. Use `c03_kv_sizing.py` with the model's own numbers.

Opening: 1. **Checkpoint cadence is maths.** The best interval grows with the square root of the checkpoint cost times the mean time between interruptions (section 2). 2. **Sharded checkpoints.** Every rank writes its own part, nobody gathers the model, and the checkpoint can be loaded at a different world size (sections 3 to 5). 3. **Elastic restart has limits.** `torchrun` restarts workers but cannot replace a dead node. Only the scheduler can (section 6). 4. **Hang detection.** The watchdog, the heartbeat monitor and the flight recorder turn an idle cluster into a dump and a kill (section 7). 5. **Straggler detection.** Total step time hides a slow rank, so record time outside collectives (section 8). It is 03:10 and a training run that started nine days ago has stopped moving. The loss curve ends in a flat line, GPU utilisation reads zero on every node, and the job is still listed as running. Somebody has to decide whether to wait, restart, or page the network team. Whatever they decide, the last
Middle: The smoke test starts two workers with `--max-restarts=6`, raises an error at step 6 on the first attempt, and checks that the second attempt resumes from step 4 and finishes at step 12. It passed. Three launches in the container went wrong at startup. Two failed with a gloo connection refused error, apparently because one worker tried to connect before the other was listening. The third hung until I stopped it. I did not root-cause any of them, and the smoke helper allows extra restarts to absorb the first kind. It shows why a restart budget above zero is useful even for a clean job, and it is a CPU-only
Exercise sample: 1. **Compute your interval.** A 256-GPU job has a 400 GB checkpoint, a 10 GB per second file system and a measured interruption every 20 hours. Compute the Daly interval and the waste with a 900-second restart. *Solution sketch:* cost is 400 divided by 10, so 40 seconds. Run `c04_checkpoint_interval.py --mtbf-hours 20 --cost-seconds 40 --restart-seconds 900`. The interval is 2,360 seconds and the waste is about 4.5 percent. Opening: It is 2 a.m. A 64-GPU pretraining run has slowed down, and the dashboard looks healthy. Every InfiniBand port reports active and every error counter reads zero. Yet gradient synchronization takes much longer than it did three months ago. The dashboard is truthful. It describes the state of the links, not the bandwidth of the data path. A fabric is not a state you can observe. It is a number you must measure, layer by layer, and then keep. Without a saved number, nobody can say whether the fabric changed or the workload did. The argument then runs on opinions while GPUs sit idle.
Middle: GPUDirect RDMA depends on physical layout. NCCL's `NCCL_NET_GDR_LEVEL` variable sets the maximum distance between NIC and GPU for which GPUDirect is used. `PIX` allows it only on the same PCI switch. When no level is set, NCCL picks one from the architecture. The common causes of a collapse are the missing module, PCIe ACS settings that block peer traffic, and a far GPU-NIC pair. The ACS and BIOS causes come from the nvidia-devops Volume 07 chapter on GPUDirect and are not re-verified here.
Exercise sample: 1. **Check the arithmetic.** A 16-rank AllReduce moves 1 GiB in 44,739.2 microseconds. Compute algbw, busbw and efficiency against a 400 Gbps link. *Solution sketch:* algbw is 1,073,741,824 bytes divided by 44,739.2 microseconds, which is 24.0 GB/s. The factor is 2 times 15 over 16, which is 1.875, so busbw is 45.0 GB/s. A 400 Gbps link is 50 GB/s, so efficiency is 0.90. 2. **Read the transport.** A run prints `NCCL INFO NCCL_IB_DISABLE set by environment to 1` and `Using network Socket`. Name the failure and the analyser flags

One GPU on that node has been reporting Xid errors for two days. Nothing drained the node and nothing paged anyone, so the scheduler kept sending work to failing hardware. Every check in that story was green. The exporter was up, Prometheus was scraping and the dashboard looked healthy. What was missing was the loop between the signal and the action. That loop is this capstone.

It runs it, reads it against the safety model, and adds what it lacks. The diagram shows the whole loop. The solid boxes are in the Go operator. The dashed boxes are in the reference model.

Opening: It is Monday morning and the chat team posts in the platform channel. Their new model server has been Pending for two hours, waiting for one slice of 3g.40gb. The dashboard shows twelve idle slices of 1g.10gb on the very same nodes. Nothing is broken. The platform simply holds the right amount of GPU in the wrong shapes. That gap between free capacity and usable capacity is the whole subject of this capstone. A whole GPU is easy to schedule and easy to bill, but a small service wastes most of it. Splitting the GPU fixes the waste and creates new problems: shapes that do not fit, slices that nobody can use, reconfigurations that need a drained node, and a bill that must be divided by slice.
Middle: At quarter end the finance partner asks which team used how much GPU, and why two teams with the same pod count have very different invoices. The platform has pod counts and no way to price a 1g slice against a 3g slice. A slice becomes money only after someone defines a price.

The planner treats `1g.20gb` as its own profile with starts 0, 2, 4 and 6. 2. **Waste less.** Two GPUs hold seven `1g.10gb` and strand a memory slice each. Opening: On a Monday the finance lead asks one question: how many GPUs do we need next year, and should we buy them or rent them? The platform team has three answers in three spreadsheets. Training says 100 GPUs for six weeks every quarter. Inference says its traffic doubled since spring. The research group says "as many as you can give us." Nobody can say what the sum is, how sure they are, or what happens if one of the numbers is wrong. Chapter 17.12 taught the first version of this skill with a simple rule: current count, times growth, times a buffer. That rule is a good start for a single pool. A real estate has four kinds of work with different shapes. Training is a large burst with a deadline. Fine-tuning is a steady trickle. Inference follows customer traffic and needs spare replicas. Notebooks follow the working day and are mostly idle.
Middle: On-demand capacity costs the most per hour and charges only for hours used. Reserved capacity costs less per hour and charges for every hour of the commitment. Owned capacity has a fixed cost for every hour of its life, plus running cost. It is cheaper per hour still, if you can keep it busy. Spot capacity is cheaper but can be taken away. The model covers spot with one formula, and the tests check it. Spot is best for work that checkpoints well.
Exercise sample: 1. **Add a spot tier for training.** Change the cost model so the burst demand from training is bought at a spot price with a rework fraction. *Solution sketch:* use `spot_effective_price` for the training share of demand above the commit, keep on-demand for inference, and add a test where a high rework fraction makes spot lose to on-demand. 2. **Fit the trend to your own history.** Replace the placeholder QPS with 12 months from your gateway and check the band. *Solution sketch:* run `forecast_band`, compare the width to the 8.8

Opening: A customer tells you on a call that they want "an AI platform by spring." Nobody on the call can say what the platform will run, who will operate it, how it will be judged, or who signs the cheque. Two months later the team has built a cluster that is technically sound and that the customer does not want. The failure was not in the cluster. It was in the first week. This capstone teaches the part of the job that happens before and around the technical build. You will learn what to ask, how to turn the answers into a short list of risks, how to score the customer's current state, how to turn requirements into a design with the costs written down, how to run a proof of concept that can fail honestly, and how to hand the result over. The method is a sequence of small artefacts, each of which another person can check.
Middle: My procedure is the author's practice. List the hard constraints such as residency, deadline and team size. List the drivers that can be traded, such as cost, speed and control. Write at least two options, including the one the customer prefers and one deliberately different. State what each option costs this customer. Choose, say why in one sentence, and write when you would change your mind.
Exercise sample: 1. **Add a question.** The questionnaire does not ask about model update frequency. Add a question with a reason and a risk rule that fires when updates are weekly and no deployment pipeline exists. *Solution sketch:* add the question to `c10_questionnaire.json` in the workload area. Add a rule that tests the update answer together with a scorecard-derived answer, or add a second question about pipelines. Opening: An interviewer says "design a training cluster" and goes quiet. Candidates who start drawing racks lose the room, because nobody has said how big the model is or how long the run may take. A good answer starts with a workload, turns it into a GPU count, and then lets that count decide the network, the storage and the scheduler. This chapter is a capstone for the whole handbook. It reuses the Slurm cluster from [19.1](./01-capstone-a-slurm-gpu-cluster.md), the Kubernetes platform from [19.2](./02-capstone-a-kubernetes-gpu-platform-with-kai-and-kueue.md), the fault-tolerant training job from [19.4](./04-capstone-multi-node-training-with-fault-tolerance.md), the fabric tests from [19.5](./05-capstone-an-nccl-tests-fabric-lab.md) and the capacity model from [19.9](./09-capstone-capacity-planning.md). It does not repeat them. It shows how to join them into one design you can speak aloud in 45 minutes.
Middle: The 3 percent budget is my policy default, not a standard. The reading is simple: a synchronous checkpoint every 30 minutes at 45 GB/s costs 1.2 percent of the run, which is about 27 thousand dollars at the placeholder price. At 10 GB/s the stall would be 5.4 percent. The GB300 document also notes that asynchronous checkpointing lets training continue while the write happens in the background. Then the stall is only the in-memory snapshot, and the script models that with an extra argument.
Exercise sample: 1. The model is 13 billion parameters on 1 trillion tokens in 14 days at 45 percent MFU on the same B200. How many ideal GPUs? Solution sketch: FLOPs are 6 times 13e9 times 1e12, which is 7.8e22. Divide by 2.25e15 times 0.45 times 14 times 86,400 seconds, which is about 63.7 GPUs. Round to 8 nodes, then run `size_gpu_pool` for inflation and spares. 2. Count switches for 1,024 endpoints on 64-port switches at 1:1. Solution sketch: 32 down per leaf gives 32 leaf switches, and 32 uplinks each give

Opening: A platform team is asked to run the language models for the whole company. The support team wants a chat assistant, finance wants document summaries overnight, and an analytics group wants a large model for reports. Each team asked for its own GPUs. Together they asked for more than the company can pay for. That request is the system design question in this chapter. It is not "design a public API", which chapter 20.13 already covers for a vendor with outside customers. Here the customers are internal teams, the traffic is small, the models differ in size, and the pool is shared. The skill being tested is how you turn several unrelated demands into one pool that stays fast, stays cheap and stays safe to change.
Middle: The policy derives its numbers from the sizing. Little's law says in-flight requests equal rate times time in system. At the ceiling, the in-flight count equals the design batch. The target is 70 percent of that batch, which leaves room for growth during a cold start. For the assistant that is 45 requests per replica, from a batch of 64.
Exercise sample: 1. **Change the traffic.** The assistant's peak rises from 30 to 60 QPS. How many replicas does it need, and what happens to the pool? Worked sketch: at 9.19 req/s and 90 percent utilisation each replica carries 8.27 req/s. Sixty divided by 8.27 is 7.3, so 8 replicas. The assistant now needs 8 GPUs at peak, so the shared peak rises by 4. Run the script with a new `Workload` to confirm. 2. **Move to H200.** Replace the GPU and read the result. Worked sketch: call `replica_capacity(w, gpu="h200")`. The 72B

Opening: An interviewer says: "Design the infrastructure for an ML research team. They run notebooks, experiments, sweeps, training and evaluation. Today it is five people. Next year it is a hundred." This capstone chapter serves that design round. It does not claim that any particular company asks this question. It says what the question can test and how to answer it. The question tests whether you can serve people who want opposite things. Researchers want a GPU now, with no questions. The platform owner wants every GPU busy and every dollar traced to a team. A senior answer shows that you see both pressures and picks mechanisms that satisfy each. It also tests whether you keep the research loop honest, so that a result from last month can be reproduced this month.
Middle: A team owns a quarter of the cluster and is not using it this week. Another team runs a hundred-run sweep on the idle quarter. On Wednesday the owners return with a 12-GPU training job and find every GPU taken. This is the central trade-off. Idle capacity is waste, and lending it makes owners wait.
Exercise sample: 1. **Change the lending rule.** Add a lending cap so that a team can lend at most half its quota. *Solution sketch:* each resource entry in a ClusterQueue has a `lendingLimit` field next to `borrowingLimit` in the CRD. In the simulator, subtract a lending cap from what other teams may borrow, then check that utilisation falls and owner wait stays at zero. 2. **Protect training from preemption.** Show that lowering the sweep priority below evaluation changes who is preempted. *Solution sketch:* change `PRIO` in `c13_fairshare_sim.py`, rerun, and count preemptions by

Reading a chapter and answering a question are different skills, and only the second one is tested in a loop. This chapter turns the handbook into a practice bank. Opening: The night before an interview you open your notes and find forty pages. You cannot reread them, and you do not know which numbers matter. A cheat sheet fixes that, but only if you can trust it. A sheet that holds one wrong figure, such as a link rate or an Xid action, costs you more than having no sheet, because you will say it with confidence. This chapter gives you one general sheet for an AI-infrastructure interview. Every row names the handbook chapter it came from, and a script refuses any row that does not. Anything those chapters labelled unverified is left out. The chapter then teaches the part that notes alone cannot: how to memorise the rows, and what order to say them in when someone is watching.
Middle: Learn the formulas of sections E to I first, because they let you rebuild many rows. Learn the Xid and failure rows next, because they are lookups. Learn the design plan last, because the mock rounds practise it.
Exercise sample: 1. **Add three rows.** Add three rows to section C from [chapter 20.23](../part-20-nebius-sre-interview/23-kubernetes-failure-drills-for-gpu-platforms.md), each with an anchor, and run the validator. *Solution sketch:* copy the row format, pick a figure that appears in the chapter text, such as a back-off value, and use it as the anchor. Opening: Honest status first. The first draft of this chapter was written on a Mac with no Docker daemon. It has since been run on Linux, with limits. The scripts ran in a throwaway container, `ubuntu:24.04`, image digest `sha256:fe81560990fae86dc4743202b2cb4d3df98d0bcf949173cecef15ce1127ef852`, arm64, on the Docker VM kernel `6.18.37-0-virt` with 2 CPUs and no swap. **A container shares the VM's kernel and hides parts of it, so some output here differs from a real server.** Section "What the container run showed" lists the differences, and "Not verified" lists what still could not run. This chapter serves the first stage of the loop, the Linux console round. Nebius says the technical interviews include "practical tasks in the Linux console" and "coding, troubleshooting, debugging". The job posting asks for debugging "from kernel to application layer". The plan, section 1, also records Nebius's own advice for every round: say your reasoning aloud, ask for hints, offer alternatives.
Middle: Notice the shape. Every step was a question that halved the search space. The book calls this latency analysis and compares it to a binary search. Is it on-CPU or off-CPU? If off-CPU, what is it waiting for? If disk, is it queueing or being serviced?

Containers and Kubernetes change the answer / How to answer out loud / Question 1. "A service logs Too many open files. What do you do?" / Question 2. "df shows the disk is full but du finds nothing. Why?" / Question 3. "The file is 666 and root runs the service, but it cannot open it." / Question 4. "How would you use strace on production, and what do you look at?" / Question 5. "A pod works locally and fails in the cluster with Permission denied." / Worked scenarios / Scenario 1: the gateway that stops accepting / Scenario 2: the disk that is full of nothing / Scenario 3: no space left, but half the disk is free / Scenario 4: mode 755 and still Permission denied / Scenario 5: the ACL that a chmod cancelled / Scenario 6: readable file, locked directory / Scenario 7: mode 777 and Read-only file system / Scenario 8: root cannot delete the file / Hands-on lab / Part A: offline checks / Part B: on Linux / What the container run showed / Drill questions / Common mistakes and weak answers / Connections to the rest of the handbook / Sources and review notes / Explicitly labelled inferences / Not verified
Opening: A service is failing and the interviewer has given you a shell. The log says `Too many open files`, or `No space left on device` while the disk looks half empty, or `Permission denied` on a file whose mode is 666. You have a few minutes and every command you type is being judged. This chapter gives you the mechanisms behind those three messages and the order in which to check them. Run on Linux, in a container. The scripts and commands here ran in a throwaway `ubuntu:24.04` container, image digest `sha256:fe81560990fae86dc4743202b2cb4d3df98d0bcf949173cecef15ce1127ef852`, arm64, on the Docker VM kernel `6.18.37-0-virt`. The scripts ran as an ordinary user named `u` that I created in the container. Three scenarios needed the container started with `--security-opt seccomp=unconfined --security-opt apparmor=unconfined`, because Docker's default profile blocks unprivileged user namespaces. Scenario 8 needed `--cap-add LINUX_IMMUTABLE`. Neither used `--privileged`. A container shares the VM's kernel and hides parts of it, so the section "What the container run showed" lists what differs from a real server. Output marked "real
Middle: Then mandatory access control. The capabilities manual says `CAP_DAC_OVERRIDE` bypasses discretionary checks, meaning mode bits and ACLs. The AppArmor manual says a confined process is restricted even as root, and that profiles run in enforce or complain mode. For evidence, the ausearch manual gives `ausearch --start today -m avc -i` for SELinux denials, and the Kubernetes AppArmor page says denials appear in `dmesg` and in `journalctl`. I did not verify `getenforce`, `ls -Z` or `restorecon` against a manual, so check them before you quote them.

Opening: This chapter serves the Linux console round. Nebius describes the technical interviews as including "practical tasks in the Linux console", and as covering "coding, troubleshooting, debugging". The job posting asks for debugging "from kernel to application layer". One candidate reported a one-hour Linux troubleshooting stage done through the command line, and a separate 30-minute production incident stage. These are anecdotes, not a promise. Prepare for the hardest version. The interviewer is checking whether you can name the resource that is short, prove it with one number, and say what you would change. Systems Performance by Brendan Gregg is on the official Nebius reading list. This chapter follows its chapters on CPUs, memory, disks, perf and BPF, and adds the container and GPU host parts.
Middle: The key detail for inference hosts: `memory.current` includes page cache. The kernel documentation lists file cache in the memory statistics of the cgroup. So reading a 15 GiB weights file adds cache to the container. That cache is reclaimable, so it rarely causes a kill, but it makes `memory.current` look alarming. cAdvisor documents `container_memory_working_set_bytes` and `container_memory_cache` as separate gauges for this reason. Alert on the working set against the limit.

Opening: This chapter serves the Linux console round, and it also feeds the 30-minute production-incident round. Nebius states that technical interviews include "practical tasks in the Linux console", "coding, troubleshooting, debugging" and knowledge of "service management, Linux/Unix, networks". The job posting asks for debugging "from kernel to application layer". One candidate reported a Linux server troubleshooting round of one hour on the command line. Nebius has not published which network faults it uses, so this chapter teaches the method and the common faults. The interviewer is checking three things. Can you name what an error message really means? Can you pick the next command by reasoning, not by habit? Can you say what you would change, and what you would not touch on a live system?
Middle: The second probe got no reply only because the dummy interface has no peer. The point is where the error comes from. This one is local. On a real overlay the same step shows up as a missing reply or an ICMP packet-too-big message from a router on the path. The book says a default ping sends 56 bytes of payload, which is 84 bytes with headers, and that matches the numbers above.

Opening: The interviewer pastes a 5xx log into a shell and says: "Which client is hammering us, and when did it start? You have five minutes." This chapter serves the Linux console round. Nebius describes the first technical round as including "practical tasks in the Linux console", and says that standard console tools and utilities are assessed. The job posting also asks for experience with Linux and Unix, service management and networks. One candidate reported a one-hour Linux troubleshooting stage done through the command line. Nebius has not published its tasks. So this chapter trains the skill that every such task needs: turn a messy text file into a defensible number quickly, without a script editor, and say aloud why the pipeline is right.
Middle: I ran this with logrotate on Ubuntu 24.04. A Python writer held two logs open and wrote a line every half second. One config used plain `create`, the other `copytruncate`, and I forced a rotation with `-f` in the middle. The result is the whole lesson in four numbers.
