Opening: You get a problem statement and an empty editor. The interviewer says: "Take your time, talk me through it." Nothing is hidden about the format, but a lot is hidden inside it. This chapter serves the coding part of the first technical interview. Nebius's interview page says the technical interviews include "at least one practical coding / algorithms / data-structures task". That is the plan's wording, taken from the official page. The same page says you must write working, testable code in a language of your choice, with Python or Go named as examples. It lists classical algorithms and data structures as the topic. It also lists the behaviours that are graded. You should say your thinking aloud, ask for hints, review your code for errors before you submit, offer alternatives, and adapt when the parameters change.
Middle: The differences to remember are these. A missing map key returns the zero value, so `m[r]++` works without a default dictionary. A heap needs five methods because it is an interface. `sort.Slice` is not stable, so use `sort.SliceStable` if equal keys must keep their order. A string is a byte sequence, and ranging over it yields runes. This costs a candidate who treats a string as an array of characters.

Tell me which endpoints are slowest, and which ones have been failing in the last five minutes." This chapter serves the coding round. One candidate reported two LeetCode easy-to-medium problems in a one-hour round. Nebius has not published its problems, so this chapter does not guess a list. It teaches the patterns behind most easy and medium problems, framed as SRE situations.
Middle: Kahn's algorithm: count how many unmet dependencies each node has, start with those at zero, and each time you finish a node, reduce the count of the nodes that waited on it. If nodes remain at the end, they are on a cycle or blocked by one.

### part-20-nebius-sre-interview/10-complexity-latency-numbers-and-measuring-your-own-code.md
Title: 20.10 Complexity, latency numbers and measuring your own code | words 5203 | code blocks 14
Headings: What the interviewer is testing / The core ideas / 1. Say Big-O in a way that survives a follow-up / 2. The hidden costs of Python operations, measured / 3. Space against time / 4. The latency numbers, with sources and dates / 5. Arithmetic that makes the numbers useful / 6. Why the p99 of a fan-out is worse than the p99 of one call / 7. Caches and locality / 8. Measuring your own code with timeit and cProfile / 9. Why measured numbers differ from the textbook / 10. Will this loop of ten million items finish in a second? / How to answer out loud / Worked scenarios / Scenario A. The blocklist check / Scenario B. The fan-out dashboard that looks healthy / Hands-on lab / Drill questions / Common mistakes and weak answers / Connections to the rest of the handbook / Sources and review notes / Verification log
Opening: You finish a coding answer and the interviewer asks, "What is the complexity, and would it survive ten million items?" This chapter serves the coding round and feeds the design round, where the plan lists "estimate performance and compute". Start every complexity answer by naming the variables. Here there are two: the number of log lines, call it n, and the number of blocked addresses, call it m. A list scan costs O(m) per line, so the loop costs O(n times m). Make the blocked list a set and the cost per line is O(1) on average, so the loop costs O(n). Say "on average" because a hash table can degrade when many keys collide.
Middle: So with 100 backends, nearly two requests in three include a call from the slow 1 percent. The 99th percentile of the whole request is no longer the 99th percentile of one call. You can run the formula backwards. To keep the overall p99 at 0.99 with 100 calls, each call must meet its target at the 0.9999 quantile, roughly the 99.99th percentile. Dean and Barroso make this argument in "The Tail at Scale". Their example is a server with a one-second p99, and with 100 such servers in parallel they report that 63 percent of user requests take more than one second, the same figure as the table above.

Opening: This chapter serves the system design round. The plan records Nebius's own wording for that round as: a whole or partial distributed system, meet load and availability targets, and "estimate performance and compute". It also lists data storage and processing trade-offs. Estimation is the part where you stop drawing boxes and say numbers. The interviewer does not want a perfect answer. They want to hear you choose inputs, state them, multiply them out, and then check the result against common sense. A candidate who says "it depends" and stops loses points. A candidate who says "about nine H100s, and here is what would change that" gains them.
Middle: That is the ceiling for one GPU: 5.68 requests per second, which is about 2,840 output tokens per second. It is not a promise. Real engines overlap prefill chunks with decode and lose some time to scheduling, so measure.

What happens?" / Question 5: "How do you handle a tenant spike?" / Worked scenarios / Scenario 1: size a quota for a tenant / Scenario 2: TTFT doubled, GPU utilisation 40% / Scenario 3: the retry storm / Hands-on lab / Drill questions / Common mistakes and weak answers / Connections to the rest of the handbook / Sources and review notes / Verification log
Opening: This chapter serves the system design round. Nebius states that this round asks you to design a whole or partial distributed system, meet load and availability targets, estimate performance and compute, and discuss data storage and processing trade-offs. It also says you may use any language or technology, should speak your reasoning aloud, ask for hints and offer alternatives. The job posting adds three lines that this design touches directly. It asks for request routing and retry logic so transient failures do not reach users. It asks for Kubernetes autoscaling tuned for GPU efficiency. It asks for alert design and SLOs for high-throughput APIs. So the interviewer wants a design where reliability is a feature of the architecture, not an afterthought.
Middle: For warm pools, keep popular models always resident with at least two replicas. Keep weights on local NVMe or a node-local cache so a new replica does not download from the object store. LLMs in Production, Chapter 6, describes a hybrid where weights are downloaded once and kept on a mounted volume so new instances skip the download. Warm up a new replica with a few real requests before it receives traffic, so CUDA graphs and caches are ready.

This chapter serves the system design round. The official wording asks you to design "a whole or partial distributed system" that meets load and availability targets, and to estimate performance and compute. The job posting adds request routing, self-healing systems and Kubernetes autoscaling tuned for GPU efficiency. Two skills are checked at once. First, can you split an endpoint into a control plane that owns lifecycle and a data plane that serves traffic. Second, can you ship a new model version when you cannot simply double your GPUs. Nebius has not published its rollout design, so the interviewer wants your reasoning and your trade-offs, not recall.
Middle: The simulator makes the trade-off visible. It models one replica as one GPU slot, a new pod that holds its slot while loading, and the controller loop of scaling down old pods while availability stays above the floor and scaling up new ones within the pod cap. I ran four settings for 8 replicas and a load time of 4 ticks.

Opening: The interviewer says: "You run telemetry for an inference platform with hundreds of GPU replicas and thousands of tenants. Design the pipeline." This chapter serves the system design round. It also feeds the project deep dive, because every operator has a story about a metric that grew out of control. The job posting asks you to "design and refine telemetry pipelines (metrics, logs, traces) that turn very large volumes of data into actionable insight". Nebius says its system design round asks for a whole or partial distributed system, load and availability targets, performance estimates and storage trade-offs. The posting also asks for Prometheus, Grafana and alert design for high-throughput APIs. Nebius has not published how its own pipeline works. Everything here about design choices is my reasoning, labelled as such.
Middle: The OpenTelemetry sampling page defines both approaches. Head sampling decides as early as possible, without looking at the whole trace. A common form hashes the trace id and keeps a set percentage. It is simple and efficient. It cannot guarantee that traces with errors are kept. Tail sampling decides after it sees all or most of the spans, so it can keep errors, slow traces and chosen attributes. The page says larger systems almost always need it. It also says high-volume services may combine both, with head sampling first to cut load.

Opening: The interviewer says: "Design a system that detects a failing GPU before the customer does, using health checks, telemetry and automatic hardware replacement." This is the system design round. One public source reports this as a Nebius prompt, but it is a secondary source and I could not verify it as an actual question. The posting lists "incident detect/isolate/remediate with automation and runbooks" and "self-healing systems". It also asks for knowledge of "how distributed back-ends fail". The design is not about one clever alert. It is about a loop: notice, decide, remove from service, repair, prove, return. Then you must show that the loop cannot hurt you. An automation that drains the fleet because one bad driver fired a false alarm is worse than no automation.
Middle: The unit of failure also matters. If one bad GPU takes down an eight-GPU replica, then the replica is the unit. For a rack-scale system with 72 GPUs in one NVLink domain, one fault can affect a much larger unit and the spare must be larger as well. I did not verify how failures are contained in such a rack, so say that out loud rather than guess.

One candidate reported a one-hour design exercise. Another, for a Senior SRE loop in London, reported no proper system design round [report]. Prepare for the hard version.
Middle: Young's rule comes from J. W. Young, Communications of the ACM 17(9), 1974. I confirmed its form in Aupy, Benoit, Herault, Robert and Dongarra, "Optimal Checkpointing Period: Time vs. Energy", fetched 2026-11-09. There the period is the square root of 2 x C x mu, plus C, where C is the checkpoint write time and mu is the mean time between failures of the whole platform. The 153 minutes above is the square-root part, the compute between checkpoints. The paper calls this a first-order approximation, valid only while C is small against mu. Here 120 seconds against 351,563 seconds is small. The shape is the point: checkpoint more often when

Middle: Confirm. Read the model load time in the startup log. The vLLM troubleshooting page says to check whether weights sit on a slow network filesystem and recommends local disk. Use `--load-format dummy` to separate download and load from everything else. Also check CPU memory, since a large model can cause swapping while it loads.

A ticket on replica count against expected is a reasonable early warning for the second.

Opening: Traffic doubles at 09:00 and your dashboards go red for eight minutes. The autoscaler did exactly what you configured, and that is the problem. This chapter serves the system design round, where you must say how capacity follows load, and the 30-minute incident round, where a bad scaling decision is a common cause. The job posting asks you to "tune Kubernetes autoscaling to improve GPU efficiency". It also lists alert design and SLOs for high-throughput APIs. Nebius has not published its autoscaling signals, thresholds or scale-down rules. What is public is thin. Token Factory dedicated endpoints let a customer choose the GPU type, GPUs per replica, and minimum and maximum replicas. The customer dashboard shows active replicas and scaling. Everything else in this chapter is a reasonable design built from books, upstream documentation and a simulator I ran. I label it that way, and you should too when you answer.
Middle: Your minimum replica count is a price you pay every hour for a latency promise. Set it too low and a burst arrives before the autoscaler can answer. Set it too high and GPUs sit idle. LLMs in Production says the minimum should sit slightly above the baseline, because steady traffic is rarely steady.
Exercise sample: (no Exercises section)

### part-20-nebius-sre-interview/21-request-routing-retries-and-overload-control.md
Title: 20.21 Request routing, retries and overload control | words 7271 | code blocks 10
Headings: What the interviewer is testing / The core ideas / 1. Decide what is safe to retry before you decide how / 2. Timeouts must nest: client above gateway above engine / 3. Why per-request retries amplify an outage / 4. Retry budgets cap the damage / 5. Exponential backoff with jitter, and the maths / 6. Hedged requests and what they cost on GPUs / 7. Circuit breakers, outlier ejection and the panic threshold / 8. Load shedding, priority, 429 versus 503 and Retry-After / 9. Backpressure on streaming responses / 10. Cache-aware routing versus load-aware routing / 11. Connection draining and graceful termination / 12. Failover across regions with data-residency limits / 13. The code, in full / How to answer out loud / Worked scenarios / Scenario A. Two of eight replicas fail and the whole service goes down / Scenario B. A timeout audit / Scenario C. Streams die on every deploy / Hands-on lab / Drill questions / Common mistakes and weak answers / Connections to the rest of the handbook / Sources and review notes / Verification log (fact-check pass, 2026-11-09)
Opening: Two of eight replicas start failing at 14:00. Ten minutes later all eight are failing, and the failure rate seen by users is far higher than the share of broken replicas. This chapter serves the system design round and the 30-minute incident round. The job posting says the engineer will "harden request routing and retry logic so transient failures don't reach users". It also asks for knowledge of how distributed back-ends fail and for alert design for high-throughput APIs. The public Nebius case study says the team changed its retry logic to stop amplification in partial outages. It also lists backpressure that stops decode queues overrunning, and cache-aware routing that lifted the cache hit rate from about 5 percent to 50 or 60 percent. All three are vendor claims. Nebius has not published its router, its retry settings or its thresholds, so the interviewer is testing your reasoning.
Middle: Envoy also has a panic threshold. Its default is 50 percent. When fewer than half the hosts are available, Envoy ignores health status and spreads traffic across all hosts. Setting it to 0 means that when everything is unhealthy it returns a 503. The documentation does not describe how the panic threshold interacts with outlier ejection, so test it before relying on it.

Title: 20.22 Operating vLLM, Triton, Ray and TensorRT-LLM | words 6457 | code blocks 10
Headings: What the interviewer is testing / The core ideas / 1. Four tools, three layers: engine, server, orchestrator / 2. What each engine is for / 3. The knobs that decide memory / 4. The knobs that decide latency: token budget and chunked prefill / 5. Parallelism: tensor, pipeline, data and expert / 6. Quantisation, prefix caching and speculative decoding / 7. Start-up: what takes the time / 8. Health endpoints: what they prove and what they do not / 9. Upgrades and version skew / 10. Reading each engine's metrics / 11. The one-page comparison / How to answer out loud / Worked scenarios / Scenario A. The command line that passes review and still preempts / Scenario B. Slow rollout, pods killed during start / Scenario C. Picking an engine for a new customer / The code / Engine config reviewer / Kubernetes Deployment for vLLM / Hands-on lab / Drill questions / Common mistakes and weak answers / Connections to the rest of the handbook / Sources and review notes / Verification log (fact-check pass, 2026-11-09)
Opening: A new model arrives on Monday. The team says "just run it on vLLM" and hands you a command line with eleven flags. You must say which flags matter, which will hurt, and what you will watch after the first deploy. This chapter serves the system design round and the project deep dive, and it also feeds the incident round, because every engine flag is a trade you may be asked to defend. The job posting lists, as a nice-to-have, "GPU-heavy workloads with vLLM, Triton, Ray, or another accelerator stack". Chapter 20.18 already covers how serving fails. This chapter covers how you run the engines so that those failures are rarer and shorter.
Middle: Prefix caching reuses KV blocks for requests that share a prefix. In v0.32.0 it is on by default. The design page says vLLM hashes each block from its tokens and its prefix, caches only full blocks, and can add cache salts to isolate tenants. The default hash is `sha256`. The page warns that `xxhash` is not cryptographically secure and theoretically raises collision risk. It also says `sha256` hashes may not be reproducible across Python or vLLM versions, which matters if a shared cache or router depends on them.

How to run a drill from this chapter / How to answer out loud / "A GPU pod is Pending. Walk me through it." / "A pod restarts every few minutes. OOM or probe?" / "A node is NotReady. What happens to its pods?" / "How do you upgrade GPU nodes without an outage?" / "An admission webhook is down and nothing deploys." / Worked scenarios / Drill 1: Pending pod that requests nvidia.com/gpu / Drill 2: ImagePullBackOff on a huge model image / Drill 3: CrashLoopBackOff, OOM or probe kill / Drill 4: A node goes NotReady / Drill 5: Eviction storm / Drill 6: A PDB blocks a drain / Drill 7: CoreDNS slow for half the lookups / Drill 8: A Service has no endpoints / Drill 9: The HPA does not scale / Drill 10: Device plugin missing after an upgrade / Drill 11: etcd is slow after a topology change / Drill 12: An admission webhook outage blocks deploys / Hands-on lab / Drill questions / Common mistakes and weak answers / Connections to the rest of the handbook / Sources and review notes / Verification log
Opening: An interviewer says "a pod has been Pending for ten minutes, the user is waiting, go". Kubernetes is the first required skill in the Nebius Senior SRE posting, and it is the platform under every inference endpoint. One candidate reported a 30-minute incident stage in an Amsterdam loop. Nebius has not published which platform that stage uses, so prepare for a Kubernetes flavour. The official interview page lists troubleshooting, debugging, practical console tasks and "say your reasoning aloud". The posting adds Kubernetes autoscaling tuned for GPU efficiency, incident remediation with runbooks, and debugging from kernel to application. This chapter gives you a fixed order of commands and twelve drills you can rehearse. The incident method itself lives in [20.26](26-the-stress-interview-production-incident-role-play.md).
Middle: GPU nodes are usually tainted. Kubernetes for Generative AI Solutions, chapter 5, shows a node group tainted with key `nvidia.com/gpu`, value `true` and effect `NoSchedule`, so that non-GPU workloads stay off. Production Kubernetes, chapter 2, notes the cost: node pools need node selectors and taints, and every GPU workload needs a matching toleration. **Pending GPU pods are a capacity, plugin, taint or quota question, in that order of likelihood once the pod is valid.**

Opening: An interviewer shares a screen with a Terraform plan on it. The last line says "Plan: 0 to add, 0 to change, 1 to destroy" and the resource is a GPU node pool. They ask: "Do you apply this?" This chapter serves the system design round and the project deep dive. It also serves the stress round, because "Terraform destroyed something" is a classic incident. The job posting lists "Terraform modules that build resilience into new clusters" and "Terraform/IaC" as required. Resilience here means three things. The cluster survives failures of its parts. The code survives mistakes by its own engineers. The state, which is Terraform's memory of what exists, survives accidents too.
Middle: Sixth, upgrades. A node image or version change is where pools die. The provider doc says most template changes roll out new nodes, while changes to taints and labels apply only to nodes created afterwards. So a taint change looks harmless in the plan and does nothing to existing nodes. It also says the defaults for `drain_timeout`, `max_surge` and `max_unavailable` are changing, and tells you to check `status.strategy` for the real values. Do not rely on defaults. Set the strategy explicitly, as the module does. Production Kubernetes, chapter 2, names three upgrade strategies: cluster replacement, node replacement and in-place upgrade. It orders them from highest cost and lowest risk to

The plan for this volume records that wording from the posting. Nebius has not published how its own monitoring is built. So the interviewer cannot test your memory of internals. They test whether you know how the query language fails quietly.
Middle: The configuration reference gives the defaults. `group_wait` is 30 seconds, how long to wait before the first notification for a new group. `group_interval` is 5 minutes, the wait before further notifications for the same group. `repeat_interval` is 4 hours. Group by `[cluster, model]` and a burst of different alert names for one model becomes one page.

Opening: An interviewer says "the alert just fired, you are on call" and starts a clock. You have about 30 minutes. One candidate reported this as the fourth stage of an Amsterdam SRE loop. Nebius has not published the format of this stage, so ask your recruiter whether it exists in your loop. The official interview page lists troubleshooting and debugging, practical Linux console tasks, and saying your reasoning aloud. The job posting adds incident remediation with runbooks and knowledge of how distributed back-ends fail. The Nebius chat-app case study names the failure shapes behind half of this chapter: tail TTFT, retry amplification, and capacity that shrank silently.
Middle: Every drill has a destructive trap, such as restarting every replica at once, defragmenting a live etcd member, sending EU traffic to a US region, or editing live config that GitOps will overwrite. Before you run anything risky, say what it does, how to undo it, and how far the damage can spread. If you cannot say how to undo it, ask for a second pair of eyes.

Headings: What the interviewer is testing / The core ideas / 1. A behavioural answer about an incident is a short report with numbers / 2. Incident roles: one person decides, one person changes things, one person talks / 3. Severity definitions: tie them to customer impact and to a response / 4. A blameless post-mortem has a fixed structure and measured impact / Summary / Impact / Detection / Timeline / Root cause and trigger / What went well / What went poorly / Where we got lucky / Action items / 5. Action items are where most post-mortems fail / 6. Toil is the work that tells you what to automate / 7. A runbook is for the engineer who is half asleep / 8. On-call health is measured, not felt / 9. Error budgets turn a reliability argument into arithmetic / How to answer out loud / Worked scenarios / Scenario 1: repair a weak post-mortem / Scenario 2: timeline and time-to-resolve from messy notes / Scenario 3: negotiate a Friday release with a budget in hand / Hands-on lab / Drill questions / Common mistakes and weak answers / Connections to the rest of the handbook / Sources and review notes / Verification log
Opening: The interviewer says: "Tell me about an incident you led." You have about three minutes. They are listening for numbers, for decisions you made under pressure, and for what you changed afterwards. Many strong engineers fail here because they tell a story about the system and forget to tell a story about themselves. This chapter serves two stages of the loop. It sharpens the production-incident stress round, covered in 20.26, and it prepares the behavioural questions that follow your project deep dive. The job posting asks you to "detect, isolate and remediate incidents quickly using automation and runbooks, then lead post-mortems to prevent recurrence". It also asks you to "work with software engineers to make reliability a built-in feature". Each phrase maps to a section below.
Middle: To prioritise, measure. For each recurring task, count how often it happens and how long it takes, and add the cost of mistakes. Then compare with the cost to automate. The workbook gives a break-even example. A fix that takes 120 working hours pays for itself after about 30 pages if each page costs about 4 hours to handle.

Opening: The interviewer says, "Walk me from GPU capacity to a Token Factory response." You know serving basics, but you now have to reason inward from the cloud boundary. It separates three kinds of evidence. Product documentation is tagged [official]. Nebius blog posts and case studies are tagged [vendor]. Your own design reasoning is tagged [inference]. Where Nebius has not published something, the chapter says so and stops.
Middle: The lifecycle guide lists six lifecycle states: Starting, Updating, Running, Warning, Stopping and Stopped. It lists three readiness states: Not ready, Partially ready and Ready. The page's table writes the Warning state as Error, which is an inconsistency in the source. Send traffic based on readiness, not on the lifecycle label or on a successful control-plane response.

Opening: A customer writes to support: "Our fine-tuned model worked in the test. In production we see 404 for ten minutes after every restart, the bill is higher than the token count suggests, and legal asks where prompts are processed." Each part of that message is a question about the product contract. This chapter serves the system design round and the project deep dive. It also feeds the incident round, because most customer incidents on a managed inference product start with a misread contract. The job posting asks you to design "a whole or partial distributed system" and to run production back-ends. The interviewer wants to hear you separate three things. What Nebius documents, what Nebius markets, and what you would design yourself. Nebius has not published its gateway, router, scheduler or billing pipeline [gap]. So the skill being tested is accuracy about the public contract, plus honest reasoning about the rest.
Middle: Why can `cached_tokens` be lower than the length of the shared part? The docs say the engine caches in internal blocks and the value is usually rounded down to a block boundary [official]. The vLLM design doc shows a likely mechanism. vLLM hashes each KV block from its tokens and its prefix, and it caches only full blocks [vLLM v0.32.0 docs]. Whether Nebius runs vLLM block logic is not stated [gap], so treat that as a plausible match [mine].

Opening: A customer says, "We want an AI factory, not a collection of GPU instances." Then the interviewer asks, "How does it survive an outage?" This chapter serves the system design round and the project deep dive. The job posting asks for knowledge of how distributed back-ends fail and for designing for load and availability. Nebius has not published the interview questions. So the skill is a method: name the boundary, name the owner, and say what is documented and what is your own design. Middle: An application needs a hosted open model, a stable region and a small operations team. A Dedicated Endpoint fits when the template, region, performance and contract fit. The application still needs a gateway of its own for end-user identity, tenant quotas, retry budgets and fallback.

Title: 20.34 Nebius hardware, data centers and the GPU fleet | words 5146 | code blocks 2
Headings: Why an SRE here should know this / The core ideas / 1. A fleet is a tree, and each level shares something / 2. How Nebius says it builds hardware, and why you care / 3. GPU generations set the shape of the failure domain / 4. The fabric: rails, leaves and what a leaf failure does / 5. Power and cooling are shared fates / 6. How hardware faults surface / 7. Storage hardware and what it does to recovery / 8. What benchmark and certification claims do and do not tell you / 9. Supply and capacity constraints, as filings state them / 10. The fleet failure-domain diagram / 11. Blast radius, spares and drain order with the model / 12. Firmware and driver fleets, and GPU return to vendor / How to answer out loud / Worked scenarios / Scenario 1: One NVL72 unit serves whole-rack replicas / Scenario 2: A leaf dies and nothing alerts / Scenario 3: A hall power event / Hands-on lab / Drill questions / Common mistakes and weak answers / Connections to the rest of the handbook / Questions to ask the interviewer / What I could not find / Sources and review notes / Verification log
Opening: At 02:10 an alert says forty GPU nodes dropped out of one pool at the same minute. The interviewer asks, "Is that forty bad GPUs or one bad thing?" You need a picture of what shares what. Which nodes share a power feed, a cooling loop, a fabric switch or a driver version? If you cannot draw that, you cannot tell forty faults from one. This chapter is technical architecture only. It leaves out site lists, company history, power-efficiency claims, capacity announcements and benchmark commentary, except where one of them changes an operational decision. It keeps what an SRE needs: failure domains, how hardware faults surface, what Nebius and the NVIDIA reference architectures actually state, and what that means for blast radius, spares and drain order. The prose stays under 4,500 words. It adds a tested model, `c34_failure_domains.py`, that you can run on any layout you state.
Middle: One operational fact is worth keeping. In the ClusterMAX test, SemiAnalysis found slow 4 KiB sequential allocating writes and metadata problems when 128 or more ranks created directories at once. Nebius retuned the filesystem and the problems were reported as resolved [vendor]. For you, it means a checkpoint pattern where many ranks create directories at the same instant is a known stress point. Stagger it or write one file per rank into a pre-created tree [inference].

Long-running operations / 5. Update is full-replace, with a reset mask / 6. Pagination and read-after-write / 7. The error model and the retry decision / 8. Idempotency / 9. Identity and tokens / 10. Quotas, capacity and rate limits / 11. Layered retries and timeouts / 12. A safe fleet-automation client / 13. How Terraform fits / 14. Audit logs and tracing a failed call / 15. Token Factory has its own control plane / How to answer out loud / Worked scenarios / Scenario A: the CI job exits with 52 / Scenario B: two 429s with different causes / Scenario C: a crash between submit and record / Scenario D: a 14-hour job fails with exit 7 / Hands-on lab / Drill questions / Common mistakes and weak answers / Connections to the rest of the handbook / Questions to ask the interviewer / What I could not find / Sources and review notes / Verification log
Opening: Your automation fails at 03:00 with `RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED`. Is that a quota, a rate limit, or missing GPU capacity? The right reaction differs for each. An SRE on an inference platform scripts against this API constantly, so the error model is operational knowledge. This chapter covers the API surface and nothing else. For the product map see [20.30](./30-nebius-ai-factory-to-token-factory-architecture.md) and [20.31](./31-nebius-ai-cloud-foundation-gpu-fabric-and-platform-choices.md). Evidence tags: `[official]` is a Nebius docs page, `[source-code]` is a public repository read at a stated commit, `[inference]` is my reasoning and `[gap]` means Nebius has not published it. All pages and repositories were read on 2026-11-11. The protobuf repository is `github.com/nebius/api` at commit `06f5307cad6ac4622f839e3cdecebb53d7222656`.
Middle: Both SDKs add a random key by default. In the Go SDK the idempotency interceptor is registered before the retry interceptor, so one key is created per call and reused by the SDK retries [source-code, my reading of the chain order]. The Go SDK also lets you pass your own key per call.

Headings: Why an SRE here should know this / The core ideas / 1. What the managed service runs and what you run / 2. Node groups, GPU drivers and the fabric / 3. How nodes fail and how Nebius repairs them / 4. Upgrades are manual until a version dies / 5. Autoscaling and capacity / 6. How Soperator maps Slurm onto Kubernetes / 7. The jail and what a shared root costs you / 8. The custom resources, validated / 9. Two repair systems and one rule to avoid a fight / 10. Slurm-aware rollouts and node maintenance / 11. Ephemeral nodes, the Slurm power-saving path / 12. What this architecture creates and removes / 13. How to monitor it / 14. Debugging a stuck Slurm job versus a stuck pod / 15. Day-2 rules in one place / 16. A tested model of the replacement chain / How to answer out loud / "What do you know about our Kubernetes and Slurm offerings?" / "A GPU node fails under a long job. Walk me through it." / "How do you upgrade a GPU node group safely?" / "How would you monitor a Soperator cluster?" / Worked scenarios / Scenario 1: Half the nodes are drained and nothing is replaced / Scenario 2: A node group upgrade hangs for hours / Scenario 3: A resumed ephemeral node never gets its job / Hands-on lab / Drill questions / Common mistakes and weak answers / Questions to ask the interviewer / What I could not find / Connections to the rest of the handbook / Sources and review notes / Verification log
Opening: A training customer pings you: "Job 4182 has been pending for an hour and `sinfo` shows half the nodes drained." You open the Kubernetes side and see worker pods that look fine. The question is which of three controllers drained those nodes, and whether it is safe to undrain them. You can only answer if you know how the layers fit. This chapter covers only how the technology works, fails and is operated. It uses Nebius docs fetched on 2026-11-11 [official], the public repositories nebius/soperator at commit f10392e1264a and nebius/nebius-solutions-library at commit 3db4054d463d [source-code], and my reasoning [inference]. The Token Factory data plane is in [20.32](./32-token-factory-model-lifecycle-serving-and-economics.md). The cloud layers are in [20.31](./31-nebius-ai-cloud-foundation-gpu-fabric-and-platform-choices.md).
Middle: With `updateStrategy: slurmAwareRollingUpdate` a template change does not delete pods. Soperator picks outdated workers within `maxUnavailable`, drains each with the reason `soperator rolling update`, and issues a Slurm reboot request with the power action `soperator-worker-handoff`. Slurm runs it only after the node's jobs finish. Then the controller deletes the old pod and the StatefulSet starts the new one [source-code, `worker-node-rollout.md`].

Opening: Your pager fires at 02:10. Error rate on your Token Factory calls is 8 percent. Is it your client, your quota, a Nebius incident, or a bad rollout of your own? The answer depends on what you can see from outside. An interviewer for a Token Factory SRE role will ask the mirror question too: "What would you need to see inside our platform to answer that for a customer?" A realistic question: "A customer says our Token Factory endpoint was down for an hour and you were on call for them. What did you see, what did you not see, and what goes in the post-mortem?"
Middle: Cautions: only the 50 most recent incidents are returned, here about 195 days from 2026-04-29. Times are posted by people. Six of 50 incidents list no component, including a 437-minute Token Factory dedicated-endpoint incident on 2026-10-31. A partial outage is posted as the whole component. And 16 of 50 have a first update at least 30 minutes after the stated start.

Title: 20.38 Token Factory inference engineering: everything Nebius has published | words 6228 | code blocks 2
Headings: Why an SRE here should know this / The core ideas / 1. What Nebius has not published / 2. Two products, one API, very different operating contracts / 3. Routing and the KV cache: the best-documented part / 4. Beyond HBM: the external cache tier / 5. Prefill and decode disaggregation: one case study, no general claim / 6. Speculative decoding is a full product line / 7. Quantisation, flavors and quality claims / 8. Rate limits, tiers and admission / 9. Model lifecycle: removal dates, flavors and fine-tuned weights / 10. Regions, failure domains and the us-central1 incident / 11. Hardware and benchmarks: what they do and do not prove / The master table / A reconstructed data plane, clearly labelled as inference / How to answer out loud / Question 1: "What do you know about our inference platform?" / Question 2: "Design our cache-aware router." / Question 3: "How would you improve the reliability of our dedicated endpoints?" / Question 4: "How would you detect silent quality regressions?" / Worked scenarios / Scenario A: three replicas worse than one / Scenario B: sizing the value of a shared cache tier / Scenario C: acceptance rate falls after a model update / Hands-on lab / Drill questions / Common mistakes and weak answers / Questions to ask the interviewer / What I could not find / Connections to the rest of the handbook / Sources and review notes / Verification log
Opening: The interviewer says: "You have read our docs. Tell me what you know about how we serve inference, and where you think it breaks." You have about two minutes. If you list product names, you lose. If you separate what Nebius documents, what Nebius markets, and what you would build yourself, you win. This chapter serves the system design round, the incident round and the project deep dive. It gathers every public technical fact about Token Factory inference. It is the lookup chapter for the other Nebius chapters, and it links to them instead of repeating them: [20.30](./30-nebius-ai-factory-to-token-factory-architecture.md) for the product boundary, [20.32](./32-token-factory-model-lifecycle-serving-and-economics.md) for lifecycle and billing, [20.37](./37-observability-status-and-operations-on-nebius.md) for the observability surface.
Middle: The API schema gives one hard fact: each model flavor has a `quantization` field [official]. The pages I read do not list the values. The chat case study gives the best evidence of why you cannot trust a single quality number. An FP4 checkpoint made replies shorter, raised repetition and produced empty outputs, and the early signals were product metrics, not benchmarks. The fix was re-quantising with thousands of real sessions and testing FP8 against BF16 for the KV cache [vendor]. One workload, one model, one claim.
